# REFERENCES MASTER — ANDE Fase 1 Diagnostic Toolkit

Consolidated reference extracts from 30 source documents. Each extract is tagged with an ID (e.g., 1A, 14B) that serves as an anchor for cross-referencing in diagnostic reports.

**How to cite:** Each reference section includes full author(s), title, year, and publisher. Use extracto IDs (e.g., "per Extracto 9A, Stam & Van de Ven 2021") when referencing specific content in analysis.

**Total:** 30 references, 180+ extractos

## [Ref 1]

# Reference Extracts: Next Generation Entrepreneurship (Palgrave, 2026)

**Source:** `978-3-031-98513-3.pdf` — Rajagopal, Goncalves & Zlatev (eds.)
**Series:** Palgrave Studies of Entrepreneurship and Social Challenges in Developing Economies. 576 pp., 20 chapters.

This document contains curated extracts from the 5 priority chapters for use in ANDE Phase 1 reports.

---

## Chapter 5 — Shaping Innovation and Entrepreneurship Ecosystems: An Outcome-Based Model

**Authors:** Odille Sanchez-Dominguez & Luis Miguel Almanza-Rueda (Tecnologico de Monterrey)
**Pages:** 145-177 (PDF pages 170-202)
**Use in reports:** Sections 2, 5, 7. Complementary model to ANDE: maps actors vs. innovation funnel stage.

### 5A. Innovation Density Funnel Model

The central framework organizes the ecosystem as a matrix:

**Columns (7 Actor Types):**
1. Academia and Research Institutions
2. Private Companies, Corporate, and Industry
3. Government and City Administration
4. Service Providers (incubators, accelerators, legal, financial, marketing)
5. Capital Sources (VCs, angels, public funding)
6. Connectors, Communities, and Media
7. Backbone Organization (administrative/orchestration entity)

**Rows (5 Stages of the Innovation Density Funnel):**
1. **Inspired People** — Individuals interested in innovating
2. **Capable People** — Those equipped with knowledge and skills to develop technology
3. **Technology/Scientific Projects with Validated Business Model** — Development of technological projects
4. **Startups** — Creation of new ventures
5. **Scale-ups** — Scaling of these ventures

At each intersection, the framework delineates specific activities or responsibilities that each actor should undertake at that stage. The model prevents overlap and redundancy by leveraging each actor's strengths at the appropriate phase.

**Actor Interventions Across Funnel Stages:**

**Academia and Research Institutions:**
- Early stages: Education, training, research opportunities that equip individuals with necessary skills
- Development stages: Advanced R&D, laboratory resources, collaboration on experimental projects
- Startup/Scale-up: Mentorship, industry partnerships, technology transfer through spin-offs and incubator programs

**Private Companies/Industry:**
- Early stages: Collaboration with academia to identify commercial applications for emerging technologies
- Startup phase: Provide funding, market access, and business expertise for commercialization
- Scale-up phase: Lead market presence expansion using infrastructure, distribution channels, and market knowledge

**Government:**
- Early stages: Incentivize innovation through policy frameworks, grants, tax incentives
- Mid-stages: Provide infrastructure, regulatory support, funding for startups
- Scale-up: Promote the region's innovation ecosystem, attract investment, support international expansion through trade agreements

**Service Providers:**
- Early stages: IP protection, business plan development, initial market research; incubators provide office space, mentorship, network access
- Startup phase: Accelerators offer capital, mentorship, opportunities for rapid growth, help secure funding
- Scale-up: Strategic guidance for managing growth complexity, new market entry, business process optimization

**Capital Sources:**
- Early stages: Seed funding for ideas and prototypes
- Startup phase: Financial resources for growth (equity-based)
- Scale-up: Further funding rounds for scaling operations, market expansion

**Connectors and Communities:**
- Early stages: Bring together individuals with ideas and those with skills, facilitating knowledge/resource exchange
- Mid-stages: Link startups with investors, partners, customers
- Scale-up: Maintain and expand network for resources and connections needed to grow

**Backbone Organization:**
- Critical for achieving collective impact by coordinating multiple stakeholders toward a common goal
- Responsible for: aligning activities across actors, communication, resource allocation, performance tracking, strategic alignment
- Ensures each actor's contributions are in sync with the density funnel stages
- Without this support, risk of fragmentation and misalignment would undermine ecosystem success

**Feedback Loops:**
Feedback from the scale-up phase (market performance, funding challenges) informs adjustments in earlier stages (refining research focus, improving accelerator support mechanisms). This iterative process ensures the ecosystem evolves continuously.

### 5B. Indicators of Innovation Ecosystem (Table 5.2)

Measurable indicators for each actor at each funnel level:

**Level 1 — Inspired People:**
- Academy: % of students and professors who have attended 8+ experiences related to scientific or technological-based entrepreneurship
- Private Companies: # employees certified in technology or entrepreneurship
- Government: % of government employees certified in scientific-technological entrepreneurship
- Service Providers: # of specialized providers (incubators, research centers, makerspaces, legal/business firms) collaborating at the ecosystem
- Capital: # of investor prospects aligned to the ecosystem
- Connectors: % of the population that attended a science, technology or entrepreneurship related community

**Level 2 — Capable People:**
- Academy: % of teachers certified in technology-based entrepreneurship
- Private Companies: % of employees involved at entrepreneurial projects within their company/organization
- Government: % of mentors certified by government in technical skills for development of tech-based mentors
- Service Providers: # of specialized providers who sell 5+ services to technology-based entrepreneurs in the last year
- Capital: # of deals and $ invested in projects pre-seed and seed; OR # of advisors linked to possible deals
- Connectors: # of entrepreneurs who lead a science or tech-based community (or vice versa)

**Level 3 — Technology/Scientific Projects with Validated Business Model:**
- Academy: # of patents or scientific-technological inventions with a potential market identified
- Private Companies: # of entrepreneurial projects within the company/organization aligned to local priorities like manufacturing
- Government: % of budget intended for development of new scientific and technological-based projects (e.g., grants)
- Service Providers: % of tech-based projects that result from a pre-incubation or incubation program
- Capital: # of deals and $ invested in startups
- Connectors: # of tech-based entrepreneurship projects identified within local communities

**Level 4 — Startups:**
- Academy: # of technology startups with more than 1 year of operation originating from local academy and universities
- Private Companies: % of spin-offs or entrepreneurial projects launched successfully within the company
- Government: % of budget intended for development of new scientific and technological-based projects (e.g., grants)
- Service Providers: % of tech-based startups that result from a pre-incubation or incubation program
- Capital: # of deals and $ invested in startups (Series A and B)
- Connectors: % of startups that are still active in a local science, technology innovation or entrepreneurship community

**Level 5 — Scale-ups:**
- Academy: N/A
- Private Companies: N/A
- Government: % of employees generated by tech-based scale-ups in the region
- Service Providers: % of tech-based scale-ups that result from a pre-incubation or incubation program
- Capital: # of deals and $ invested in startups (Series B) and scale-ups
- Connectors: N/A

### 5C. Backbone Organization

Critical for achieving collective impact by coordinating multiple stakeholders toward a common goal. Responsibilities:
- Aligning activities across actors
- Communication across the ecosystem
- Resource allocation
- Performance tracking
- Strategic alignment

Ensures each actor's contributions are in sync with the density funnel stages. Without this support, risk of fragmentation and misalignment would undermine ecosystem success.

Based on Collective Impact Theory (Kania & Kramer, 2011): large-scale innovation requires coordinated, cross-sector efforts guided by shared goals and metrics, facilitated by backbone organizations.

### 5D. Five-Dimension Application Process

When applying the framework to diagnose an ecosystem:

1. **Establish the ideal state** for each actor at each funnel stage (benchmark)
2. **Develop specific metrics** to measure each actor's performance and contributions
3. **Map the current state** of resources, activities, and initiatives already present
4. **Conduct gap analysis** comparing ideal vs. current reality to pinpoint where the ecosystem is lacking
5. **Prioritize actions** for each actor, focusing on interventions that yield the greatest impact

Validated through application in an emerging region in northern Mexico with a strong manufacturing base and emerging technological sector.

### 5E. Comparative Table of Ecosystem Model Types (Table 5.1)

Six categories of existing models analyzed across 7 dimensions:

| Category | Key Models | Advantages | Deficiencies |
|----------|-----------|------------|--------------|
| Actor-Based and Stakeholder Models | Triple Helix, Quadruple Helix, Quintuple Helix, Stakeholder Mapping, Collective Impact Framework | Helps visualize key actor roles; Encourages collaboration | Does not detail specific innovation processes; Lacks specific indicators for performance; Difficult to measure individual actor contributions |
| Systems and Ecosystem-Level Models | National Innovation Systems, Regional Innovation Systems, Cluster Theory, Innovation Ecosystem Framework (Adner) | Provides macro-level understanding; Highlights institutional roles and policy impacts | May not capture micro-level interactions or firm-specific processes; Lacks granular detail; Difficult to measure direct outcomes |
| Process-Oriented and Lifecycle Models | Innovation Diffusion Theory, Innovation Funnel Model, Technology Roadmapping, Dynamic Capabilities Framework, Lean Startup Methodology | Focuses on entire innovation lifecycle; Helps track progress | May overlook broader ecosystem dynamics; Does not fully capture actor collaboration; Lacks systemic perspective |
| Value Creation and Business Models | Business Model Canvas, Value Proposition Canvas, Value Chain Framework, JTBD Framework | Provides clear visual tools; Focuses on value creation | Does not integrate macro-level ecosystem interactions; Limited application for policy design; Focuses on firm-level not ecosystem |
| Knowledge and Resource Flow Models | Open Innovation Model, Knowledge Spillover Theory, Resource-Based View | Highlights knowledge sharing; Emphasizes external collaboration | Limited in addressing broader ecosystem-level policies; Focuses primarily on firm interactions |
| Outcome-Driven Ecosystem Models | Balanced Scorecard, OKR, Theory of Change, Innovation Metrics Framework | Provides measurable indicators; Tracks progress toward goals | May overlook intangible outcomes; Focuses heavily on quantitative metrics; Long-term changes hard to quantify |

### 5F. Theoretical Foundations (Eight Macro Theories)

1. **Complex Systems Theory (Simon, 1962):** Ecosystems are dynamic, adaptive environments with non-linear interactions and emergent behaviors. Feedback loops characterize system responses.
2. **Innovation Ecosystem Theory (Adner, 2017):** Co-evolution of firms, alignment of partners' interests, integration of efforts for mutual success.
3. **Resource-Based View (Barney, 1991):** Strategic mobilization of financial, intellectual, and human resources is critical to measurable outcomes.
4. **Collective Impact Theory (Kania & Kramer, 2011):** Large-scale innovation requires coordinated, cross-sector efforts guided by shared goals, facilitated by backbone organizations.
5. **Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Theory (Isenberg, 2010):** Dynamic, institutionally embedded systems of interaction.
6. **Stakeholder Theory (Freeman, 1984):** Stakeholder mapping and engagement.
7. **Ecological Theory:** Positioning the ecosystem as an adaptive, evolving system.
8. **Dynamic Capabilities Framework (Teece et al., 1997):** Process-oriented model for adaptation.

### 5G. Case Study: Northern Mexico

Applied and validated in an emerging region in northern Mexico known for a strong manufacturing base and an emerging technological sector. Application involved mapping across five dimensions (ideal state, metrics, current mapping, gap analysis, prioritization). The chapter does not disclose the specific city name. Given the authors' Tec de Monterrey affiliation, this is likely the Monterrey metropolitan area or a nearby region in Nuevo Leon. No specific numeric data from the application is provided — the chapter is primarily a framework/model paper.

## Chapter 11 — Believing the Less Known: Nexus of AI in Entrepreneurship

**Authors:** Rajagopal & Ananya Rajagopal (EGADE Business School, Tec de Monterrey / Universidad Anahuac Mexico)
**Pages:** 289-316 (PDF pages 308-335)
**Use in reports:** Sections 6 (challenges), 7 (recommendations). Context on AI adoption in Mexican startups.

### 11A. Key Data Points

From qualitative content analysis of 16 in-depth interviews with startup entrepreneurs in Mexico City:

- **43.75%** of respondents were comfortable exploring AI-based information as value addition to their knowledge
- **81.25%** revealed they acquire knowledge through learning by doing and feel comfortable to validate their knowledge in exploring innovative entrepreneurial initiatives
- **68.75%** of entrepreneurs suffer from the low-trust issue in accepting AI-based information and implementing them in entrepreneurial practices due to CTR (cost-time-risk) factors
- **56.25%** of entrepreneurs had cognitive exhaustion due to chaos of AI-based information
- **68.75%** expressed task-level disagreements with AI-based suggestions
- **87.5%** felt they are less known to AI-based information, which causes indecisiveness and cognitive and emotional exhaustion
- **~75%** of respondents opined that psychological filters (personality, subjectivity, trust, confidence) need to be improved culturally to adapt AI-based information

### 11B. Interview Data (Table 11.2)

16 entrepreneurs interviewed with pseudonyms, ages 32-62, 11 men and 5 women. Key entrepreneurial drivers and representative perspectives:

| Pseudonym | Age/Sex | Driver | Key Perspective |
|-----------|---------|--------|----------------|
| Pedro | M, 35 | Design ideas | AI provides differentiated ideas, but embedded risk needs assessment |
| Carina | F, 37 | Accuracy | AI gives exciting information, but no means to test accuracy |
| Teodoro | M, 42 | Validation | AI is amazing, but needs validation for each information; operational feasibility is a challenge |
| Brandon | M, 47 | Measurability | Can use AI-generated ideas but feels the darker side of yield as it cannot be measured |
| Joaquin | M, 53 | Incongruity | Converging AI ideas with pre-conceived ideas is difficult; they do not match |
| Emanuel | M, 59 | Subjectivity | Trusts own mind; AI information comes from a "darker web"; doesn't want to dash on a blind spot |
| George | M, 40 | Low trust | Trust is above AI; validates everyone's information whether friend or foe |
| Concepción | F, 33 | Confidence | AI is "soulmate" but thousand minds = exhaustive; unable to develop confidence |
| Maricela | F, 32 | Learning by doing | Father's mantra: believe in learning by doing; AI goes to back of mind but experience filters to top |
| Enzo | M, 45 | Cognitive syndrome | Mind is chaotic; AI makes it more complex; needs someone to help |
| Sebastian | M, 58 | Entrepreneurial planning | Plans for day after tomorrow; AI suggests for today based on yesterday; finds gap |
| Tom | M, 54 | Task-level disagreement | Enjoys thinking with technology but faces task-level disagreements banking on AI suggestions |
| Patricia | F, 55 | Cognitive exhaustion | Not born with technology; with AI feels like swimming in ocean of information, can't breathe |
| Guillermo | M, 42 | Less known knowledge | Knows what he sees, touches, feels; beyond this is darkness; afraid of exploring less known knowledge |

### 11C. Content Analysis Keywords (Table 11.3)

| Keyword | Frequency | Research Proposition |
|---------|-----------|---------------------|
| Human-Computer-Interface | 102 | — |
| ChatGPT | 98 | — |
| Reliability | 84 | — |
| Real-time | 80 | — |
| Self-actualization | 61 | RP2a, RP2b |
| Competitiveness | 61 | RP3 |
| Personality | 51 | RP2a, RP2b |
| Creativity | 46 | — |
| Design-thinking | 45 | RP1 |
| Cognitive exhaustion | 44 | — |
| Experience | 44 | — |
| Machine-learning | 43 | — |
| Confidence | 40 | — |
| Less known | 36 | — |
| Accuracy | 35 | — |

| Validation | 30 | — |

| Learning-by-doing | 27 | — |

| Measurability | 25 | — |
| Capability | 24 | — |
| AI-based design | 20 | RP1 |
| Incongruity | 20 | RP2a, RP2b |

### 11D. Research Propositions

- **RP1:** AI-based design thinking positively drives creativity of start-up entrepreneurs to develop unique products and technology for market competitiveness.
- **RP2a:** Incongruity between the subjective notion of start-up entrepreneurs and AI-based information creates low-trust cognitive syndrome in exploring innovative entrepreneurial tasks.
- **RP2b:** Start-up entrepreneurs acquire knowledge through learning-by-doing and feel more comfortable than planning based on AI information due to low-trust cognitive syndrome.
- **RP3:** Cognitive exhaustion among start-up entrepreneurs occurs due to less known knowledge of AI information and task-level disagreements.

All three propositions were supported by the qualitative data.

### 11E. Managerial Implications and Recommendations

- Interactions in community forums on social media and AI-based training for start-up entrepreneurs would significantly influence bonding with AI support
- In the absence of institutionalizing AI education at the grassroots of start-up enterprises, subjective cognition of entrepreneurs radically intervenes with the technology adaptation process
- Nodal entrepreneurs with confidence in AI adaptation should be encouraged as peer training instructors to popularize the use of AI in entrepreneurial planning, design thinking, manufacturing, and marketing operations
- Linking the learning process of AI with entrepreneurial culture will help in improving the entrepreneurial mindset with contemporary technology
- AI should be identified as a profit partner of entrepreneurs rather than just a source of magical information
- Psychological and operational discrepancies in adapting to AI information can be resolved by effective training and applied demonstrations through institutional efforts

## Chapter 13 — The Impact of AI on Future Social Entrepreneurs' Sense of Purpose: Gen Z in Mexico

**Author:** Andree Marie Lopez-Fernandez (Universidad Panamericana, Mexico City)
**Pages:** 335-353 (PDF pages 353-371)
**Use in reports:** Section 7 (culture recommendations). Data on 300 university students in CDMX.

### 13A. Mexico Context Data

- Over 98% of organizations in Mexico are MSMEs (~4.7 million MSMEs employing over 68% of the workforce)
- 47.3% led by women entrepreneurs, 52.6% by men entrepreneurs
- ~46% of organizations across sectors use AI in their processes
- ~22% of companies use AI in recruitment processes
- Of the 46% using AI, only ~6% have designed an adequate strategy for its use
- Gender digital gap: women entrepreneurs mostly use AI for communication and design; men for data analysis
- Gen Zers represent 31% of Internet users in Mexico (the majority segment)

### 13B. Key Results (n=300 university students, Mexico City)

**Entrepreneurial Intention (general, by occupation):**
- 75% of students without a job: agreed/completely agreed on entrepreneurial intention
- 73% of student-entrepreneurs: agreed/completely agreed they would continue as entrepreneurs
- 72% of student-dependent-workers: agreed/completely agreed
- 58% of student-independent-workers: agreed/completely agreed
- Mean entrepreneurial intention: 3.916/5 (SD=1.148)

**Social Entrepreneurial Intention (significantly lower):**
- 46% of students without a job: agreed/completely agreed
- Student-entrepreneurs: majority (45%) neither agreed nor disagreed; only 9% completely agreed
- Student-independent-workers: 50% neither agreed nor disagreed; 0% completely agreed
- Student-dependent-workers: 41% agreed/completely agreed
- Mean social entrepreneurial intention: 3.297/5 (SD=1.173)
- **None of the respondents identified as current social entrepreneurs**

**Sense of Purpose Breakdown:**
- Only 5% of women and 6% of men stated they do NOT have a sense of purpose
- 34.4% tie purpose to personal growth, happiness, peace, basic needs
- 22.1% relate purpose to financial stability and success
- 20.4% relate purpose to having their dream job
- 17.7% integrate social and environmental impact into purpose
- 15.0% relate purpose to travel
- 6.0% relate purpose to spiritual growth
- 5.6% relate purpose to family

**AI Use Among Respondents (100% used AI):**
- 33% for homework/school projects
- 18% for tips
- 16% for creative purposes
- 14% for work tasks
- 14% for research
- 5% chat with AI

**Dream Job Perception:**
- 81.6% agreed/completely agreed their dream job should have a positive social and environmental impact
- Mean: 4.08/5 (SD=1.026)

### 13C. Hypothesis Results

- **H1: ACCEPTED.** Positive and significant correlation between perceiving dream job should have social/environmental impact and social entrepreneurial intention (r=0.215**)
- **H2: ACCEPTED.** Increase in sense of purpose aligned with positive social/environmental impact increases social entrepreneurial intention (r=0.203**)
- **H3: NOT ACCEPTED.** No significant correlation between AI potentially fulfilling sense of purpose and social entrepreneurial intention (r=0.041, not significant). 43.8% disagreed/completely disagreed they would change purpose if AI could fulfill it.

### 13D. Correlation Analysis (Table 13.4)

25 significant correlations found (14 positive, 11 negative, at 95% and 99% confidence).

Social entrepreneurial intention (SIN) correlated positively and significantly with:
- Entrepreneurial intention (EIN): r = 0.141*
- Dream job should have social/environmental impact (DJS): r = 0.215**
- Degree of social/environmental impact in sense of purpose (PSI): r = 0.203**
- Spiritual growth in purpose (PSG): r = 0.188**
- Family in purpose (PFA): r = 0.135*
- Dream job should impact sense of purpose (DJP): r = 0.278**

Social entrepreneurial intention correlated negatively with:
- Traveling in sense of purpose (PTR): r = -0.131*

AI impact on sense of purpose (AIP) showed NO significant correlation with social entrepreneurial intention (r = 0.041, n.s.)

**Descriptive Statistics:**
| Variable | Mean | SD |
|----------|------|-----|
| Entrepreneurial intention (EIN) | 3.916 | 1.148 |
| Social entrepreneurial intention (SIN) | 3.297 | 1.173 |
| Dream job social/environmental impact (DJS) | 4.080 | 1.026 |
| Dream job impacts sense of purpose (DJP) | 4.066 | 0.945 |
| AI impact on sense of purpose (AIP) | 2.792 | 1.357 |

### 13E. Policy Implications

- There is a clear indication of Gen Zs' intention to engage in social entrepreneurial activities in Mexico
- Combined with their perception that a dream job should positively impact society and the environment, this signals Gen Zers seek to contribute to development
- This attribute "should be fostered as its implications are highly relevant for the development of emerging markets"
- Entrepreneurial intention does not guarantee entrepreneurial action (Bogatyreva et al., 2019)
- **It is up to policy makers and academicians to create optimal circumstances to potentiate social entrepreneurial intention**, as well as strategies and policies that help aspiring entrepreneurs transition from intention to action effectively
- Social entrepreneurial self-efficacy and perceived social support both impact social entrepreneurial intention (Bui et al., 2023)

## Chapter 16 — The Dual Role of Opportunity Recognition in Mediating and Moderating the Relationship Between Social Networks and Knowledge Acquisition

**Authors:** Aldahir Caballero-Campbell & Hugo Alvarez-Perez (Tecnologico de Monterrey, Monterrey)
**Pages:** 415-441 (PDF pages 434-457)
**Use in reports:** Section 7. Recommendations on mentor networks, communities, and social capital.

### 16A. Core Finding: The Dual Role

Opportunity recognition operates simultaneously as:

1. **Mediator:** Serves as the translating mechanism that converts social connections into actual knowledge. Entrepreneurs do not automatically absorb knowledge from their networks — they must first recognize opportunities within those networks, which then enables knowledge acquisition. Process chain: Social Networks → Opportunity Recognition → Knowledge (tacit and explicit).

2. **Moderator (amplifier):** Beyond being a conduit, opportunity recognition amplifies the strength of the relationship between social networks and knowledge acquisition. When opportunity recognition capability is high, the same social network yields significantly more knowledge.

**Four hypotheses tested and supported:**
- H1: OR mediates the relationship between SN and tacit knowledge acquisition — **SUPPORTED**
- H2: OR mediates the relationship between SN and explicit knowledge acquisition — **SUPPORTED**
- H3: OR moderates the SN → tacit knowledge relationship (stronger when OR is high) — **SUPPORTED**
- H4: OR moderates the SN → explicit knowledge relationship (stronger when OR is high) — **SUPPORTED**

### 16B. SEM Results (n=169 business students, UANL Monterrey)

**Descriptive Statistics:**
| Variable | Mean | SD |
|----------|------|-----|
| Opportunity Recognition | 3.75 | 0.82 |
| Social Networks | 3.60 | 0.70 |
| Tacit Knowledge | 3.85 | 0.65 |
| Explicit Knowledge | 3.68 | 0.75 |

**Bivariate Correlations (all p < 0.01):**
| | OR | SN | TK | EK |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opportunity Recognition | 1.00 | | | |
| Social Networks | 0.55** | 1.00 | | |
| Tacit Knowledge | 0.48** | 0.52** | 1.00 | |
| Explicit Knowledge | 0.46** | 0.50** | 0.49** | 1.00 |

**Structural Paths:**
| Path | Coefficient | SE | z-value | p-value |
|------|------------|-----|---------|---------|
| Social Networks → Opportunity Recognition | 0.45** | 0.08 | 5.63 | <0.001 |
| Opportunity Recognition → Tacit Knowledge | 0.38** | 0.07 | 5.14 | <0.001 |
| Opportunity Recognition → Explicit Knowledge | 0.42** | 0.09 | 4.67 | <0.001 |

**Mediation (Bootstrap 5,000 samples):**
| Relationship | Total Effect | Direct | Indirect | 95% CI |
|-------------|-------------|--------|----------|--------|
| SN → Tacit Knowledge | 0.39** | 0.22* | 0.17** | [0.10, 0.26] |
| SN → Explicit Knowledge | 0.41** | 0.25* | 0.16** | [0.09, 0.24] |

**Moderation:**
| Interaction | Coefficient | SE | z-value | p-value |
|------------|------------|-----|---------|---------|
| SN × OR → Tacit Knowledge | 0.12* | 0.05 | 2.40 | 0.016 |
| SN × OR → Explicit Knowledge | 0.15* | 0.06 | 2.50 | 0.013 |

**CFA Model Fit:** CFI = 0.97, TLI = 0.96, RMSEA = 0.03. All CR > 0.70, all AVE > 0.50.

### 16C. Unexpected Finding: Bonding Social Capital

Even within bonding social capital (networks of family and close friends, typically viewed as limiting for innovation because they lack information diversity), opportunity recognition can still significantly influence knowledge acquisition. This challenges the prevailing notion that bonding networks are inherently less valuable for entrepreneurial activities, suggesting that the quality of opportunity recognition within these networks can mitigate the downsides of limited information diversity.

**Particularly relevant for Latin American ecosystems where family and community ties are culturally strong.**

### 16D. Three Types of Social Capital

1. **Bonding social capital:** Ties within closed groups (family, close friends). Provides emotional support but traditionally viewed as limiting information diversity.
2. **Bridging social capital:** Connections across different social groups. Provides access to diverse information and resources. More professional contacts. Associated with growth expectations and innovative strategies.
3. **Linking social capital:** Connections with individuals in positions of power/authority (government officials, industry leaders, mentors). Essential for navigating formal structures and gaining strategic support.

**Knowledge Typology:**
- **Tacit Knowledge:** Implicit, acquired through experience and practice. Includes practical learning, intuition, internalization of entrepreneurial skills. Difficult to acquire through formal channels.
- **Explicit Knowledge:** Formal, codified, accessible and transferable. Market data, industry trends, best practices.

### 16E. Implications for Ecosystems

**For mentor networks:**
- Mentors serve as sources of both tacit and explicit knowledge, but their value is maximized when entrepreneurs possess strong opportunity recognition
- Mentor program design should include opportunity recognition training alongside traditional mentoring
- Linking social capital (connecting with mentors, industry leaders) is essential for entrepreneurs to navigate formal structures

**For communities:**
- Knowledge sharing within communities enhances innovative behavior and opportunity recognition
- Communities thrive when members actively share ("knowledge donating"), not just absorb ("knowledge collecting")
- Meetups, events, and online communities create contexts for opportunity recognition
- Entrepreneurial alertness is enhanced through active participation in knowledge-sharing networks

**For ecosystem design and policy:**
- Interventions should not only build networks (supply side) but also build the capacity to recognize opportunities within those networks (demand side)
- Programs that combine network building with opportunity recognition training will have stronger knowledge acquisition outcomes
- Even informal, close-tie networks contribute to entrepreneurial knowledge — if opportunity recognition skills are present

### 16F. Survey Instrument (23 items retained after CFA)

**Explicit Knowledge items (EK, 7 items):** Sharing knowledge/business ideas based on experience, identifying industries of interest, seeking information via social media/news/TV, taking courses/conferences with experts, comparing industry knowledge with family/friends, reading books/articles about business, attending expert conferences.

**Tacit Knowledge items (TK, 6 items):** Seeking advice from entrepreneurs in person or at events, speaking with experienced people about business, sharing work experiences with family/friends, asking teammates/experts for tips, sharing experiential knowledge with colleagues and learning from others' experiences, relying on expert testimony over textbook information.

**Social Networks items (SN, 5 items):** Preferring advice from experts/mentors for business, seeking relationships with entrepreneurs/experts for advice, having friends/relatives to potentially partner with, having contacts with their own businesses, having contacts for sharing business experiences.

**Opportunity Recognition items (OP, 5 items):** Seeking information about new product/service ideas, setting up an organization to pursue a perceived opportunity, developing a business in the market based on a perceived opportunity, approaching investors (angel investors, VCs) for funding, putting together an entrepreneurial team to pursue an opportunity.

## Chapter 20 — Entrepreneurship 5.0: Synchronizing Technology and Human Factor

**Author:** Rajagopal (EGADE Business School, Tec de Monterrey, Mexico City)
**Pages:** 535-557 (PDF pages 546-568)
**Use in reports:** Sections 6, 7. Conceptual framework for ecosystems integrating AI and sustainability.

### 20A. Entrepreneurship 5.0 Definition and Frameworks

**Definition:** An outgrowth of Society 5.0, emphasizing integration of human creativity and technology with focus on AI and robotics to create technology-driven sustainable entrepreneurial solutions.

**Core characteristics:**
- **Human centricity:** Aligns AI, ML, and robotics in managing productivity while keeping humans at the center. Prioritizes human well-being and societal value over purely economic gains.
- **Data-driven and people-centric conceptualization:** Supports Knowledge-Action-Performance (KAP) features through an iterative cycle between cyberspace and physical space.
- **ESG integration:** Environmental, Social, and Governance concepts increasingly used to evaluate performance of technology-based MSMEs.
- **Super-smart enterprise:** Digital transformation and technological innovation are basic components.
- **Design thinking through digital platforms** to streamline operations, improve customer relationships, and create new business models.

**Knowledge-Action-Performance (KAP) Cycle:**
Data, information, and knowledge drive both social and entrepreneurial innovation through an iterative cycle between cyberspace and physical space.

**Cost-Time-Risk (CTR) Factor Model:**
High growth entrepreneurship using technology platforms amplifies CTR factors to transform existing businesses. Service enterprises like Airbnb, Uber, and Amazon drive transitions to reduce CTR factors.

**Technology-Industry-Market Triadic Fit:**
Collaborative robotics between expert firms and startups homogenize the use of technology and develop an appropriate triadic fit.

**Industry Evolution (4.0 → 5.0):**
- Industry 4.0: Automation and digitization
- Industry 5.0: Combines human factor with machine learning; collaborative robotics, exoskeletons, AR within sophisticated AI systems; highly personalized and demand-led manufacturing

### 20B. Green Entrepreneurship

- Green entrepreneurship has emerged as a growing need in an age of technical innovation and environmental protection
- Low-carbon development through sustainability entrepreneurial practices is a global consensus aligned with OECD Green Growth Declaration and SDGs aiming for carbon neutrality by 2030
- Generative AI (GAI) has a crucial role in promoting sustainable development, fostering sustainability norms
- GAI can assist enterprises in acquiring and applying new green knowledge to foster green innovation
- However, pathways for technopreneurship and innovation are unclear in most developing countries where government support remains obscure
- The mechanisms by which GAI propels green entrepreneurship are still in a nascent state

### 20C. Consortium Entrepreneurship

A powerful force enabling businesses to streamline supply chains and marketing strategies under a shared economy model, integrating capital, technology, and managerial expertise.

In developing economies: consortium strategies link local SMEs with MNCs, improving productivity and global presence through Industry 5.0 partnerships that blend cost-effective manufacturing with innovation facilitated by AI, ML, and human-centric design.

### 20D. Book Synthesis (Key Themes from 19 Chapters)

**Part I — Women Entrepreneurship & Ecosystems:**
- Women entrepreneurs face distinct motivations and barriers shaped by personal, socio-cultural, and economic factors
- Chapter 5's innovation ecosystem framework provides an actionable tool integrating Collective Impact Theory, Innovation Ecosystem Theory, RBV, and Complex Systems Theory

**Part II — Technology and AI:**
- AI/ML transforming supply chains, consulting, workforce structure
- Generative AI changing skill demands (increasing need for AI-related skills)
- AI adoption in Mexican startups: significance despite cognitive exhaustion, trust issues, validation challenges
- Gen Z: sense of purpose and AI perception influence social entrepreneurship intentions

**Part III — Behavioral and Knowledge Management:**
- Opportunity recognition as dual mediator/moderator between social networks and knowledge acquisition
- Knowledge sharing, consumer privacy, and ethical tensions in the AI era

**Part IV — Sustainability and Development:**
- Rural employment programs' role in fostering sustainable livelihoods

### 20E. Implications for Ecosystem Development

1. **Technology as ecosystem catalyst, not replacement:** AI, robotics, AR/VR enhance human capabilities rather than replacing them. Focus on restructuring businesses that address social challenges.

2. **MSMEs and Entrepreneurship 5.0:** New opportunities for MSMEs to access advanced technologies and develop innovative products with positive societal impact.

3. **Government role in developing economies:** Large-scale financial support needed to converge small enterprises with Industry 5.0. However, government support in most developing countries remains obscure, and pathways for technopreneurship are not properly paved.

4. **Regional technology development is highly skewed:** Varied due to socioeconomic and behavioral challenges causing obstructions in diffusing technology-based knowledge and developing entrepreneurial capital.

5. **Green entrepreneurship through GAI:** Potential to drive green entrepreneurial efforts with enhanced human-machine interactions, but mechanisms are nascent.

6. **Consortium strategies:** Linking local SMEs with MNCs through Industry 5.0 partnerships as a practical model for building market depth in emerging ecosystems.

7. **ESG as evaluation metric:** Ecosystems should incorporate ESG evaluation of technology-based MSMEs as a maturity indicator.

8. **Integration challenge:** Major challenge is integrating value chains of conventional systems with the new technology ecosystem. Society 5.0 requires balancing what is best for society with what is best for the individual.

## [Ref 2]

# Reference Extracts: OECD Data and Methods for EE Diagnostics (2025)

**Source:** `REF_5173f4a2en.pdf`
**Title:** Data and Methods for Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Diagnostics
**Authors:** Roberto Crotti, Jonathan Potter, Pablo Shah & Erik Stam
**Year:** 2025
**Publisher:** OECD SME and Entrepreneurship Papers
**DOI:** https://doi.org/10.1787/5173f4a2-en
**Pages:** 84

Methodological companion paper to the OECD Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Diagnostics Report (pilot edition, 38 OECD economies).

---

## 2B. 29 Indicators Across 10 Elements

### 1. Institutions (4 indicators)
- Rule of law (0-100 best) — EIU via World Bank WGI
- Control of corruption (0-100 low incidence) — V-Dem via World Bank WGI
- Product Market Regulation index (0-6 stringent) — OECD
- Effective corporate tax rate (% taxable income) — OECD
- PCA: two dimensions — institutional quality (corruption, rule of law, PMR) and taxation
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.51

### 2. Culture (3 indicators)
- Entrepreneurship as a good career choice (% 18-64 pop.) — GEM
- High status to successful entrepreneurs (% 18-64 pop.) — GEM
- Trust in others (% respondents) — World Values Survey
- Discarded: Fear of failure (volatile), creative thinking PISA (too few countries), openness to technology (too few countries)
- PCA: two dimensions — trust+status, and desirability of entrepreneurial career
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.42

### 3. Networks (2 indicators)
- SMEs collaborating on innovation (% total SMEs) — European Commission Innovation Scoreboard
- University-business collaboration (1-7 best) — WEF Executive Opinion Survey
- Correlation: ~50%
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.18 (limited — data scarcity)

### 4. Infrastructure (3 indicators)
- Mobile data use (Gb per subscription/month) — OECD Broadband & Telecom
- Fixed broadband subscriptions (per 100 pop.) — OECD Telecommunications
- Transport infrastructure quality (1-5 high) — World Bank Logistics Performance Index
- PCA: transport + fixed broadband form one dimension; mobile data adds a second
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.60

### 5. Markets (2 indicators)
- Gross domestic product, PPP$ million — OECD
- Trade facilitation index (0-2 best) — OECD Trade Facilitation Indicators
- GDP used instead of population or disposable income because it captures B2B markets too
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.44

### 6. Finance (4 indicators)
- Early-stage VC investment, USD per capita — OECD Entrepreneurship Financing Database
- Later-stage VC investment, USD per capita — OECD Entrepreneurship Financing Database
- Outstanding SME loans, thousands USD per capita — OECD SME Financing Scoreboard
- Factoring, thousands USD per capita — OECD SME Financing Scoreboard
- VC early and later are 97% correlated; VC and SME loans have very low correlation (<5%)
- VC scaled by population, not GDP, to avoid normalization disparities
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.28 (limited — different financial instruments across countries)

### 7. Knowledge (3 indicators)
- Patents per million population (triadic patent families) — OECD Main S&T Indicators
- R&D expenditure % GDP — OECD Main S&T Indicators
- GitHub software uploads per thousand people — GitHub
- R&D and patents correlated at 69%; GitHub adds unique dimension
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.82 (high)

### 8. Talent (4 indicators)
- Mean years of schooling — UNESCO
- PISA scores (average of math, reading, science) — OECD
- Internet users % population — World Bank WDI
- Perceived entrepreneurial capabilities (% 18-64 pop.) — GEM
- Noteworthy: perceived entrepreneurial capabilities is NEGATIVELY correlated with all other talent indicators
- PCA: two dimensions — educational system (PISA, internet, schooling) and entrepreneurial skills
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.81 (high)

### 9. Leadership (1 indicator)
- Serial entrepreneurs (unit count, absolute not per capita) — Crunchbase
- Single indicator due to data scarcity; acknowledged as imperfect proxy

### 10. Intermediate Services (3 indicators)
- Incubators per million population (includes accelerators, co-working spaces, entrepreneurship programs) — Crunchbase & OECD
- Coaches/Mentors (unit count, absolute) — Crunchbase
- Technical employment % total employment (ISIC Section M) — OECD
- Low correlation between incubators and coaches (~10%)
- Cronbach's alpha: 0.41

---

## 2C. Normalization Method: Clipped Min-Max

**Formula:**
```
norm_ikt = 100 × (raw_value_ikt - min_kt) / (max_kt - min_kt)
```
Where:
- min_kt = mean_kt - 2 × stdev_kt
- max_kt = mean_kt + 2 × stdev_kt

**For inverted indicators** (higher = worse, e.g., taxation, PMR):
```
norm_ikt = 100 × (raw_value_ikt - max_kt) / (min_kt - max_kt)
```

**Key features:**
- Scores anchored to 2020-2023 period values (min/max derived from that period)
- Values below min get 0; above max get 100
- Using 2 standard deviations reduces distortion from outliers
- Compared against z-scores and winsorized min-max; clipping method performs better with extreme outliers

**Why not other methods:**
- Z-scores: weak to outliers, hard to communicate
- Winsorized min-max: at least one country always gets 0 and one gets 100 (undesirable)
- Clipped min-max: best balance between maintaining distribution properties and handling outliers

---

## 2D. Aggregation: Geometric Mean

**Formula:**
```
geomean_score_it = exp( (1/n) × sum( ln(1 + x_ikt) ) )
```

**Why geometric mean (not arithmetic mean):**
- Does not allow full compensability (a very low score on one indicator cannot be fully offset by high scores on others)
- Aligns with ecosystem concept: an incomplete ecosystem can hinder entrepreneurship even if other conditions are good
- Similar logic to "penalty for bottleneck" approaches (Acs, Rappai & Szerb, 2012)
- Arithmetic mean allows full compensability, inappropriate for ecosystems where elements are not perfect substitutes

**Why not weighted averages:**
- Tested PCA-derived weights (factor loadings of first two principal components)
- Rejected because: no strong theoretical logic to assign weights; PCA weights explain input variance, not importance for outcome

**Equal weights within elements.** All indicators in each element receive equal weight.

**No overall aggregation.** The ten elements are NOT combined into a single index. The authors argue a single score would be "an overly simplified depiction of the complexity of entrepreneurial ecosystems."

---

## 2E. Missing Data Handling (6-step procedure)

1. **Indicator selection:** Chosen to minimize missing data (80% coverage threshold)
2. **Interpolation:** Between two known data points
3. **Moving averages:** 5-year windows reduce gaps
4. **Linear regression imputation:** Cross-country regression with external predictor and country fixed effects
   - Examples: Trust imputed using Control of Corruption; VC investment using Crunchbase funding rounds; Mean years of schooling using tertiary education share
5. **Geometric mean imputation:** For remaining gaps, geometric mean of non-missing values in the element
6. **Protection rule:** If more than one indicator in an element is missing or imputed, the element score is NOT computed for that country

Post-imputation: only 9 missing values remain for 2020-2023 (0.8% of dataset).

Time periods: Three periods produced retroactively (2016-2020, 2018-2022, 2020-2023). 5-year moving averages used within each window. Two-year intervals between editions.

---

## 2I. Cross-Element Correlations

- About 40% of pairwise coefficients > 60%
- **Strong correlations (>80%):**
  - Infrastructure ↔ Knowledge
  - Institutions ↔ Talent
- **Strong correlations (>70%):**
  - Intermediate Services ↔ Leadership (0.75)
  - Intermediate Services ↔ Networks (0.77)
- **Weak correlations:**
  - Institutions ↔ Leadership
  - Markets ↔ Culture

**Interpretation:** Ecosystems tend to develop elements in clusters. Strong institutions correlate with strong talent pools. Good infrastructure correlates with knowledge production. Support services develop alongside leadership and network capacity.

**Time series patterns (2016-2023):**
- Most time variance in: Talent, Markets, Infrastructure, Culture
- Most stable: Finance, Knowledge
- Talent driven by improving education attainment and internet access
- Markets driven by GDP growth and trade facilitation improvements
- Infrastructure driven by increasing ICT use

**Cronbach's Alpha by Element (internal consistency):**

| Element | Alpha | Assessment |
|---------|-------|------------|
| Knowledge | 0.82 | High |
| Talent | 0.81 | High |
| Infrastructure | 0.60 | Moderate |
| Institutions | 0.51 | Moderate |
| Markets | 0.44 | Moderate |
| Culture | 0.42 | Moderate |
| Intermediate Services | 0.41 | Moderate |
| Finance | 0.28 | Limited |
| Networks | 0.18 | Limited |
| Leadership | N/A | Single indicator |

## 3A. Latin American Country Scores (Table 3.1, 2020-2023)

| Element | Mexico | Chile | Colombia | Costa Rica | OECD pattern |
|---------|--------|-------|----------|------------|--------------|
| 1. Institutions | **4.6** | 50.4 | **6.6** | **9.6** | Lowest scores in OECD |
| 2. Culture | 28.3 | 38.8 | **14.7** | n/a | Below average |
| 3. Networks | n/a | **25.9** | n/a | n/a | Very weak where data exists |
| 4. Infrastructure | **6.9** | **17.3** | **5.3** | **5.7** | Severe gap vs OECD |
| 5. Markets | 35.3 | **21.0** | 25.1 | **15.0** | Below average |
| 6. Finance | n/a | n/a | n/a | n/a | No data available |
| 7. Knowledge | **14.1** | **18.1** | **17.8** | **19.7** | Bottom of OECD |
| 8. Talent | **3.7** | **35.0** | **3.0** | **2.2** | Critical weakness |
| 9. Leadership | **50.1** | **30.4** | **37.5** | **23.6** | Mexico surprisingly decent |
| 10. Intermediate Svcs | **29.3** | **32.8** | **17.7** | **14.9** | Below average |

Key LatAm findings:

- **Mexico, Colombia, Costa Rica** classified as "ecosystems that are mostly still underdeveloped, where a large number of elements have below-OECD-average scores."
- **Chile** performs somewhat better but still has severe weaknesses in Infrastructure (17.3), Knowledge (18.1), and Markets (21.0).
- **Mexico's Leadership score (50.1)** is a notable outlier — above the OECD median — driven by the absolute count of serial entrepreneurs (Crunchbase data). Contrasts with near-bottom scores on Institutions (4.6), Talent (3.7), and Infrastructure (6.9).
- **Infrastructure** is a defining gap: Mexico (6.9), Colombia (5.3), Costa Rica (5.7) are the three lowest in the entire OECD. Chile at 17.3 is also near the bottom.
- **Talent** equally critical: Costa Rica (2.2), Colombia (3.0), Mexico (3.7) are the three lowest OECD countries. Low mean years of schooling, low PISA scores, and low internet user rates drive these scores. However, Mexico and Chile score very high on perceived entrepreneurial capabilities (>90/100 on that sub-indicator).
- **Knowledge (R&D, patents, GitHub)**: All four LatAm countries cluster at the bottom (14-20 range). Top performers (Korea, Israel, Switzerland) score 80-87.
- **Finance**: No OECD data available for any four LatAm countries. Cross-country analysis notes that "in less developed countries such as Chile, Mexico, Colombia and Turkiye, access to bank credit remains significantly more difficult than in other OECD countries."

Key output findings:
- **Colombia has the highest business birth rate in the entire OECD (21.7%)** but near-zero equity-based startups or unicorns — high quantity but low "innovative" entrepreneurship.
- **Mexico and Chile have the highest job-creation expectations** among OECD entrepreneurs (30.7% and 32.9%), even outperforming the US (28.8%).
- **Colombia has the most inclusive entrepreneurship** in the OECD with only 3.7% "missing entrepreneurs" rate (vs. 70.8% for Italy).
- **Mexico has highly dispersed startup activity** geographically (9.2, vs. 83.5 for Estonia), meaning entrepreneurship is not concentrated in one city.

---

## 3C. Four Ecosystem Types (Cross-Country Analysis)

1. **Mostly underdeveloped**: Large number of elements below OECD average (Colombia, Costa Rica, Mexico)
2. **Started to develop**: Mostly average performance, no excellence in any area (Czechia, Slovenia)
3. **Developed unevenly**: Average in some elements, strong in few (France, Germany)
4. **Well advanced**: Strong on majority of elements (US, UK, Switzerland)

**Common weak links across all OECD countries** (highest share of countries with low scores):
- Leadership
- Markets
- Knowledge
- Intermediate Services

These offer "the strongest potential for catch up" by lagging countries.

**Leadership is the most differentiating factor**: The gap between top 3 and bottom 3 countries is largest for Leadership. Developing serial entrepreneurs "takes time and may require significant efforts."

**Finance has the smallest gap** between top and bottom on overall scores, but VC gaps between top and bottom are enormous (Israel and US score 98-100/100 on seed VC; most countries score below 30).

**No country achieves top scores in all elements.** In only two cases does a country achieve the highest score on two elements. Every ecosystem has improvement areas.

**Scores are fairly stable over time**, but rapid improvements are possible:
- France improved Institutions (corporate tax reforms)
- Colombia, Czechia, Greece, Mexico, Turkiye improved Markets (trade facilitation)
- Estonia and Turkiye strengthened Infrastructure (transport quality, mobile data)
- Portugal, Lithuania, Poland improved Talent (digital skills)

---

## 3D. Policy-Element Mapping (Table 5.1)

| Element | Policy types for startups and scaleups |
|---------|---------------------------------------|
| **Institutions** | Tax incentives for investors; Tax incentives to reduce costs for new firms; Tax incentives to support business growth; R&D tax credits; Improving access to information on regulations; Reducing compliance costs of regulations |
| **Culture** | Entrepreneurship awareness raising campaigns and events; Entrepreneurship competitions, prizes and awards; Entrepreneurship education in schools and universities |
| **Networks** | Cluster policies; Networks of entrepreneurs |
| **Infrastructure** | Investments in broadband infrastructure; Digital innovation hubs |
| **Markets** | Export awareness campaigns; Information and advisory services on how to export; International trade missions; Logistical support through trade desks; Export credit guarantees; Trade insurance; Measures to improve SMEs/startups access to public procurement; Simplification of public procurement; Certifications, labels and standards |
| **Finance** | Grants for startups; Loans for startups; Public sector VC funds; Co-investment funds; Mezzanine financing; Business angel networks; Loan guarantees; Investment-readiness training; Financial literacy training; Facilitation of fintech for startup support |
| **Knowledge** | Innovation vouchers; Science parks; Technology transfer support programmes; Incentives for researchers for "third-mission" activities; Intellectual property facilitation; Public procurement for innovation |
| **Talent** | Entrepreneurship training programmes targeting aspiring entrepreneurs; Startup visas |
| **Leadership** | Private sector consultation in entrepreneurship policymaking; Alumni networks for entrepreneurship programmes (including incubators and accelerators) |
| **Intermediate Services** | Business advisory services; Mentoring programmes; Coaching; Incubators; Accelerators |

---

## 3E. Case Study: French Tech (France, since 2013)

**What it is:** A unified branding and coordination initiative bringing together all entrepreneurial ecosystem stakeholders under one name/logo. Run by a team of 30 people (the "French Tech Mission") attached to the Ministry of Economy, with EUR 25 million operating budget.

**Two main activities:**
1. Labelling and coordinating 116 "French Tech Capitals and Communities" — private associations of local ecosystem actors who apply for the label, which requires mapping the local ecosystem and demonstrating collaborative work.
2. Support programs for innovative startups (French Tech Tremplin for underrepresented founders, Next 40/120 for top potential companies, French Tech 2030 for societal challenges aligned with France 2030 Plan).

**Specific measures by element:**
- **Institutions**: French Tech Central facilitates startup access to public services through representatives from 60+ public administrations in 17 flagship locations.
- **Networks**: The labelling process itself forces ecosystem mapping and collaborative work. 116 Capitals and Communities with ~1,400 volunteers (70% entrepreneurs) and 6,000 startups.
- **Markets**: 67 international communities across 52 countries of French/Francophile entrepreneurs, supported by embassies. "I choose French Tech" initiative (June 2023) to increase use of French Tech products by public administrations and large companies — 300+ companies and 80 institutional players signed up.
- **Finance**: French Tech Rise reduces inequalities in startup financing across France. Local meetings between VCs and startups, then 20 regional "champion" startups invited to national event with major investment funds.
- **Leadership**: Community members act as mentors, helping develop skills, recruit, identify funding, and direct to local incubators/accelerators.

**Results:** Since 2013, incubators and serial entrepreneurs doubled; venture capital invested tripled.

**Success factors:**
- Mobilization of **local actors as creators and drivers** (not merely beneficiaries) of initiatives.
- **Agility**: Small, young team of 30 with high autonomy. Programs evolved in response to stakeholder demands.
- **Bottom-up approach**: Rather than large top-down national schemes, French Tech stimulated local initiatives that build on existing resources and are financed/operated by local ecosystem actors.
- **Longstanding support from public authorities at the highest level.**

**Key takeaway for LatAm:** The model addresses the problem of startup activity being concentrated in certain cities. It works by empowering local entrepreneurs to become initiative creators, thereby increasing the total pool of resources in local ecosystems. The labelling process itself is a powerful tool for ecosystem mapping and stakeholder alignment.

---

## 3F. Case Study: Italian Startup Act (Italy, since 2012)

**What it is:** A comprehensive legal framework introducing a formal definition for "innovative startup" (limited, unlisted companies HQ'd in Italy, <5 years old, <EUR 5M annual turnover, not distributing profits, meeting at least one innovation criterion: 15% R&D spend, 1/3 staff PhDs/researchers or 2/3 master's, or patent/software ownership). Companies self-certify at local Chamber of Commerce. Annual re-certification required.

**Specific measures:**
- **Institutions**: Exemption from tax penalties for loss-making companies; exemption from Chamber of Commerce fees; extension of deadlines to cover capital loss; allowances for flexible share structures.
- **Markets**: 30% discount on Italian Trade Agency internationalization assistance.
- **Finance**: Tax deductions for equity investors in innovative startups; equity crowdfunding regulations and portal register; privileged access to SME Guarantee Fund (fee waiver, priority processing, removal of public credit assessment).
- **Talent**: Unlimited fixed-term contract renewals; exemption from cap on fixed-term contracts; stock options and work-for-equity schemes.
- **Intermediate Services**: Definition of "certified incubators" with requirements for facilities, staff, equipment, track record. Certified incubators get some of the same benefits as startups.

**Results:**
- Independent evaluation found positive causal impacts on firms' revenues, value-added and assets (Menon et al., 2018).
- Venture capital invested increased 5-fold over the past decade; incubators doubled.
- Strong monitoring/evaluation: Annual reports plus commissioned independent impact evaluations.

**Success factors:**
- **Clear definitions** for "innovative startup" and "innovative SME" enable other institutions and programs to easily target these companies. Transparency through public searchable list.
- **Continuity of support**: The "innovative SME" classification extends support beyond the 5-year startup window for companies with longer development timelines.
- **Comprehensiveness**: Addresses finance, talent, markets, institutions, and intermediate services together.
- **Integration**: Subsequent complementary measures (R&D tax credits, Smart&Start Italia financing) aligned easily with ISA thanks to clear definitions.

**Key takeaway for LatAm:** The power of clear legal definitions. By formally defining "innovative startup" with verifiable criteria, the ISA created a common target for all ecosystem support measures. The public registry creates transparency and discourages gaming. The continuity feature (innovative SME reclassification) prevents premature support withdrawal.

---

## 3G. Case Study: TechLeap (Netherlands, since 2014/2019)

**What it is:** An independent non-profit, publicly funded (Ministry of Economic Affairs), operating at arm's length from government. Evolved from Startup Delta (2014) to TechLeap (2019) with larger budget, 4-year program period, and increased emphasis on scale-ups.

**Specific measures:**
- **Institutions**: Successfully advocated for reducing R&D tax deduction application time from 3 months to 1 month.
- **Culture**: "Leading Entrepreneur in Tech" annual award; Knowledge Hub with blogs, reports, and Scalelab podcast inviting successful founders.
- **Networks**: TechLeap for Scaleups community (500+ members, tight criteria: >EUR 3M funding, >20 FTE, path to internationalization, tech differentiator). "Finder" platform — online portal to explore ecosystem and identify startups, investors, accelerators, service providers.
- **Markets**: Partner of ScaleNL — soft-landing acceleration for cohorts of 10 founders to the US (3 months coaching + mission). Startup Officers Network (government representatives startups can contact to explore public procurement opportunities).
- **Finance**: "Connector for Ventures" selects high-tech companies looking to raise >EUR 2M, matches them with curated investors through "Connector for Investors." Knowledge sessions and annual investor event.
- **Knowledge**: "Science to Impact" initiative to increase research commercialization; network of knowledge institutions, companies, investors, policy makers. Priorities: integrate research commercialization in academic culture, promote public-private co-creation, ensure financial resources for academic startups.
- **Talent**: "Boost Sessions" for community members on accessing talent, capital, internationalizing, product development, leadership. "Rise" program: 8 weeks in-person training for early-stage scaleups (9 per quarter). "Shine" program for more mature scaleups.
- **Intermediate Services**: Database of 260 organizations supporting deeptech companies. "Incubators United" — collaboration of public incubators connected to universities, organizing cross-regional activities, sharing mentor networks, best practices.

**Results:**
- Netherlands performs above OECD average on 9 of 10 elements.
- Venture capital invested increased 5-fold over past decade; incubators doubled.
- Stakeholders report TechLeap performs functions that would not exist without it (van den Toren et al., 2023 evaluation).

**Success factors:**
- **Arms-length independence**: High flexibility to set agenda and respond to evolving needs. Agility was critical during COVID-19.
- **Community-building focus**: Activities geared toward creating a more cohesive, collaborative ecosystem rather than direct financial support.
- **Visibility**: Extensive events, networking, media appearances, data publications.
- **Current focus on deeptech and scaleups** reflects targeted approach based on effectiveness.

**Key takeaway for LatAm:** TechLeap's core innovation is addressing financing bottlenecks through curated networks rather than direct funding. The "Connector" model (matching vetted companies with vetted investors) and the "Incubators United" model (cross-regional incubator collaboration sharing mentors and best practices) are both replicable at lower budgets. The Science to Impact initiative shows how to bridge academia-entrepreneurship gaps.

---

## 3H. Common Characteristics of Successful Ecosystem Policies

All three case studies share these characteristics:

1. **Holistic**: They strengthen multiple ecosystem elements simultaneously rather than targeting just one.
2. **Arms-length**: They operate through independent entities, not directly through government bureaucracy.
3. **Entrepreneur-empowering**: They give entrepreneurs themselves a key role in developing and driving initiatives (not just being beneficiaries).
4. **Regional**: French Tech and TechLeap both contain specific actions for regional ecosystems within the country.
5. **Ecosystem-aligned**: Their activities were deliberately designed to address specific bottlenecks identified through diagnostics (e.g., French Tech addressed France's relatively weak Networks score; ISA addressed Italy's Talent and Institutions bottlenecks).
6. **Evolved over time**: All three started simpler and evolved based on stakeholder feedback and effectiveness evidence.



---

## [Ref 4]

# EXTRACTS: REF_ANDECentral_AmericaGALI_Briefv2AD.pdf
## Acceleration and Access to Finance in Central America
### GALI Knowledge Brief (ANDE / Emory University, March 2021)

**Source:** Davidson, A. (Author); Aguilera, C. (Consultant); Ducoing, M., Hernandez, E., Hume, V. (Editors). Global Accelerator Learning Initiative (GALI), ANDE / Emory University.

**Sample:** 809 ventures in Central America that applied to 21 acceleration programs (2013-2018). Programs run by TechnoServe (8 programs, 345 ventures), Pomona Impact (5, 188), Agora Partnerships (4, 102), INCAE (1, 74), Thunderbird for Good (1, 54), Impact Hub (1, 24), Fusades (1, 22).

---

### Extracto 4A: Venture Profile at Application — Pre-Acceleration Baseline
**PDF Section:** How much funding do entrepreneurs have pre-acceleration? (pp. 7-8)
**ANDE Domain:** F (Financiamiento), S (Servicios de Apoyo)

| Characteristic | Value |
|---|---|
| Sample size | 809 ventures, Central America |
| Ventures in existence <=5 years | Majority |
| Post-revenue at application | 73% |
| At least 1 full-time employee | 79% |
| Had received ANY financing | 40% |
| Most common financing type | Debt (banks most common source) |
| Equity sources (N=78) | Angels, Government, Companies |
| Debt sources (N=173) | Banks (98), Non-bank FIs (31), Other (17), Friends/Family (13) |
| Philanthropy sources (N=132) | Government (44), Nonprofits (44), Friends/Family (26), Business plan competitions (26) |
| Ventures with funding: accepted vs. rejected | 41% vs. 39% (nearly identical) |
| Funded ventures older on avg | 7.4 years vs. 6.9 years |
| Funded ventures 2x more likely to | Earn revenue and have >=1 FTE |

**Sectors most likely to have pre-acceleration funding:**
| Sector | N | % with funding |
|---|---|---|
| Energy | 20 | 70% |
| Supply chain services | 37 | 57% |
| Artisanal | 59 | 53% |
| Education | 28 | 46% |
| Agriculture | 154 | 39% |
| Environment | 69 | 39% |
| ICT | 45 | 38% |
| Health | 38 | 37% |
| Tourism | 38 | 37% |
| Infrastructure | 27 | 19% |

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Benchmark for F4 (angel activity), F6 (seed capital), and S1 (accelerator effectiveness). In nascent Central American ecosystems, only 40% of accelerator applicants have ANY prior financing. Banks dominate debt supply; angel investors are scarce. These baselines help score F-domain indicators in comparable Latin American ecosystems.

---

### Extracto 4B: Fundraising Expectations vs. Reality — The Equity Gap
**PDF Section:** What types of financing are accelerator applicants seeking? + Are accelerated ventures securing new investment? (pp. 9-12)
**ANDE Domain:** F (Financiamiento), S (Servicios de Apoyo)

**12-month fundraising plans (N=809, Central America):**
| Capital type | % seeking (12-mo) | % seeking (3-yr) |
|---|---|---|
| Equity | 32% | 35% |
| Debt | 32% | 30% |
| Philanthropy | 30% | 26% |

**Latin America comparison (N=7,502):**
| Capital type | % seeking (12-mo) | % seeking (3-yr) |
|---|---|---|
| Equity | 47% | 45% |
| Debt | 23% | 20% |
| Philanthropy | 33% | 26% |

**Key finding:** Central American entrepreneurs seek more debt and less equity compared to broader LatAm. Entrepreneurs are often not prepared to receive equity: they cannot demonstrate profitability/scalability, many are family businesses resistant to outside partners, and fewer are open to foreign investors.

**Fundraising expectations vs. actuals for accelerated ventures (N=255):**
| Capital type | % expected to fundraise | % actually raised | Avg expected | Avg raised |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Equity (N=84) | 33% | 5% | ,707 | ,740 |
| Debt (N=97) | 38% | 26% | ,114 | ,187 |
| Philanthropy (N=76) | 30% | 15% | ,146 | ,430 |

**Critical gap:** Only 5% of accelerated ventures secured equity investment (vs. 33% expected). Average equity raised was ,740 against expectations of ,707 -- a 97% shortfall. This is the most severe fundraising gap.

**Central America vs. Latin America fundraising actuals:** Central American ventures secured new investment at similar rates to broader LatAm, but average amounts were significantly lower.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Fundamental benchmark for scoring F1 (total investment), F2 (rounds), F3 (VCs), F4 (angels). The 97% equity gap quantifies the financing bottleneck. Use these figures when evaluating whether an ecosystem's financing landscape is "typical" for the region or significantly below/above norm.

---

### Extracto 4C: Top Desired Benefits of Acceleration
**PDF Section:** Figure 5 — Top desired benefit of acceleration (p. 10)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo), F (Financiamiento)

**What Central American entrepreneurs want most from accelerators (N=809):**
| Benefit | % citing as top |
|---|---|
| Business skills | 26% |
| Network development | 20% |
| Direct funding | 19% |
| Mentorship | 14% |
| Access to investors | 10% |
| Awareness and credibility | 6% |
| Access to other entrepreneurs | 4% |

**Key insight:** "Access to investors" ranks only 5th despite most ventures seeking financing. Entrepreneurs prioritize business skills and networks over direct investor connections. "Direct funding" (from accelerator itself) outranks investor access, suggesting entrepreneurs prefer accelerator-provided capital over investor matchmaking.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Informs S1 (accelerator) and S4 (mentor) scoring. When evaluating accelerator quality, assess whether programs deliver what entrepreneurs actually value: skills, networks, and direct capital -- not just demo days. Accelerators that only offer investor access miss the primary demand.

---

### Extracto 4D: Gender Gaps in Acceleration and Finance
**PDF Section:** Gender differences sidebar (p. 14)
**ANDE Domain:** F (Financiamiento), C (Cultura)

**Pre-acceleration investment by founding team gender:**
| Team composition | % with investment pre-acceleration |
|---|---|
| Women-only teams | 27% |
| Mixed teams | 47% |
| Men-only teams | 40% |

**Key findings:**
- Women-led ventures are less likely to enter accelerators with financing (27% vs. 40% men-only)
- Women-led ventures often fall further behind men-led ventures POST-acceleration
- Some gap explained by lower risk tolerance and less openness to outside ownership among women founders
- A significant unexplained gap remains, likely attributable to gender biases in investment and acceleration practices

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Critical for gender-disaggregated analysis in F-domain and C-domain. When ecosystems report low female entrepreneurship, these benchmarks quantify the structural disadvantage. A 13-point gap (27% vs. 40%) in pre-acceleration funding represents the baseline gender financing gap in Central America.

---

### Extracto 4E: Accelerator-Investor Relationship Models
**PDF Section:** The relationship between accelerators and finance providers (pp. 15-16)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo), F (Financiamiento)

**Two categories of accelerator-investor relationships:**

1. **Formal relationship:** Agreement to feed accelerated ventures into investment pipeline. Accelerator and investor often under the same umbrella organization, or formal partnerships where a lender provides capital for graduates.

2. **Informal relationship:** Investors in accelerator's general network, invited to demo days, one-on-one meetings, or ecosystem convenings. Sometimes provide financial knowledge as curriculum. Described as need-based, not strategic or ongoing.

**Supply-side barriers (investor perspective):**
- Ventures too early-stage and risky for sizeable investments ("another league")
- Entrepreneurs unprepared to pitch (cannot communicate financial projections, lack historical documentation)
- Entrepreneurs unable to demonstrate scalability
- Aversions to international financing and ownership sharing
- Difficulty accessing accurate market size information; reliance on proxies adds risk

**Demand-side barriers (accelerator perspective):**
- Traditional financiers have unrealistic expectations of startups
- Banks require proof of revenue and collateral
- Inflexible interest rates not differentiated for startups vs. established businesses
- Regional economic volatility makes SMEs unpredictable

**Recommendations from the brief:**
1. Accelerators should make "capital raised" an impact metric for program success
2. Accelerators should prioritize relationships with impact investors willing to work with riskier early-stage ventures
3. Donors should provide flexible capital to accelerators to invest directly in ventures

**53% of impact investors in Latin America target below-market-rate returns** (per ANDE's bi-annual impact investing trends report), making them more appropriate for early-stage ventures from acceleration programs.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Directly relevant to S1 (accelerator quality), F3 (VC presence), F4 (angel activity). When an ecosystem has accelerators but low F-domain scores, this framework diagnoses the disconnect. The formal vs. informal relationship model helps assess whether accelerator-investor linkages are functional or superficial.

---

### Extracto 4F: Structural Barriers to Financing in Small/Volatile Economies
**PDF Section:** Investor and accelerator quotes (pp. 13, 16)
**ANDE Domain:** F (Financiamiento), M (Mercados), P (Politica)

**Core structural challenges identified:**
1. **Market size constraint:** Individual Central American country markets are very small and risky. Investors uncertain how to achieve profitable exits.
2. **Scalability gap:** Entrepreneurs struggle to articulate how to expand beyond a single small-country market.
3. **Cultural resistance:** Entrepreneurs seek subsidized financing and low amounts; do not understand what partnership with an investment fund entails. Some expect fund to behave like a commercial bank.
4. **Size mismatch:** Companies with impact profiles do not reach the level/size required by investment funds.
5. **Economic volatility:** Central American economies are volatile, making SME revenues unpredictable and increasing risk premiums.
6. **Bank inflexibility:** Banks claim SME focus but apply same requirements as for any client. No differentiation for startups.

**Key benchmark data:**
- In 2016: B VC invested in USA vs. .6B in India vs. M in Mexico
- CrunchBase data: seed-stage investment rounds are smaller on average in developing economies
- Developing-economy accelerators more often must facilitate deals with investors outside the country

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Essential context for M3 (sector specialization), F1 (total investment), P2 (fiscal regime). These structural barriers explain why F-domain scores remain low even when S-domain (accelerators) scores are moderate. Small market size, volatility, and cultural resistance to equity are systemic factors that cannot be solved by accelerator programming alone.

---

### Extracto 4G: COVID-19 Impact on Accelerator-Investor Connections
**PDF Section:** COVID-19 sidebar (p. 16)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo), F (Financiamiento)

**Findings:**
- Half of accelerators interviewed did not track pandemic impact on investor connections
- Mixed effects reported: transition to digital events reduced in-person interaction (negative), but may have forced reevaluation of engagement strategies (potential positive)
- Finance providers did NOT change or relax financial product requirements during pandemic
- Accelerators note investment requirements were already too demanding pre-pandemic, and remained unchanged

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Relevant for assessing post-pandemic ecosystem resilience. If investment requirements remained unchanged during a global crisis, this signals structural rigidity in the financing ecosystem that disadvantages early-stage ventures regardless of external conditions.



---

## [Ref 5]

# EXTRACTS: REF_ANDESept2021v6.pdf
## What We Know About Cultivating Entrepreneurial Ecosystems
### ANDE Evidence Synthesis (September 2021)

**Source:** Hume, V. (Research Analyst) and Davidson, A. (Research Manager), ANDE. Editorial contributions by Guttentag, M. (Director of Research and Impact, ANDE). Part of the Global Inclusive Growth Partnership (GIGP) between the Aspen Institute and the Mastercard Center for Inclusive Growth.

**Purpose:** Evidence synthesis summarizing existing research on cultivating entrepreneurial ecosystems, designed as a resource for practitioners working with small and growing businesses (SGBs).

**SGB Definition (ANDE):** Commercially viable businesses with 5-250 employees that have significant potential and ambition for growth. Typically seek growth capital from \,000 to \ million.

---

### Extracto 5A: Key Takeaways — Three Core Lessons for Ecosystem Building
**PDF Section:** Introduction and Key Takeaways (pp. 5-6)
**ANDE Domain:** All domains (cross-cutting)

**Three key takeaways:**

1. **Mature ecosystems share a common set of characteristics.** The presence and strength of entrepreneur and institutional networks, entrepreneurial culture, accelerators and other support organizations, finance providers, and supportive policy are common among mature ecosystems.

2. **Ecosystem development involves complex, non-linear interactions among these factors.** There is no singular growth path for ecosystems; entrepreneurial communities must build upon existing strengths and resources to identify areas of competitive advantage.

3. **Interventions should build upon local attributes and strengths.** While there is some evidence of successful outcomes from ecosystem-building interventions, policymakers should focus primarily on setting a strong foundation so the ecosystem can take advantage of new market opportunities as they arise organically.

**Core statistics:**
- SMEs contribute up to 40% of national GDP in developing economies
- SMEs account for 90% of jobs worldwide
- These figures reflect only formalized/registered businesses; actual contribution likely much higher

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These three takeaways underpin the diagnostic philosophy. When writing Section 7 (Recommendations), every recommendation must respect the non-linear, context-specific nature of ecosystem growth. Do not prescribe generic solutions; build upon what exists locally.

---

### Extracto 5B: Stages of Ecosystem Development — Maturity Framework
**PDF Section:** The Stages of Ecosystem Development (pp. 7-8)
**ANDE Domain:** All domains (cross-cutting)

**Three stages identified (synthesizing Startup Genome, Cukier & Kon 2018, Mack & Mayer 2015, Cantner et al. 2020):**

| Stage | Alternative names | Characteristics | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Activation** | Nascent, Birth phase | <1,000 startups, limited founder experience, significant resource gaps, few financing/mentorship opportunities, incubators beginning to emerge | Kampala, Uganda |
| **Growth** | Evolving, Globalization phase | Ecosystem elements become more apparent and specialized, high-profile entrepreneurs with some exits, investors more open to risk, entrepreneurship becoming acceptable career path | Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia (\M raised by startups in 2019) |
| **Mature** | Attraction and Integration phase | Globally recognized unicorns, exits >\B, increasing global connectedness, effective policies and programming, globally recognized as good place to start a business | Singapore (\.4B early-stage funding market), Silicon Valley |

**Kampala (Activation stage) details:**
- Ranked most entrepreneurial country by GEM in 2014
- Startup death rate still too high for significant growth
- Young, tech-savvy population; burgeoning innovation environment
- ANDE's Uganda Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Initiative (UEEI) launched in 2018: diagnostic assessment of Kampala and Gulu ecosystems, resulting in five-year development strategy

**Kuala Lumpur (Growth stage) details:**
- \M raised by startups in 2019
- Strong startup funding landscape, quality of life, growing talent base, pro-startup government policies
- Challenges: startups reluctant to expand beyond domestic markets; young talent prefers large corporations over early-stage firms
