**Critical insight:** Growth path between stages is non-linear. Most of today's mature ecosystems developed organically; intentional ecosystem-building interventions are a more recent experiment.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Maps directly to ANDE maturity levels (Naciente, Emergente, En Desarrollo, Autosostenible). Use these stage descriptions to contextualize maturity scores. The Kampala example is a model for Activation-stage ecosystem diagnostics; Kuala Lumpur illustrates Growth-stage dynamics.

---

### Extracto 5C: Five Critical Factors for Ecosystem Development
**PDF Section:** Factors Critical for Ecosystem Development (pp. 9-13)
**ANDE Domain:** All 7 domains

**Factor 1: Strong Network Connections Among Entrepreneurs and Institutions**
- **ANDE Domain mapping:** C (Cultura), S (Servicios de Apoyo)
- Social networks are critical for sourcing capital and finding reliable information
- Interpersonal connections create high-trust environments enabling experimentation, inter-sector collaboration, and access to new markets
- **NYC example:** Entrepreneurs formed tech meetups; successful entrepreneurs started the New York Angels group to invest in nascent ventures
- **Nairobi example (Endeavor):** Key strength is successful entrepreneurs' interest in reinvesting in local ventures, generating capital, confidence, and sector knowledge
- **Lagos Angels Network:** Started 2014 by local entrepreneurs, grew from 20 to 65 members, sources deals from local incubators/accelerators, partners with Bank of Industry Nigeria. Initial operational costs subsidized by infoDev
- **Atlanta counter-example:** Key technical and financial factors present, but social fragmentation and lack of local embeddedness stagnated growth and pushed enterprises out of the city

**Factor 2: An Entrepreneurial Culture**
- **ANDE Domain mapping:** C (Cultura), H (Capital Humano)
- Defined by societal norms that tolerate risk and failure, celebrate innovation and creativity, provide high social status to entrepreneurs
- "Pay it forward" behavior: entrepreneurs serve as mentors, investors, advisors to other founders
- "Entrepreneur recycling": serial entrepreneurs show repeated engagement
- Negative perceptions of entrepreneurship significantly hinder ecosystem development in developing economies
- **Extremadura, Spain example:** Government program integrating entrepreneurial skill-building into primary/secondary education, focusing on social entrepreneurship for SDGs. Evaluation found improved personal competencies and attitudes toward social responsibility.

**Factor 3: Anchor Institutions**
- **ANDE Domain mapping:** H (Capital Humano), I (I+D e Innovacion)
- Universities, large corporations, and R&D laboratories provide talent pool and technological innovation
- Startup rates higher in regions with more specialized workforces and research universities (2016 review)
- **Waterloo, Canada example:** University of Waterloo and Blackberry attracted highly skilled talent; university environment encourages student-faculty collaboration to spin off ventures
- **Techstars model:** Acceleration in partnership with corporates (back-office, space, staffing, marketing)
- Microsoft and Nike created own accelerators; often culminate in acquisitions or pilot projects

**Factor 4: Early-Stage Finance**
- **ANDE Domain mapping:** F (Financiamiento)
- Mature ecosystems have "strong, dense, and supportive community of VCs, business angels, seed investors" that are "available, visible, and accessible across sectors, demographics and geography"
- VC and angel investors attracted to more developed ecosystems, creating gaps in earlier-stage ecosystems that donors/government can fill
- **USAID MENA II (Lebanon):** Training for angel investors, matching capital, 50% equity guarantee. Trained 125+ angels, led to \.7M in investments
- **Government-backed VC funds (European sample, 2020 analysis):** Less effective in closing funding gap for older companies in developing/corrupt economies. When syndicated with private investors, effectiveness improves.
- **Ghana VCTF:** Created 2004, leveraged seed funding into >\M private sector funding for SMEs. Created Ghana Angel Investor Network (2011) and Ghana Alternative Market (GAX) on stock exchange for growth-potential SMEs.

**Factor 5: A Strong Policy Environment**
- **ANDE Domain mapping:** P (Politica y Regulacion)
- Policies that "decriminalize bankruptcy, shield shareholders from creditors, and allow entrepreneurs to quickly start over" are generally effective
- Must be formulated for specific contexts (rural, high-tech, specific sectors)
- **Uganda PMA example:** Government incentivized private companies to work with smallholder farmers in distributing effective inputs; farmers improved outputs to sustainable income
- **Chattanooga, US example:** City of ~500,000 people. First US metro with gigabit fiber optic (2010). Mayor created Chattanooga Forward Task Force, raised entrepreneurship as public policy priority. Created Innovation District with private sector, university, and foundations. Results: new accelerators/incubators, increased external funding for top 16 startups, cultural shift celebrating entrepreneurship.

**Cross-cutting insight:** "In isolation each [pillar] is conducive to entrepreneurship but insufficient to sustain it" (Isenberg). "How well the elements in an ecosystem are connected is crucial to how the system functions" (Motoyama & Knowlton).

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These 5 factors map directly onto the 7 ANDE domains. Use as qualitative framework when analyzing domain interactions in Section 5 (strategic analysis). The counter-example of Atlanta (strong factors but weak networks = stagnation) is powerful evidence that connectivity matters as much as individual domain scores.

---

### Extracto 5D: The Role of Local Context and Entrepreneur Leadership
**PDF Section:** Factors Critical for Ecosystem Development, second half (pp. 13-14)
**ANDE Domain:** C (Cultura), S (Servicios de Apoyo)

**Key argument: Build on what exists locally, not imported models.**
- Policymakers should use local conditions as building blocks rather than emulating other ecosystems with different core strengths
- Mature ecosystems have "place-specific" qualities that acted as foundations: Oxford's proximity to Heathrow, Swiss medical tech from precision watch-making skills
- Serendipity often involved: 2008 financial crisis led to NYC tech industry; fiber optic cable arrival spurred Chattanooga

**Resource-deprived or island nations investing in human capital:**
- Taiwan: Developed strategies to retain engineers trained in the US. Between 1988-1998, ~40,000 engineers returned, forming the backbone of IT components industry
- Iceland and New Zealand: Similarly invested heavily in human capital as base for ecosystem development

**Entrepreneurs must lead (not development organizations):**
- "State interventions like public venture capital investments, building incubators, or training schemes can add resources to an ecosystem... but without sufficiently thick networks between entrepreneurs based on a supportive culture... the resources will likely have limited impact" (Spigel & Harrison 2017)
- "The creation of strong, well-functioning ecosystems depends on leadership from the entrepreneurial community to create cohesive and dense networks based on a culture of trust, reciprocity, and risk taking" (Spigel & Harrison 2017)
- Bangalore vs. Nairobi: Bangalore had more expansive growth because it was driven by highly successful entrepreneurs rather than by NGO initiatives (Endeavor study, Morris & Torok 2018)

**Isenberg's principle:** "There's no exact formula for creating an entrepreneurial economy; there are only practical, if imperfect, road maps."

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Critical framing for Section 7 (Recommendations). Recommendations must identify which local actors can lead implementation -- not just what should be done, but who should drive it. If C-domain (culture/networks) is weak, top-down interventions in other domains will have limited impact. Use Bangalore vs. Nairobi comparison as evidence for entrepreneur-led ecosystem development.

---

### Extracto 5E: Case Study — New York City Ecosystem Intervention
**PDF Section:** Case Studies — NYC (pp. 15-16)
**ANDE Domain:** P (Politica), H (Capital Humano), F (Financiamiento), C (Cultura), S (Servicios)

**Context:** 2008 financial crisis as exogenous shock. NYC lost 90,000 jobs, consumer spending fell 11% by 2009.

**Intervention:** NYCEDC + mayor's office designed initiative to diversify regional economy. First step: stakeholder-driven analysis of the tech startup ecosystem.

**Four challenges identified and policies designed:**
| Challenge | Policy/Program |
|---|---|
| 1. Lack of physical space for entrepreneurs | Network of co-working spaces/incubators |
| 2. Lack of technology-specialized talent | Attraction of science/tech universities (PPP models "Applied Sciences NYC") |
| 3. Insufficient seed capital for local startups | City-supported seed fund ("NYC Entrepreneurial Fund" and "NYC Seed") |
| 4. Limited, uncoordinated community of tech innovators | Competitions ("Big Apps") + political support for community events + marketing campaign with mayor's office |

**Results (by 2015):**
- Second largest tech ecosystem in US (after Silicon Valley)
- Multinational tech companies (Facebook, Google, IBM) opened offices
- >14,000 new ventures
- \ billion in VC investment
- High-tech startup sector = 12% of total tax revenue
- Jobs generated at 18% annual growth rate since 2008

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Model intervention framework: diagnostic first, then targeted policies per gap. The 4-challenge/4-response matrix is a template for structuring recommendations in Section 7. Also demonstrates that city-level interventions (P-domain) can trigger rapid ecosystem growth when combined with talent attraction (H-domain) and seed capital (F-domain).

---

### Extracto 5F: Case Study — Caribbean EPIC Program (Regional Ecosystem Building)
**PDF Section:** Case Studies — Caribbean Island Countries (pp. 16-17)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo), F (Financiamiento), P (Politica), C (Cultura)

**Context:** Most Caribbean islands have low GDP, high subsistence-based micro-entrepreneurship, low private sector productivity. Specific challenges: low education, limited finance access, crime/corruption.

**Intervention:** Entrepreneurship Program for Innovation in the Caribbean (EPIC), World Bank + Global Affairs Canada. Seven-year, CAD \M trust fund across 14 Caribbean countries (launched 2010).

**Program components:**
- Two sector-specific programs (climate and mobile innovations)
- Three sector-agnostic programs (general growth-oriented entrepreneurship, one dedicated to women)
- Gender and climate integration
- Goals: grow pool of growth-oriented entrepreneurs, strengthen enabler capacity, enhance access to finance

**Results:**
- Successfully generated entrepreneurship awareness
- Increased capacity and collaboration among local enablers
- First angel investor network in the Caribbean established by EPIC
- Some evidence of increased firm competitiveness

**Shortcomings:**
- Insufficient effort to engage policymakers in sustaining the project
- Failed to identify revenue models to continue the work
- Aimed to impact multiple countries without sufficient customization for varying size, development levels, and ecosystem characteristics

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Cautionary tale for multi-geography interventions. The failure to customize for local context and engage policymakers mirrors ANDE's Table 4.3 logic: interventions must match the ecosystem's maturity level. Also demonstrates that creating first angel networks (F4) is achievable through donor intervention, but sustainability requires policy engagement (P-domain).

---

### Extracto 5G: Case Study — Start-Up Chile (International Talent Attraction)
**PDF Section:** Case Studies — Santiago, Chile (pp. 18)
**ANDE Domain:** P (Politica), C (Cultura), F (Financiamiento), S (Servicios de Apoyo)

**Context:** Chile's entrepreneurial activity grew from 17% to 26% (2020), peaking at 37% (2019).

**Intervention model (Start-Up Chile, launched 2010):**
- Government initiative to attract international entrepreneurial talent
- Initial equity-free investment of US \,000 + 6-month incubation + mentorship + demo day
- Later: programs last 4 months, offer \,000 USD with possible extension
- **Unique feature: "Social capital points"** — participants must reinvest in local community (workshops, mentoring, classes, hackathons)
- **Policy changes:** Easy immigration, single-day company incorporation

**Results:**
- Global recognition
- 1,500+ entrepreneurs from 80+ countries completed program (as of 2018)
- Total of >US \ billion raised by participants

**Challenge — Retention problem:**
- Santiago's under-developed VC market: local investors prefer locally-founded companies, require large equity stakes
- Most graduates seek investment outside Chile, subsequently relocate (often to US)
- Response: SCALE program (\,000 for growth-stage companies willing to incorporate in Chile, stay 12+ months)

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Benchmark for P1 (days to incorporate: 1 day), P2 (fiscal regime: equity-free seed capital), P3 (public programs: Start-Up Chile). Demonstrates that strong P-domain + S-domain interventions can build C-domain (culture) through social capital requirements. But F-domain weakness (underdeveloped local VC) creates retention risk. Relevant for any ecosystem where policy support outpaces financing maturity.

---

### Extracto 5H: Case Study — Estonia (Regulatory Friction Reduction)
**PDF Section:** Case Studies — Estonia (p. 19)
**ANDE Domain:** P (Politica y Regulacion), H (Capital Humano)

**Context:** Second-highest entrepreneurship performance in the EU. Significant challenges in talent, innovation, and healthy entrepreneurial environment.

**Intervention (Startup Estonia, 2013):**
- Multifaceted, multistakeholder approach to attract, train, and finance entrepreneurs from outside EU
- Reduce regulatory friction for startups
- Provide technical assistance
- Connect participants with funding
- Educate local investors on how to meet entrepreneurs' needs
- **Startup Visa program:** Easing ability of foreigners to start businesses in-country
- **Residence permit program:** Allows startups to recruit internationally

**Results:**
- 1,000+ entrepreneurs benefited
- 1,000+ founders/employees used visa support
- Impact on total startup activity unclear

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Model for P1 (days to incorporate) and P2 (fiscal regime) interventions. Estonia demonstrates that regulatory simplification + talent attraction programs can boost ecosystem even in small countries. The visa/permit programs directly address H4 (tech talent availability) through international recruitment.

---

### Extracto 5I: Synthesis — Ecosystem Building Principles for Diagnostics
**PDF Section:** Conclusion (p. 20)
**ANDE Domain:** All domains (cross-cutting)

**Three concluding lessons:**

1. **Certain factors are common among mature ecosystems:** Strong networks among entrepreneurs and institutions, entrepreneurial culture, accelerators and other support organizations, accessible early-stage finance, and supportive regulatory environment.

2. **Interventions must consider unique local characteristics.** Ecosystems are not simple; interventions need to consider interconnectedness of elements and actors. Identify existing interventions and complement them rather than implement standalone programs.

3. **Significant degree of randomness and serendipity involved.** A more practical strategy for policymakers and donors is to focus on creating "fertile soil" as outcomes will vary regardless. Building a strong foundation prepares the ecosystem for unexpected shocks and opportunities.

**Research gaps identified:**
- How diagnostic tools can develop effective interventions
- How to identify leverage points
- How to mature the local investment market

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The research gap on "how diagnostic tools can develop effective interventions" is precisely what the ANDE Phase 1 diagnostic addresses. Use this framing in Section 1 (Executive Summary) to position the diagnostic within the broader evidence base. The "fertile soil" metaphor is useful for explaining why Condition domains (P, S, H, I) must be strengthened before Outcome domains (F, C, M) can flourish -- aligning with the ANDE evolutionary sequence.

---

### Extracto 5J: Accelerators' Spillover Effect on Regional Investment
**PDF Section:** Accelerators and Other Entrepreneur Support Providers (pp. 11-12)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo), F (Financiamiento)

**Key evidence on accelerator impact:**
- Graduating startups more likely to attract equity financing than non-participants, even after accounting for selection effects (Lall et al. 2020)
- Study of 59 accelerators in 38 US metropolitan regions: establishing an accelerator increases the number of early-stage investors and can "create an entrepreneurial cluster where it did not exist before" (Fehder & Hochberg 2014)
- Bangalore research: accelerators connect entrepreneurs to broader ecosystem by effectively matching entrepreneurs to right ecosystem players (Goswami et al. 2017)

**USAID PACE program (est. 2013):**
- Designed to catalyze small business growth through increased private investment
- Interventions: direct investment in early-stage businesses, de-risking via guarantees, promoting collaboration between private capital and philanthropy, supporting intermediaries, researching findings
- Critical strategy: tackling support and financing gap through multifaceted approach combining innovative financing structures and capacity development services

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Evidence that S1 (accelerator presence) directly drives F-domain outcomes. The Fehder & Hochberg finding that accelerators "create entrepreneurial clusters" supports using accelerator presence as a leading indicator of ecosystem development. When scoring S1, consider not just the number of accelerators but their capacity to generate spillover investment activity.

---

### Extracto 5K: Government-Backed Finance Interventions — Benchmarks
**PDF Section:** Early-Stage Finance (p. 11)
**ANDE Domain:** F (Financiamiento), P (Politica)

**Benchmark data for government/donor finance interventions:**

| Intervention | Location | Key Metrics |
|---|---|---|
| USAID MENA II | Lebanon | 125+ angels trained, \.7M investments, 50% equity guarantee |
| Ghana VCTF | Ghana | Created 2004, leveraged seed into >\M private funding, created angel network (2011), created SME stock exchange listing (GAX) |
| Government VC funds | European sample | Less effective for older companies in developing/corrupt economies; more effective when syndicated with private investors |

**Key finding on public VC funds (Alperovych 2020):** Government-backed venture capital is less effective in closing funding gaps for older companies in developing economies or those perceived as corrupt. However, when syndicated with private investors, likelihood of closing the funding gap improves.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Benchmarks for F6 (public seed capital) and F3 (VC activity). The Ghana VCTF model (seed fund -> angel network -> SME stock listing) represents a complete F-domain development pathway. The finding that public VC works better when syndicated with private capital is critical for P3 (public programs) recommendations.



---

## [Ref 6]

# EXTRACTS: REF_Bernd_Wurth__Erik_Stam_and_Ben_Spigel__2023__Entrepreneurial_Ecosystem_Mechanisms.pdf
## Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Mechanisms

**Source:** `REF_Bernd_Wurth__Erik_Stam_and_Ben_Spigel__2023__Entrepreneurial_Ecosystem_Mechanisms.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Mechanisms
**Authors:** Bernd Wurth (University of Strathclyde), Erik Stam (Utrecht University), Ben Spigel (University of Edinburgh)
**Year:** 2023
**Published in:** Foundations and Trends in Entrepreneurship, Vol. 19, No. 3, pp. 224-339
**Foco:** Revision sistematica de 136 papers empiricos sobre mecanismos causales en ecosistemas emprendedores: interdependencias, causalidad ascendente/descendente, evidencia sobre como los elementos producen resultados

---

### 6A -- Marco conceptual: Cuatro categorias de mecanismos ecosistemicos
**Seccion:** Sections 3-5 (Conceptual and Empirical Perspective on EE Mechanisms)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (marco teorico)
**Tipo:** Marco analitico basado en revision sistematica

El paper organiza los mecanismos de ecosistemas en cuatro categorias, derivadas del analisis de 136 articulos empiricos:

1. **Interdependencias dentro del ecosistema** -- Como los elementos se relacionan y refuerzan mutuamente
2. **Ecosistema hacia outputs emprendedores (causalidad ascendente)** -- Como el contexto ecosistemico produce emprendimiento
3. **Ecosistema hacia resultados socioeconomicos** -- Como el output emprendedor se traduce en valor agregado
4. **Causalidad descendente y dependencia de trayectoria** -- Como la actividad emprendedora pasada retroalimenta al ecosistema

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Mapeo directo con la logica diagnostica ANDE. Los dominios Condicion (P, S, H, I) operan en las categorias 1-2. Los dominios Resultado (F, C, M) capturan la categoria 3. La categoria 4 explica el patron Condicion-Resultado: cuando Condiciones son fuertes pero Resultados debiles ("problema de conexion"), falla la categoria 2. Cuando Resultados son fuertes pero Condiciones debiles ("sistema fragil"), la retroalimentacion de categoria 4 es insostenible.

---

### 6B -- Evidencia empirica sobre interdependencias entre elementos
**Seccion:** Section 5.1 (Interdependencies Within Entrepreneurial Ecosystems)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (todos los dominios)
**Tipo:** Hallazgos empiricos de revision sistematica

La revision identifica tres clusters de elementos fuertemente interdependientes (correlaciones significativas tanto simultaneas como intertemporales):

**Cluster 1: Talento-Cultura-Servicios.** Niveles de educacion, cultura emprendedora (tasa de nuevas firmas) y servicios empresariales muestran correlaciones fuertes. Forman un subsistema auto-reforzante.

**Cluster 2: Conocimiento-Liderazgo.** Inversion en I+D y prevalencia de lideres de proyectos de innovacion estan fuertemente correlacionados, reflejando la dimension de economia del conocimiento.

**Cluster 3: Infraestructura-Demanda.** Infraestructura fisica (accesibilidad) y demanda de mercado estan fuertemente correlacionadas, reflejando la dimension poblacion/prosperidad.

**Hallazgo contra-intuitivo:** La calidad de gobierno esta consistentemente correlacionada negativamente con los otros elementos en regiones holandesas. Esto sugiere que la calidad institucional formal puede no ser el motor de otros elementos en economias avanzadas, o que regiones con instituciones mas debiles compensan a traves de otros mecanismos.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Al puntuar indicadores interdependientes, se esperan patrones correlacionados. Si S1-S5 puntuan alto pero H1-H5 bajo, puede haber un problema de medicion o un desacoplamiento inusual que merece investigacion. Los tres clusters proveen una plantilla para identificar si las fortalezas del ecosistema estan concentradas en un subsistema o distribuidas. Usar en Seccion 5 del reporte.

---

### 6C -- Mecanismos especificos de causalidad ascendente (ecosistema a output)
**Seccion:** Section 5.2 (Entrepreneurial Ecosystems and Entrepreneurial Outputs)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (F, S, H, I, C)
**Tipo:** Sintesis de evidencia empirica sobre mecanismos causales

Cuatro mecanismos principales a traves de los cuales las condiciones ecosistemicas se traducen en actividad emprendedora:

**Derrames de conocimiento:** Universidades e instituciones de investigacion generan conocimiento que emprendedores comercializan. Medido a traves de patentes, gasto en I+D, colaboraciones universidad-industria. Los derrames son geograficamente acotados y disminuyen con la distancia.

**Efectos de red:** Las redes sociales distribuyen conocimiento, trabajo y capital. El rol del "dealmaker" (Feldman y Zoller, 2012) es particularmente importante -- un numero reducido de individuos altamente conectados que conectan diferentes partes del ecosistema. La estructura de la red importa mas que su tamano.

**Munificencia de recursos:** La disponibilidad de financiamiento (especialmente VC), talento y servicios de apoyo reduce barreras a la entrada y crecimiento. Pero la disponibilidad sola es insuficiente -- los recursos deben ser accesibles a los emprendedores, lo que depende de organizaciones intermediarias.

**Legitimidad y cultura:** Normas sociales sobre emprendimiento, tolerancia al fracaso y presencia de role models visibles crean un contexto cultural que fomenta el comportamiento emprendedor. Es el mecanismo mas dificil de medir pero con fuerte respaldo empirico.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Estos mecanismos explican POR QUE importan ciertos indicadores. Al puntuar F3 (VCs locales) o F4 (Angeles activos), el diagnostico debe evaluar no solo su conteo sino su accesibilidad. Un fondo de VC con sede en la ciudad pero que solo invierte fuera contribuye menos de lo que sugiere el conteo. Para S4 (Mentores), la calidad de red ("dealmakers") puede importar mas que la cantidad. Usar en notas cualitativas de indicadores.

---

### 6D -- Causalidad descendente y el efecto de reciclaje emprendedor
**Seccion:** Section 5.4 (Downward Causation and Path Dependencies)
**Dominio ANDE:** F (Financiamiento), C (Cultura), S (Servicios de Apoyo)
**Tipo:** Evidencia empirica sobre retroalimentacion

La revision provee evidencia solida del "reciclaje emprendedor" -- el proceso por el cual emprendedores exitosos reinvierten en el ecosistema:

**Patron empirico:** Fundadores de firmas de alto crecimiento luego se convierten en VCs, angeles inversores, mentores, miembros de directorio y lideres de organizaciones ecosistemicas. Esto crea un ciclo de retroalimentacion positiva.

**Evidencia cuantitativa:** La prevalencia de firmas de alto crecimiento en tiempo T se correlaciona positivamente con valores subsecuentes de elementos ecosistemicos en T+3, particularmente:
- Demanda (crecimiento de mercado)
- Liderazgo de proyectos
- Servicios empresariales
- Disponibilidad de capital de riesgo

**Dependencia de trayectoria:** Correlaciones intertemporales muy fuertes en los valores de elementos ecosistemicos sugieren alta dependencia de trayectoria. Regiones fuertes tienden a mantenerse fuertes; regiones debiles tienden a mantenerse debiles. Implicacion critica: la intervencion temprana importa desproporcionadamente porque establece la trayectoria.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Para ecosistemas "Naciente" o "Emergente," la ausencia de exits (F5) y angeles inversores (F4) es tanto sintoma como causa de debilidad. El mecanismo de reciclaje esta ausente. Las recomendaciones deben apuntar a crear la primera generacion de recicladores -- a traves de emprendedores de diaspora que retornan, spin-offs corporativos, o importando mentores experimentados de ecosistemas maduros. Usar en Secciones 6 (Retos) y 7 (Recomendaciones).

---

### 6E -- El sistema no se puede descomponer: logica del indice compuesto
**Seccion:** Section 6.2 (referenciando Stam y Van de Ven extensamente)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (metodologia)
**Tipo:** Hallazgo metodologico critico

**El ecosistema debe tratarse como un sistema completo.** La regresion multivariada de elementos individuales sobre output emprendedor es inapropiada por razones sustantivas y estadisticas:
- **Sustantiva:** En un ecosistema no hay relaciones directas uno-a-uno. Los efectos son mediados, moderados e interactivos.
- **Estadistica:** Alta multicolinealidad entre elementos invalida los coeficientes individuales.

**Enfoques de construccion de indice probados (datos holandeses):**
- **Indice aditivo** (E1 + E2 + ... + En): Valores de 5.99 a 12.42. Variacion moderada.
- **Indice multiplicativo** (E1 x E2 x ... x En): Valores de 0.001 a 6.376. La region mas baja tiene menos de 0.01% del valor de la mas alta.
- **Indice logaritmico multiplicativo** (ln(E1) + ... + ln(En)): Valores de -2.52 a 0.67. Mejor simetria.

**Hallazgo clave:** El indice multiplicativo captura mejor la naturaleza interactiva del sistema -- una debilidad en cualquier elemento individual arrastra al indice completo de manera desproporcionada. Esto provee la base matematica para el concepto de "cuello de botella."

**R-cuadrado del indice ecosistemico prediciendo firmas de alto crecimiento:** 0.437 (2009), 0.625 (2012), 0.526 (2015).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Valida el enfoque ANDE de identificar "cuellos de botella." Un modelo multiplicativo significa que el dominio mas debil tiene un impacto negativo desproporcionado en el desempeno general. La prioridad de politica debe ser elevar el piso (abordar el dominio mas debil) y no el techo (fortalecer mas los ya fuertes). Esto respalda directamente la logica de scoring ANDE donde el dominio con puntuacion mas baja se senala como cuello de botella critico.

---

### 6F -- Vinculos inter-ecosistemicos y apertura del sistema
**Seccion:** Section 3.5 (Inter-Ecosystem Links)
**Dominio ANDE:** F (Financiamiento), H (Capital Humano), M (Mercados)
**Tipo:** Marco conceptual con evidencia empirica

Los ecosistemas no operan en aislamiento:

**Mecanismos inter-ecosistemicos:**
- **Movilidad de talento:** Emprendedores y trabajadores calificados se mueven entre regiones, llevando conocimiento y redes.
- **Flujos de inversion:** El VC es cada vez menos localmente acotado. Inversores externos pueden fortalecer ecosistemas locales (trayendo capital y conexiones) o debilitarlos (extrayendo valor hacia otras regiones).
- **Flujos de conocimiento:** A traves de universidades, conferencias y redes profesionales, el conocimiento cruza fronteras ecosistemicas.

**Anidamiento y jerarquia:** Algunos elementos son mas localmente acotados que otros. Cultura y redes son fuertemente locales. Financiamiento y conocimiento son cada vez mas globales. Politica opera en multiples niveles (local, regional, nacional).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Al evaluar indicadores como F1 (Inversion total) o F3 (VCs locales), considerar si el capital es endogeno o exogeno. Un ecosistema que depende enteramente de capital externo es vulnerable a ciclos de inversion. Para H4 (Talento tech), evaluar la retencion -- una ciudad que produce talento pero lo pierde hacia hubs mas grandes tiene una "fuga" que socava otras inversiones. El campo `notes` debe capturar estos flujos direccionales.

---

### 6G -- Sintesis de implicaciones de politica publica
**Seccion:** Section 7 (Discussion and Policy Implications)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (recomendaciones)
**Tipo:** Implicaciones para politicas derivadas de evidencia

Cinco conclusiones politicamente relevantes de la evidencia empirica:

1. **Intervencion a nivel sistema, no a nivel de elemento.** Politicas que apuntan a elementos individuales (ej: mas fondos VC) sin abordar el sistema amplio tienden a fracasar. Brown, Gregson y Mason (2016) documentan el fracaso de la Intermediate Technology Initiative de Escocia, que invirtio fuertemente en creacion de conocimiento sin construir la infraestructura complementaria de redes y financiamiento.

2. **La secuencia importa.** La evidencia sobre dependencia de trayectoria sugiere que los elementos ecosistemicos deben construirse en secuencia, no simultaneamente. Cultura y capital humano proveen la base; servicios y redes habilitan la actividad; financiamiento y mercados siguen a partir de actividad demostrada.

3. **No "elegir ganadores."** Iniciativas gubernamentales de ecosistemas que seleccionan sectores o firmas especificas tienden a rendir menos que el desarrollo organico de abajo hacia arriba. La evidencia favorece crear condiciones habilitantes sobre dirigir resultados.

4. **Medicion sistemica.** Evaluar ecosistemas por indicadores individuales pierde los efectos interactivos. Indices compuestos que capturan el sistema como un todo son mas predictivos del output emprendedor real.

5. **Horizontes temporales largos.** Los ciclos de retroalimentacion documentados operan en ciclos de 3-10 anos. Politicas que esperan resultados a corto plazo de intervenciones ecosistemicas seran probablemente abandonadas prematuramente.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Estas conclusiones refuerzan el enfoque secuencial de ANDE (Tabla 4.3: construir Cultura y Capital Humano primero, luego Servicios, luego Politica y Financiamiento, luego Mercados e I+D). Las recomendaciones en el reporte Fase 1 deben respetar esta secuencia y establecer horizontes temporales realistas para cada intervencion. El punto 1 respalda recomendar intervenciones coordinadas que aborden multiples dominios simultaneamente, no acciones aisladas.



---

## [Ref 7]

# EXTRACTS: REF_Boutillier-Entrepreneurial_Ecosystems-2016-Wiley.pdf
## Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (Smart Innovation Set, Volume 2)

**Source:** `REF_Boutillier-Entrepreneurial_Ecosystems-2016-Wiley.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial Ecosystems
**Authors:** Sophie Boutillier, Denis Carre, Nadine Levratto
**Year:** 2016
**Published by:** ISTE Ltd / John Wiley & Sons
**ISBN:** 978-1-84821-875-8
**Foco:** Economia territorial, efectos de aglomeracion, distritos industriales, polos de competitividad franceses, politicas publicas territoriales

---

**NOTA:** Este libro es primariamente teorico, con un enfoque historico en la economia territorial europea (particularmente francesa). Su contribucion directa a diagnosticos ANDE es limitada. La mayor parte del contenido (Capitulo 1, ~50% del libro) es una revision de autores clasicos de economia territorial (Marshall, Perroux, Krugman, Becattini, Porter). Se incluyen solo los extractos con contenido mas directamente aplicable.

---

### 7A -- Los siete factores del ecosistema emprendedor segun Cohen (2006)
**Seccion:** Chapter 2, Section 2.1 (Actors and Components, pp. 47-50)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal
**Tipo:** Marco conceptual (referencia a otros autores)

El libro recopila marcos de otros autores sobre componentes del ecosistema emprendedor. Cohen (2006) identifica siete factores: (1) red informal, (2) red formal, (3) universidad, (4) gobierno, (5) material profesional y apoyo, (6) servicios de capital, y (7) talento.

Isenberg (2010, Harvard Business Review) amplia a trece factores: liderazgo, politica gubernamental, cultura, historias de exito, capital humano, capital financiero, organizaciones emprendedoras, educacion, infraestructura, clusters economicos, redes, servicios de apoyo y clientes principales.

Suresh y Ramraj (2012) -- basados en 100 casos de Tamil Nadu, India -- proponen ocho sistemas de soporte: (1) soporte moral (familia), (2) soporte financiero (bancos, VCs, angeles, mercados de capital), (3) red de apoyo (organizaciones industriales, alumni, redes online), (4) soporte gubernamental (clusters, incubadoras, infraestructura), (5) soporte tecnologico, (6) soporte de mercado, (7) soporte social (tolerancia al fracaso, vision positiva del emprendimiento), (8) soporte ambiental (recursos naturales, amenidades).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Estos marcos convergen con los 7 dominios ANDE. El marco de Suresh/Ramraj es particularmente util por venir de un contexto de pais en desarrollo (India) donde el "soporte moral" (familia/comunidad) juega un rol que no siempre se captura en los indicadores ANDE formales. Considerar en notas cualitativas del dominio Cultura (C5: Confianza social).

---

### 7B -- Los tres estratos del ecosistema emprendedor
**Seccion:** Chapter 2, Section 2.2 (Operation of the EE, pp. 56-57, Figure 2.3)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (modelo conceptual)
**Tipo:** Marco de capas

Los autores proponen un modelo de tres estratos del ecosistema:

**Estrato 1 (Sustrato/Base):** Elementos fundamentales del ecosistema divididos en dos familias:
- **Superestructuras:** Instituciones politicas y legales (estado y autoridades locales), representantes academicos y colectivos, cultura, redes de financiamiento, demanda de empresas y hogares
- **Activos intangibles:** Redes de actores, recursos financieros, servicios de apoyo, conocimiento, talento, coordinacion territorial y emergencia de liderazgo

**Estrato 2 (Medio/Actividad):** Creacion de empresas -- firmas jovenes innovadoras, firmas de alto crecimiento ("gacelas"), empresas sostenibles, proyectos colectivos (clusters, polos, etc.)

**Estrato 3 (Resultado/Valor):** Creacion de valor -- productividad, ingreso, empleo, bienestar

**Aplicacion ANDE:** El modelo de tres estratos corresponde al flujo ANDE: Condiciones (estrato 1) producen Actividad emprendedora (estrato 2) que genera Resultados (estrato 3). Refuerza que los indicadores ANDE de conteo (M1: Startups activas) son mediciones del estrato 2, mientras que los dominios Condicion miden el estrato 1.

---

### 7C -- Economias de aglomeracion y critica de la "masa critica"
**Seccion:** Chapter 2, Box 2.2 (Agglomeration Economies, pp. 52-53) y Chapter 3, Section 3.1.1
**Dominio ANDE:** M (Mercados), S (Servicios de Apoyo)
**Tipo:** Marco conceptual con implicacion para ajuste por poblacion

Tres fuentes de economias de aglomeracion segun la tradicion Marshalliana:

1. **Economias de proveedores especializados** -- proximidad a proveedores de bienes y servicios intermedios reduce costos
2. **Economias de mercado laboral** -- acceso a fuerza de trabajo mas grande y especializada; empresas reclutan personal especializado mas facilmente, trabajadores invierten en formacion sabiendo que hay demanda
3. **Externalidades de conocimiento** -- diseminacion de conocimiento tecnologico; introducida en modelos de crecimiento endogeno integrando stock de conocimiento o capital humano

**El debate densidad vs. diversificacion:** Dos hipotesis competidoras:
- **Marshall-Arrow-Romer (MAR):** Las interacciones crecen con el grado de especializacion de las firmas (clusters sectoriales)
- **Jacobs:** Las interacciones son mas importantes cuanto mas diversificadas son las firmas agrupadas (diversidad sectorial)

Los autores argumentan que la aglomeracion sola no garantiza activacion del ecosistema. La proximidad de recursos basicos no asegura su funcionamiento -- se requiere activacion a traves de medios externos (politicas publicas, liderazgo). Esta distincion diferencia los proponentes de la "nueva geografia economica" (para quienes la aglomeracion basta para generar ganancias de productividad) de los autores que razonan en terminos de vinculos externos entre actores.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Relevante para el ajuste por poblacion en ciudades menores a 1M habitantes. La logica de aglomeracion sugiere que ciudades mas pequenas enfrentan desventajas estructurales no solo por menor conteo absoluto sino por menor densidad de interacciones. Sin embargo, la hipotesis de Jacobs sugiere que ciudades pequenas con alta diversidad sectorial pueden compensar parcialmente la falta de escala. Considerar al interpretar scores ajustados por poblacion.

---

### 7D -- Tipologia de politicas de incentivo para ecosistemas
**Seccion:** Chapter 2, Section 2.3.2 (Forms of State Assistance, pp. 67-69, Figure 2.6)
**Dominio ANDE:** P (Politica y Regulacion), S (Servicios de Apoyo)
**Tipo:** Tipologia de politicas publicas

Los autores proponen una matriz 2x2 de politicas de incentivo:

|  | Modificar comportamientos actuales | Adoptar nuevos comportamientos |
|---|---|---|
| **Mejora de rentabilidad via distorsion de precios** | Q-I: Crecimiento individual. Asistencia a la inversion. Reduccion de contribuciones sociales. Acceso a compras publicas. | Q-II: Eficiencia organizacional. Servicios de asesoria. Apoyo a startups. Apoyo a I+D. |
| **Incentivos condicionados a integracion colectiva** | Q-III: Mancomunacion de recursos. Accion colectiva. Compartir costos e indivisibilidades. | Q-IV: Competitividad colectiva. Apoyo a investigacion colectiva dentro de polos de competitividad. |

Las dos columnas distinguen entre eficiencia estatica (mejorar lo existente) y eficiencia dinamica (adoptar nuevos comportamientos). Las dos filas distinguen entre decisiones individuales y colectivas.

**Hallazgo clave del libro:** La mayoria del gasto publico se concentra en Q-I (asistencia individual para mejorar rentabilidad), pese a que el discurso politico habla de clusters y ecosistemas. Hay una contradiccion entre el discurso (ecosistemico, colectivo) y los flujos financieros reales (individuales, de subsidio).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Al evaluar P3 (Programas publicos activos), clasificar los programas segun esta tipologia. Programas tipo Q-I (subsidios individuales) contribuyen menos al ecosistema que programas tipo Q-IV (apoyo a competitividad colectiva). Las recomendaciones de politica deben favorecer el cuadrante Q-IV. Usar en Seccion 7 (Recomendaciones) cuando se evaluen intervenciones gubernamentales existentes.

---

### 7E -- La separacion entre creacion de empresas y ecosistemas emprendedores
**Seccion:** Chapter 2, Section 2.3.1 (Risks of Business Creation, pp. 65-67)
**Dominio ANDE:** C (Cultura), M (Mercados)
**Tipo:** Hallazgo empirico (datos franceses con aplicabilidad general)

Los autores documentan una separacion critica entre tasas de creacion de empresas y calidad del ecosistema emprendedor:

- **Alta creacion no implica alto crecimiento:** Ambientes con alta tasa de startups no son necesariamente los mas dinamicos ni los mas resilientes ante crisis economicas.
- **Emprendedores "por necesidad" vs. "por oportunidad":** La predominancia de emprendedores por necesidad (auto-empleados, micro-emprendedores) infla los numeros de creacion pero no contribuye a la competitividad territorial.
- **Firmas de alto crecimiento necesitan ambientes especificos:** Los mecanismos de apoyo a la creacion de empresas NO favorecen a las firmas de alto crecimiento. Los "gacelas" requieren recursos especificos (VC, mentoria especializada, mercados sofisticados) sin relacion con los mecanismos de creacion estandar.

**Implicacion para politica:** Las politicas que favorecen ecosistemas emprendedores deben disenarse independientemente de las que favorecen emprendimiento general. Enfocarse solo en la creacion de empresas tiene poco o nulo efecto porque pocas startups sobreviven y logran crear sinergias con otros actores economicos.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Refuerza que M1 (Startups activas) debe interpretarse con matices. Un conteo alto de startups no significa un ecosistema saludable si la mayoria son micro-empresas de subsistencia. El campo `notes` debe distinguir entre startups tech/innovadoras y auto-empleo general. Igualmente, al evaluar C2 (Eventos) y S1 (Aceleradoras), verificar si estan orientados a emprendimiento de alto crecimiento o a emprendimiento general.



---

## [Ref 8]

# EXTRACTS: REF_GALI_Knowledge_Synthesis_English.pdf
## "Does Acceleration Work? Five Years of Evidence from the Global Accelerator Learning Initiative"
**Authors:** Matthew Guttentag, Abigayle Davidson, Victoria Hume (ANDE)  
**Published:** May 2021  
**Source:** GALI / ANDE / Emory University  
**Dataset:** 360+ accelerator programs, 23,000+ ventures, 150+ countries (2013-2020)  
**Regional composition:** Latin America & Caribbean 32%, Sub-Saharan Africa 25%, US & Canada 23%, South Asia 11%

---

## Extracto 8A — Dataset Scale and Accelerator Landscape

**Section:** About the GALI data (p. 5) + The accelerator model and landscape (pp. 6-8)

Between 2013 and 2020, Emory University partnered with more than 360 acceleration programs to collect detailed data from more than 23,000 entrepreneurs who applied to their programs. Whether accepted or rejected, these entrepreneurs were resurveyed annually to gather follow-up data. The anonymized and aggregated dataset covers early-stage ventures from over 150 countries.

GALI separately conducted an online search (June 2020 - March 2021) identifying and characterizing accelerator programs worldwide, confirming 280 active accelerators across regions:

| Region | Active Accelerators |
|--------|-------------------|
| United States & Canada | 101 |
| Europe & Central Asia | 72 |
| South Asia | 49 |
| East Asia & Pacific | 35 |
| Latin America & Caribbean | 33 |
| Sub-Saharan Africa | 21 |
| Middle East & North Africa | 20 |

Roughly half of all accelerators support social impact-focused ventures. Most common sector focuses: ICT (14%), Health (12%), Financial services (11%), Agriculture/food (10%), IoT (9%). 47% are sector-agnostic.

Three accelerator archetypes by funding source (per Nesta classification):
1. **Investor-led:** driven by return-seeking VCs and angel investors
2. **Matchmaker:** set up by corporates seeking innovative solutions
3. **Ecosystem-focused:** supported by government and donors seeking to strengthen local entrepreneurial activity

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), S2 (Incubators — for differentiation benchmarks)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The 280-program global census provides benchmarks for scoring S1. For cities in Latin America, 33 programs across the region sets a baseline. The archetype classification (investor-led, matchmaker, ecosystem-focused) provides a qualitative framework for evaluating accelerator quality beyond simple counts. On average, programs select fewer than 13% of applicants per cohort — this selectivity rate is a quality signal.

---

## Extracto 8B — Accelerator vs. Incubator Distinguishing Characteristics

**Section:** The accelerator model and landscape (p. 6)

Key defining characteristics that distinguish accelerators from incubators:

| Feature | Accelerator | Incubator |
|---------|-------------|-----------|
| Duration | Fixed-term, typically 3-6 months | Open-ended |
| Structure | Cohort-based ("batches") | Individual tenants |
| Support model | Mentorship + training + seed funding | Subsidized office space + services |
| Investment | Takes equity in exchange for seed funding | No equity typically |
| Goal | Speed up: figure out which ventures can scale vs. should shut down | Prolong survival: protected space |
| Culmination | Demo day | No fixed endpoint |
| Peer dynamics | High-pressure competitive environment, peer learning | Co-location, limited structured interaction |

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), S2 (Incubators)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When scoring S1 and S2, ensure that programs counted as "accelerators" meet the cohort-based, time-limited, investment-focused criteria. Programs labeled "accelerators" that function as incubators (open-ended, no equity, no cohorts) should be reclassified under S2. This distinction is critical for accurate ecosystem mapping.

---

## Extracto 8C — Core Effectiveness Data: Revenue, Employment, and Investment

**Section:** Does acceleration work? (pp. 9-10)

Quantitative benchmarks from N=8,658 ventures comparing accepted (accelerated) vs. rejected (non-accelerated) ventures:

### Average Annual Revenue
| Timepoint | Rejected | Accepted |
|-----------|----------|----------|
| At application | $57,366 | $105,855 |
| 1 year later | $64,920 | $135,019 |
| **Growth** | **$7,554 (+13.2%)** | **$29,164 (+27.6%)** |

### Average Full-time Employees
| Timepoint | Rejected | Accepted |
|-----------|----------|----------|
| At application | 3.2 | 4.2 |
| 1 year later | 4.2 | 5.6 |
| **Growth** | **+1.0 (+31.3%)** | **+1.4 (+33.3%)** |

### Average New Investment
| Timepoint | Rejected | Accepted |
|-----------|----------|----------|
| At application | $25,723 | $52,165 |
| 1 year later | $37,578 | $76,663 |
| **Growth** | **$11,855 (+46.1%)** | **$24,498 (+47.0%)** |

These benefits come from a combination of: (a) ability to select high-potential ventures, (b) market signal of venture quality, and (c) programming that helps ventures grow. Benefits persist and strengthen in subsequent years after acceleration.

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), F1 (Total Investment), F2 (Annual Rounds), M1 (Active Startups)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These are the most robust global benchmarks for accelerator effectiveness. The ~28% revenue growth rate and ~47% investment growth rate for accelerated ventures provide outcome targets for evaluating whether local accelerators deliver comparable results. Employment growth of +1.4 FTEs per venture per year provides a job-creation benchmark for economic impact arguments in Sections 5 and 7 of ANDE reports.

---

## Extracto 8D — Selection Effects and Selectivity Rates

**Section:** Selection versus programming effects (p. 11)

On average, programs in the GALI dataset select fewer than 13% of applicants per cohort.

Key finding from Colombia study: The program had a positive impact on growth for ventures with the highest potential going into the accelerator, but entrepreneurs with less promising enterprises were not able to transform into high-growth companies through participation. This means acceleration services can only be effective when matched with the right kind of entrepreneur.

The process of identifying talent and predicting which business models have potential for scale is itself a service to the entrepreneurial ecosystem. A study of regional VC activity in the United States found that the presence of accelerators increases early-stage investment activity, not just for accelerated ventures but as an overflow to other enterprises in the region.

Both selection and programming contribute to venture success. Academic analysis found that the selection effect alone did not account for the full benefit to participating ventures in terms of equity investment — programming adds real value beyond selection.

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), F1-F4 (Finance indicators)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The 13% selectivity rate is a benchmark for accelerator quality. Programs accepting >50% of applicants may not be functioning as true accelerators. The finding that accelerator presence increases regional investment activity (spillover effect) supports arguments for accelerator development even in ecosystems with limited VC — they serve as deal-flow curators that attract investors. For scoring S1, consider both the presence AND selectivity of accelerators.

---

## Extracto 8E — Program Performance Variability

**Section:** Program performance variability (p. 12)

GALI calculated "net flow of funds" (revenue + investment growth of accelerated minus non-accelerated ventures) across 52 programs:

| Metric | Min | Mean | Max |
|--------|-----|------|-----|
| Net flow of funds | -$342,734 | $30,846 | $297,024 |
| Net revenue growth | -$343,658 | $6,021 | $251,332 |
| Net equity growth | -$119,672 | $15,517 | $252,667 |
| Net debt growth | -$111,862 | $5,050 | $111,833 |
| Net philanthropy growth | -$142,560 | $4,258 | $79,846 |

While the average performance was greater than zero, there were programs performing very poorly, with rejected ventures outperforming those that were accelerated. A 2019 paper found that distinct differences in program characteristics, including specialization and location, can consistently explain differences in the performance of accelerated ventures.

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), F1 (Total Investment)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Not all accelerators create value. When scoring S1, the mere existence of accelerators is insufficient — program quality matters enormously. The range from -$342K to +$297K in net value shows that bad accelerators actively destroy value. In ANDE diagnostics, qualitative notes about accelerator track records (cohort sizes, graduate outcomes, investment raised by alumni) should accompany raw counts. An ecosystem with 3 low-performing accelerators may score lower than one with a single high-performing program.

---

## Extracto 8F — Geographic Differences: High-Income vs. Low/Middle-Income

**Section:** Where does acceleration work? (pp. 13-14)

Critical finding for developing economy ecosystems:

1. **Investment benefits are concentrated in high-income countries.** Essentially all of the net benefits of investment for ventures participating in GALI programs went to teams in high-income countries.

2. **Revenue benefits are present in low- and middle-income countries.** Despite difficulties connecting ventures to financing, GALI data show revenue growth benefits even where other ecosystem parts are lacking.

3. **Accelerators cannot solve investment gaps alone.** To achieve full potential in connecting entrepreneurs with capital, a pool of early-stage capital must be ready and willing to make investments in program graduates.

4. **Ecosystem maturity matters.** Comparison of Nairobi vs. Bangalore: despite many support programs in Nairobi, venture ability to access capital and grow quickly is much higher in Bangalore, where the ecosystem has bottom-up development and high connectivity among successful entrepreneurs.

5. **Creative financing workarounds exist.** Examples:
   - Kinara (Indonesia): guarantees seed investments to a subset of cohort ventures, providing capital + track record for future fundraising
   - Invest2Innovate (Pakistan): raising own fund with alternative deal structures like revenue-based financing

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), F1-F6 (all Finance indicators), M1 (Active Startups)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This is the most important finding for Latin American and developing economy diagnostics. Accelerators alone cannot fix the finance bottleneck — they need a functioning investment ecosystem. When an ANDE diagnostic reveals a low F1-F4 combined with moderate S1, the Condition-Result pattern analysis should flag a "connection problem": support infrastructure exists but cannot translate to funding outcomes. Recommendations should emphasize building the capital supply side (F3 VCs, F4 Angels) alongside accelerator development, not one in isolation.

---

## Extracto 8G — Program Design: No Single Recipe, Key Principles

**Section:** How does acceleration work? (pp. 14-15)

Overarching conclusion: There is no one clear "recipe" for design choices that promotes or hinders success. No strong correlations exist between the presence or absence of any particular program element and ultimate venture success.

However, evidence points to several key principles:

1. **Customized support > standardized training.** Business support research shows standardized training programs are largely ineffective, while customized consulting support is more effective. But some standard training helps participants reach the same baseline skill level.

2. **Peer learning matters.** Facilitating transparency and networking among participants is a design choice that programs should consider carefully.

3. **Localization is essential.** Silicon Valley blueprints do not work everywhere. Demo days are unlikely to lead to funding in ecosystems with little early-stage VC.

4. **Selection-program fit is crucial.** The same program may produce completely different outcomes for entrepreneurs at different stages or sectors. GALI examination of ventures participating in multiple acceleration programs in Mexico found these ventures benefitted from both experiences — suggesting accelerators successfully provide different services for different growth stages.

5. **Qualitative selection criteria matter.** GALI analysis in Asia: ventures with financial track records were not more likely to be accepted. Managers look at less quantifiable characteristics like venture fit with market niche.

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), S4 (Mentors), H1 (Universities with entrepreneurship)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When evaluating accelerator quality for S1 scoring, assess whether programs have adapted their model to local ecosystem realities rather than importing a generic template. For recommendations (Section 7), emphasize localization and customization over replication of external models. The Mexico finding on sequential acceleration validates multi-program ecosystems — cities with accelerators serving different stages (pre-seed, seed, growth) should score higher than those with identical programs.

---

## Extracto 8H — Gender Gap in Acceleration Outcomes

**Section:** For whom does acceleration work? (pp. 15-16)

Clear pattern of all-women teams benefitting significantly less from acceleration than all-men teams:

1. **Equity gap persists after controls.** After accounting for different founder backgrounds, business stages, and sectors, a gap in accessing equity financing remains for all-women teams. This suggests investor bias.

2. **Accelerators exacerbate the gap.** Village Capital study: accelerator services allow all-men teams to more than double the amount of equity they raise post-acceleration relative to all-women teams. Women are not getting the equity-raising benefit that men get through acceleration.

3. **Representation data:** Only about 1 in 10 teams in accelerators are made up of all women, whereas just over half are all men.

4. **Selection committee diversity matters.** GALI found evidence that greater gender diversity among the selection committee is associated with greater representation of all-women teams in the program.

5. **Expatriate vs. local founder gap.** In low- and middle-income countries, expatriate entrepreneurs secure more grant funding than local founders, even after controlling for education and experience. Accelerators may unintentionally drive capital towards expatriates at the expense of local entrepreneurs.

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), F1 (Total Investment), F4 (Angel Investors), C5 (Social Trust)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Gender-disaggregated data should be noted in indicator notes where available. When recommending accelerator development (Section 7), include gender equity in program design as a best practice. The 1-in-10 representation rate for all-women teams is a benchmark — ecosystems with programs achieving higher rates should note this as a strength. The expatriate-local gap is particularly relevant for Latin American ecosystems receiving international development funding.

---

## Extracto 8I — Concentration of Outcomes (Top 10% Effect)

**Section:** Where, how, and for whom does acceleration work? (p. 13)

Only 10% of ventures in the GALI dataset account for over 95% of the total equity investment reported.

This extreme concentration means that average statistics mask highly skewed distributions. Most accelerated ventures do not achieve outsized growth — the "accelerator advantage" is concentrated among a small group of high-performing enterprises.

**ANDE domain mapping:** F1 (Total Investment), F2 (Annual Rounds), M1 (Active Startups)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When scoring F1 and F2, be aware that a small number of deals can dramatically skew ecosystem-level investment totals. A city reporting $50M in total investment may have one megadeal and near-zero activity otherwise. For accurate scoring, use median values or deal counts alongside totals. The 10%/95% concentration ratio is a global benchmark for understanding the power law distribution in startup ecosystems.

---

## Extracto 8J — Recommendations by Stakeholder Group

**Section:** Implications and recommendations (pp. 17-18)

### For Accelerator Programs:
- Adjust programming to match local ecosystem — Silicon Valley blueprint will not work everywhere
- In ecosystems with little early-stage VC, traditional demo days are unlikely to lead to funding; consider alternative investment facilitation
- Funding through equity returns on participating ventures has yet to prove sustainable outside the US; donor/government support will likely be part of the mix
- Facilitating networking and mentorship is as essential as training curriculum
- Consider how processes may contribute to biases against women and local founders
- Clearly define value-add; trying to be everything for everyone is unlikely to work

### For Entrepreneurs:
- Consider exactly what you hope to get from a program before applying; match stage and needs
- Look at types of past participants — these are the peers you will connect with
- Consider attending multiple programs if designed for different problems at different stages
- Growth benefits accrue to a relatively small number of participants — set expectations accordingly
- In locations with limited early-stage funding, accelerator participation alone is unlikely to solve the funding constraint

### For Donors and Policymakers:
- Accelerators are, on the whole, effective ways to support high-growth ventures, but financially sustaining them in nascent ecosystems is difficult — donor/government support may be necessary
- Flexible support that avoids imposing additional constraints and burdens is most useful
- Accelerators alone cannot solve constraints in nascent ecosystems; they are not replacements for broader business support and policies for SMEs
- Do not assume acceleration benefits all types of entrepreneurs equally
- No one-size-fits-all "recipe" exists — match program elements to targeted entrepreneurs and local ecosystem realities

### For Investors:
- Be clear upfront about the entrepreneur profile you are realistically likely to invest in
- Engage more closely with accelerators to understand their investment readiness programming
- Consider alternative investment approaches (e.g., Village Capital's peer scoring fund model)

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), P3 (Public Programs), F1-F6 (Finance), C1-C2 (Culture events)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These recommendations directly map to ANDE Section 7 guidance. For ecosystems scored as "Naciente" or "Emergente," the donor/policymaker insights about flexible funding and ecosystem-level support are critical. The explicit warning that accelerators cannot solve nascent ecosystem constraints should be reflected in diagnostic recommendations — avoid recommending accelerators as a silver bullet when underlying conditions (capital, talent, culture) are missing. The Tabla 4.3 guidance to "Esperar" on certain domains at early maturity levels aligns with GALI's finding that accelerators need complementary ecosystem elements to function.

---

## Extracto 8K — Future Research Priorities and Unanswered Questions

**Section:** The future of accelerator research (p. 19)

GALI's prioritized research questions (2021 onward):

1. **Funding model sustainability:** How does funding source (donor, corporate, equity returns) strengthen or hinder accelerator impact? What models allow self-sustainability in nascent ecosystems?

2. **Social/environmental impact measurement:** Do impact-oriented accelerators actually affect ventures' social/environmental impact? Do ventures adjust toward growth at the expense of impact, or vice versa?

3. **Ecosystem stage matching:** At what stage of entrepreneurial ecosystem development can accelerators provide the greatest ecosystem-level impact?

4. **Serving underrepresented entrepreneurs:** How can program design (selection, programming, network development) better serve women and local entrepreneurs in developing economies?

**ANDE domain mapping:** S1 (Accelerators), I+D (Innovation), P3 (Public Programs)  
**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Question 3 (ecosystem stage matching) directly connects to ANDE's Tabla 4.3 intervention-by-maturity framework. In "Naciente" ecosystems, ANDE guidance says to "Esperar" on services — GALI's acknowledgment that accelerators may not be effective at the earliest ecosystem stages reinforces this. Question 1 about funding sustainability is relevant when evaluating whether counted accelerators (S1) are financially viable long-term or dependent on expiring donor funding.

---

## [Ref 9]

# EXTRACTS: REF_STAM.pdf
## Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Elements

**Source:** `REF_STAM.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Elements
**Authors:** Erik Stam (Utrecht University) y Andrew Van de Ven (University of Minnesota)
**Year:** 2021 (aceptado 2019, publicado en Small Business Economics, 57, pp. 1307-1326)
**Foco:** Modelo de 10 elementos del ecosistema emprendedor, instrumento de medicion, Indice de Ecosistema Emprendedor, datos de 12 regiones holandesas, interdependencia de elementos, concepto de cuello de botella
**REFERENCIA FUNDACIONAL para diagnosticos ANDE**

---

### 9A -- El modelo de 10 elementos del ecosistema emprendedor
**Seccion:** Section 4 (Propositions) y Table 1
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (marco teorico fundacional)
**Tipo:** Modelo conceptual con definiciones operativas

Stam y Van de Ven definen el ecosistema emprendedor como todos los actores y factores interdependientes que habilitan y restringen el emprendimiento dentro de un territorio particular. Proponen 10 elementos agrupados en conceptos mas abstractos:

| Concepto | Constructo | Elemento | Definicion |
|---|---|---|---|
| Instituciones | Instituciones formales | Instituciones formales | Las reglas del juego en la sociedad |
| Instituciones | Instituciones informales | Cultura | Contexto cultural |
| Redes sociales | Redes | Redes | El contexto social de los actores, el grado en que estan conectados |
| Recursos | Recursos fisicos | Infraestructura fisica | El contexto fisico que permite a los actores encontrarse |
| Recursos | Recursos financieros | Finanzas | La presencia de medios financieros para invertir en actividades que aun no generan retorno |
| Recursos | Liderazgo | Liderazgo | Liderazgo que provee guia y direccion para la accion colectiva |
| Recursos | Capital humano | Talento | Las habilidades, conocimiento y experiencia de los individuos |
| Recursos | Conocimiento | Conocimiento | Inversiones en creacion de conocimiento (cientifico y tecnologico) |
| Recursos | Medios de consumo | Demanda | La presencia de medios financieros en la poblacion para comprar bienes y servicios |
| Recursos | Servicios al productor | Servicios intermediarios | Los insumos de servicios intermediarios en funciones propietarias |
| Creacion de valor | Emprendimiento productivo | Emprendimiento productivo | Cualquier actividad emprendedora que contribuye directa o indirectamente al output neto de la economia |

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Este modelo es el antecedente directo de los 7 dominios ANDE. El mapeo es:

| Stam | ANDE |
|---|---|
| Instituciones formales | P: Politica y Regulacion |
| Cultura | C: Cultura |
| Redes | C + S (transversal) |
| Infraestructura fisica | S5 (internet), H5 (acceso internet) |
| Finanzas | F: Financiamiento |
| Liderazgo | No tiene dominio propio en ANDE; se distribuye en S y C |
| Talento | H: Capital Humano |
| Conocimiento | I: I+D e Innovacion |
| Demanda | M: Mercados (parcialmente) |
| Servicios intermediarios | S: Servicios de Apoyo |
| Emprendimiento productivo | Output medido por M1 (Startups activas) |

Elementos de Stam que ANDE no tiene como dominio separado: Liderazgo (distribuido), Demanda (parcial en M), Infraestructura fisica (parcial en S/H). Esto puede crear puntos ciegos en el diagnostico ANDE.

---

### 9B -- Las tres proposiciones causales del modelo
**Seccion:** Section 4 (Propositions, pp. 1312-1313)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (logica causal)
**Tipo:** Proposiciones teoricas centrales con evidencia empirica

El modelo plantea tres proposiciones causales que constituyen el nucleo teorico:

**Proposicion 1 (Co-evolucion):** Los elementos del ecosistema emprendedor son mutuamente interdependientes y co-evolucionan en un territorio.

**Proposicion 2 (Causalidad ascendente -- estructura afecta agencia):** Los diez elementos observables del ecosistema explican los niveles de actividad emprendedora en un territorio.

**Proposicion 3 (Causalidad descendente -- agencia afecta estructura):** Las actividades emprendedoras previas retroalimentan los elementos del ecosistema en un territorio. Ejemplos: emprendedores exitosos se convierten en capitalistas de riesgo, role models, lideres y desarrolladores de redes en la region (Bosma et al. 2012; Mason y Harrison 2006).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** La Proposicion 1 justifica el uso de un score compuesto por dominio (promedio de indicadores). La Proposicion 2 es la base del diagnostico Fase 1: los scores de los 7 dominios predicen el nivel de emprendimiento productivo. La Proposicion 3 explica por que ecosistemas emergentes sin historial de exits (F5=0) tienen dificultad para desarrollar financiamiento: el ciclo de retroalimentacion no ha arrancado.

---

### 9C -- Operacionalizacion de los 10 elementos: indicadores empiricos
**Seccion:** Section 5 (Measuring EE Elements, pp. 1313-1318) y Table 2
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (instrumentacion)
**Tipo:** Definiciones operativas con fuentes de datos -- REFERENCIA CRITICA

Mediciones utilizadas para los 12 regiones (provincias) de Holanda:

| Elemento | Variable | Indicador empirico | Fuente |
|---|---|---|---|
| Instituciones formales | QUALGOV | 4 componentes: corrupcion, estado de derecho, efectividad gubernamental, voz y rendicion de cuentas | Quality of Government Survey |
| Cultura | NEWFIRM | Nuevas firmas registradas por cada 1,000 habitantes | CBS (Oficina Nacional de Estadisticas) |
| Infraestructura | ACCESS | 3 componentes: accesibilidad por carretera, ferrocarril, aeropuertos (vuelos de pasajeros a 90 min en auto); relativo al promedio UE | EU Regional Competitiveness Index |
| Demanda | DEMAND | 3 componentes: poder adquisitivo per capita, producto regional, poblacion total; relativo al promedio UE | EU Regional Competitiveness Index |
| Redes | INNOCOL | Porcentaje de firmas que colaboran para innovacion | EU Community Innovation Survey |
| Liderazgo | PROLEAD | Lideres de proyectos de innovacion por cada 1,000 empresas (proyectos con subsidio publico holandes o europeo, 2010-2013) | Birch Consultants |
| Talento | EDU | Porcentaje de poblacion adulta con educacion superior | CBS |
| Finanzas | VC | Monto de capital de riesgo (startup y crecimiento) por cada 1,000 establecimientos (promedio 3 anos rezagado) | Asociacion Nacional de Private Equity |
| Conocimiento | RD | Porcentaje del PIB invertido en I+D (organizaciones publicas y privadas) | CBS |
| Servicios intermediarios | BUSSERV | Porcentaje de firmas de servicios empresariales en la poblacion de negocios | CBS |

**Sobre la medicion del output emprendedor:** Los autores usan HGFIRMS (participacion de firmas de alto crecimiento en la poblacion empresarial regional) como proxy de emprendimiento productivo. Una firma de alto crecimiento tiene crecimiento rentable en facturacion de al menos 20% anual durante 3 anos (al menos 72.8% acumulado). En la muestra holandesa de 2013: de 2 millones de firmas registradas, solo 394 califican como alto crecimiento.

**Diferencias regionales:** Incluso en un pais pequeno como Holanda, hay una diferencia de 15x entre la region con menor proporcion de firmas de alto crecimiento (0.003%) y la de mayor (0.045%).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Esta tabla es una referencia directa para validar la seleccion de indicadores ANDE. Notablemente:
- F1 (Inversion total 3 anos) corresponde a VC pero con horizonte temporal similar (promedios de 3 anos)
- H2 (Graduados STEM) es una version mas especifica de EDU
- I1 (Patentes) e I3 (Publicaciones) son proxies de RD
- C1 (Meetups) captura parcialmente INNOCOL (redes de innovacion)
- PROLEAD (liderazgo) no tiene equivalente directo en los 30 indicadores ANDE -- esto es un punto ciego potencial

---

### 9D -- Evidencia empirica: interdependencia de elementos en regiones holandesas
**Seccion:** Section 6.1 (Descriptive Statistics, pp. 1318-1321) y Tables 3-4, Figure 2
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (validacion empirica)
**Tipo:** Datos cuantitativos -- hallazgos centrales

Datos de 12 provincias holandesas en tres olas (2009, 2012, 2015):

**Variacion inter-regional sustancial:** La region con mayor valor absoluto tipicamente duplica a la de menor valor en la mayoria de los elementos. Para firmas de alto crecimiento, la diferencia es de 15x.

**Tres clusters de interdependencia confirmados por correlaciones (datos 2009):**
1. **Educacion + Nuevas firmas + Servicios empresariales** -- fuertemente correlacionados entre si (r > 0.7)
2. **I+D + Lideres de proyecto** -- fuertemente correlacionados (r = 0.83)
3. **Accesibilidad + Demanda** -- fuertemente correlacionados (r = 0.88)

**Capital de riesgo como nexo:** VC muestra correlaciones fuertes con educacion (r = 0.93), servicios empresariales (r = 0.79), y nuevas firmas (r = 0.75). Esto sugiere que VC es atraido a regiones con base de talento y servicios, no viceversa.

**Calidad de gobierno consistentemente negativa:** QUALGOV se correlaciona negativamente con casi todos los otros elementos. Los autores no ofrecen explicacion definitiva.

**Evidencia de causalidad descendente (Proposicion 3):** La prevalencia de firmas de alto crecimiento se correlaciona positivamente con valores subsecuentes de demanda, liderazgo de proyectos, y servicios empresariales. Tambien hay correlaciones fuertes entre tasas de firmas de alto crecimiento en 2009-2012 y 2012-2015 (la concentracion se perpetua).

**Componente principal:** El analisis de componentes principales revela la dominancia de una dimension que cubre todos los elementos excepto instituciones formales (en 2009 y 2012), y todos excepto instituciones formales y redes (en 2015).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Estos datos validan el enfoque de scoring ANDE donde cada dominio recibe ponderacion igual en el promedio global. La dominancia de un componente principal sugiere que la calidad del ecosistema es fundamentalmente unidimensional -- las regiones son buenas o malas en todo. Sin embargo, la excepcion de instituciones formales (calidad de gobierno) sugiere que el dominio P puede comportarse de manera independiente al resto del ecosistema.

---

### 9E -- El Indice de Ecosistema Emprendedor (EEINDEX): construccion y validacion
**Seccion:** Section 6.2 (Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Index, pp. 1321-1324) y Figures 3-4
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (metodologia de scoring)
**Tipo:** Metodologia con resultados empiricos -- REFERENCIA CRITICA

**Construccion del indice:**
1. Normalizar cada elemento dividiendo por el promedio, de modo que el valor promedio = 1
2. Regiones por encima del promedio > 1, por debajo < 1
3. Sumar los 10 elementos normalizados (indice aditivo, media = 10)

**Tres enfoques probados:**

| Enfoque | Formula | Rango (2009) | Interpretacion |
|---|---|---|---|
| Aditivo | E1 + E2 + ... + E10 | 5.99 (Drenthe) a 12.42 (Utrecht) | Variacion moderada, ~2x |
| Multiplicativo | E1 x E2 x ... x E10 | 0.001 (Drenthe) a 6.376 (Utrecht) | Variacion extrema, >6000x |
| Log-multiplicativo | ln(E1) + ... + ln(E10) | -2.52 (Drenthe) a 0.67 (Utrecht) | Simetria alrededor de 0 |

**Hallazgo clave sobre multiplicatividad:** El indice multiplicativo captura mejor la naturaleza interactiva del sistema porque una debilidad en un solo elemento arrastra al indice completo de manera desproporcionada. Esto formaliza matematicamente el concepto de "cuello de botella": un elemento muy bajo "estrangula" al sistema entero. Sin embargo, el indice multiplicativo tiene validez externa insuficiente (variacion excesiva).

**El indice log-multiplicativo revela asimetria:** Las 5 regiones rezagadas se desvian mucho mas negativamente que las 5 regiones lideres se desvian positivamente. Esto sugiere que los ecosistemas debiles son "mas debiles" de lo que los ecosistemas fuertes son "fuertes."

**Validacion contra output emprendedor:**
- R-cuadrado del indice aditivo prediciendo firmas de alto crecimiento: 0.437 (2009), 0.625 (2012), 0.526 (2015)
- Correlaciones siempre significativas y positivas
- El indice explica entre 44% y 62% de la variacion en firmas de alto crecimiento entre regiones

**Robustez:** Los rankings regionales son robustos a: (a) diferentes metodos de indice, (b) eliminacion de un elemento a la vez (indice de 9 elementos), (c) uso de Z-scores en lugar de normalizacion.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** El score global ANDE (promedio de 7 dominios) es un indice aditivo. La evidencia de Stam sugiere que un enfoque multiplicativo capturaria mejor la logica de cuellos de botella. Sin embargo, para comunicar a stakeholders, el aditivo es mas intuitivo. La implicacion practica: cuando un dominio puntua muy bajo (<25), su impacto real en el ecosistema es mucho peor de lo que el promedio aditivo sugiere. El reporte debe enfatizar esto al describir cuellos de botella.

---

### 9F -- La perspectiva sistemica: por que no descomponer en elementos individuales
**Seccion:** Section 7.2 (A Systems Perspective, pp. 1324-1326)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (filosofia del diagnostico)
**Tipo:** Argumento teorico-metodologico central

Los autores argumentan contra el enfoque reduccionista de aislar elementos individuales:

**El problema de la descomposicion funcional limitada:** Debido a su conectividad inherente, no-linealidad y apertura, un sistema complejo tiene descomposicion funcional limitada (Martin y Sunley 2007). El funcionamiento general del ecosistema emprendedor no puede deducirse del conocimiento del funcionamiento de sus elementos individuales.

**Implicacion para regresion:** Descomponer el ecosistema en elementos y luego regresar estos sobre el output NO es adecuado por razones sustantivas (el ecosistema es un sistema, no un conjunto de elementos independientes) y estadisticas (multicolinealidad).

**Enfoque correcto:** Analisis de sistemas -- enfocarse en la emergencia de efectos a nivel del sistema completo y analizar los resultados de desempeno relativo de conjuntos enteros de multiples elementos (Drazin y Van de Ven 1985). En el futuro, analisis booleano comparativo (QCA) podria identificar configuraciones de factores asociados a altos niveles de emprendimiento (Vedula y Fitza 2019 lo aplican con fuzzy-set QCA a regiones de EE.UU.).

**Tres implicaciones del enfoque sistemico:**
1. Academicos y tomadores de decisiones deben ser mas sensibles al contexto macro del emprendimiento; frecuentemente se trata como "exogeno" y se controla estadisticamente, cuando deberia ser el foco especifico de investigacion.
2. El emprendimiento no se limita al sector con fines de lucro; actores publicos y sin fines de lucro juegan roles cruciales en facilitar o restringir elementos del ecosistema.
3. Cada firma emprendedora es un solo actor con roles limitados, dependiente de muchos otros actores para cumplir todas las funciones del ecosistema.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Refuerza que el reporte Fase 1 no debe limitarse a describir indicador por indicador. El analisis estrategico (Secciones 2, 3, 5, 6) debe tratar el ecosistema como sistema: explicar como las debilidades en un dominio afectan a otros, identificar ciclos de retroalimentacion, y fundamentar las recomendaciones en la logica sistemica, no en la logica de "arreglar cada indicador bajo." La Seccion 5 del reporte (analisis por dominio) debe explicar las relaciones entre dominios, no solo el estado de cada uno.

---

### 9G -- El concepto de cuello de botella y desarrollo desigual
**Seccion:** Section 7.3 (Limitations, p. 1326)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (diagnostico)
**Tipo:** Concepto analitico central

Los autores introducen la nocion de cuello de botella en ecosistemas (referenciando a Szerb y Acs, 2011):

**Desarrollo temporal desigual:** Los elementos de un ecosistema se desarrollan y cambian de manera desigual en el tiempo. Como resultado, los desarrollos temporales desiguales de elementos ecosistemicos pueden actuar como cuellos de botella que inhiben el emprendimiento en una region.
