**Paradoja de la facilitacion:** Los mismos elementos ecosistemicos creados para facilitar la emergencia de emprendimiento en un area pueden obstaculizar el desarrollo posterior en otras areas. Este proceso generativo tiene una historia dinamica de destruccion creativa (Schumpeter 1934).

**Autocorrelacion temporal alta:** Las fuertes autocorrelaciones intertemporales encontradas indican que se necesitan periodos de tiempo mas largos para examinar cambios en elementos ecosistemicos. Un diagnostico de un solo punto en el tiempo (como ANDE Fase 1) captura una instantanea pero no la trayectoria.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** El concepto de cuello de botella es central al diagnostico ANDE. La operacionalizacion en CLAUDE.md (dominio con score mas bajo; si diferencia <5 puntos con el segundo, ambos son criticos) es consistente con la logica de Stam. La implicacion adicional del desarrollo desigual es que cuellos de botella pueden rotar: si Financiamiento es el cuello de botella hoy y se aborda, el proximo puede ser Servicios de Apoyo. Las recomendaciones deben anticipar esta rotacion.

---

### 9H -- Datos descriptivos de referencia: regiones holandesas
**Seccion:** Table 3 (pp. 1319-1320)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (benchmarks)
**Tipo:** Datos de referencia

Estadisticas descriptivas de los 10 elementos normalizados (promedio = 1) para 12 provincias holandesas, 2009:

| Elemento | Minimo | Maximo | Media | Desv. Est. |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Calidad de gobierno | 0.797 | 1.275 | 1.000 | 0.176 |
| Nuevas firmas | 0.775 | 1.295 | 0.952 | 0.150 |
| Accesibilidad | 0.547 | 1.373 | 1.000 | 0.284 |
| Demanda | 0.274 | 1.191 | 0.651 | 0.342 |
| Colaboracion para innovacion | 0.886 | 1.086 | 1.000 | 0.060 |
| Lideres de proyecto | 0.190 | 1.434 | 0.780 | 0.436 |
| Capital de riesgo | 0.102 | 2.192 | 0.830 | 0.635 |
| Educacion | 0.747 | 1.314 | 0.944 | 0.162 |
| I+D | 0.220 | 1.320 | 0.851 | 0.340 |
| Servicios empresariales | 0.560 | 1.349 | 0.891 | 0.231 |
| **Indice ecosistemico** | **5.99** | **12.42** | **8.90** | **2.12** |
| **Firmas alto crecimiento** | **0.0003%** | **0.0008%** | **0.0005%** | **0.0002%** |

**Elementos con mayor variacion regional:** Capital de riesgo (coef. variacion = 0.77), Lideres de proyecto (0.56), I+D (0.40), Demanda (0.53). Estos son los elementos que mas diferencian a las regiones.

**Elementos con menor variacion regional:** Colaboracion para innovacion (0.06), Educacion (0.17), Calidad de gobierno (0.18). Estos son relativamente homogeneos incluso dentro de un pais.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** La alta variacion en VC y Liderazgo sugiere que estos son los elementos donde las diferencias entre ecosistemas son mas pronunciadas -- y por tanto donde las intervenciones pueden tener mayor impacto diferenciador. En contraste, la educacion tiende a ser mas homogenea regionalmente (determinada a nivel nacional). Para ecosistemas latinoamericanos, esperar aun mayor variacion en financiamiento y menor en instituciones formales nacionales.

---

### 9I -- Retroalimentacion y causalidad bidireccional: implicaciones para diagnostico
**Seccion:** Section 7.1 (Propositions, pp. 1323-1324)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (interpretacion de resultados)
**Tipo:** Sintesis de hallazgos

Resumen de evidencia para las tres proposiciones:

**Proposicion 1 (Co-evolucion): Evidencia sustancial.** Interdependencia general fuerte y tres clusters especificos. Muy fuertes correlaciones intertemporales positivas sugieren fuerte dependencia de trayectoria. El unico elemento que muestra comportamiento independiente es calidad de gobierno.

**Proposicion 2 (Causalidad ascendente): Evidencia fuerte.** Los diez elementos combinados en un indice se relacionan fuertemente con niveles subsecuentes de actividad emprendedora en un territorio. El ecosistema debe tratarse como sistema completo: su calidad general esta positivamente relacionada con el output emprendedor; no debe descomponerse en diez elementos para explicar la influencia relativa de diferentes elementos.

**Proposicion 3 (Causalidad descendente): Evidencia parcial.** Efectos positivos de retroalimentacion de firmas de alto crecimiento sobre la mayoria de los valores subsecuentes de elementos ecosistemicos, aunque no consistentemente en todos los periodos. Correlaciones fuertes positivas entre la tasa de firmas de alto crecimiento en T0 y la tasa en T3. La falta de evidencia consistente para retroalimentacion temporal puede estar relacionada con el analisis crudo (rezago de 3 anos) de los efectos presumidos.

**Advertencia:** Los tests de las proposiciones no deben verse como tests directos de mecanismos causales, sino como indicaciones de tendencias derivadas de un contexto muy especifico en tiempo y espacio (Holanda, 2009-2015).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** La causalidad bidireccional significa que un diagnostico de punto unico (Fase 1) no puede determinar si un score bajo en Financiamiento causa bajo output emprendedor, o si el bajo output emprendedor ha degradado el financiamiento a lo largo del tiempo. Las recomendaciones deben abordar ambas direcciones: crear condiciones Y estimular actividad simultaneamente. Esto respalda la logica ANDE de recomendar intervenciones tanto en dominios Condicion como en dominios Resultado para ecosistemas en etapa Emergente.



---

## [Ref 10]

# Reference Extracts: WEF Entrepreneurial Ecosystems Report
**Source:** `REF_WEF.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial Ecosystems Around the Globe and Company Growth Dynamics
**Authors:** George Foster, Carlos Shimizu (Stanford University); Maria Pinelli (Ernst & Young); Linda Rottenberg (Endeavor)
**Year:** 2013
---

## Extracto 10A — WEF 8-Pillar Ecosystem Framework

The WEF report defines an entrepreneurial ecosystem through 8 pillars, each with specific subcomponents. This framework complements and maps directly to ANDE s 7-domain model:

| WEF Pillar | Key Components | ANDE Domain Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| **Accessible Markets** | Domestic large/SME/gov customers; Foreign large/SME/gov customers | M: Mercados |
| **Human Capital/Workforce** | Management talent, Technical talent, Entrepreneurial experience, Outsourcing, Immigrant workforce | H: Capital Humano |
| **Funding & Finance** | Friends & family, Angels, PE, VC, Debt access | F: Financiamiento |
| **Mentors/Advisors/Support Systems** | Mentors, Professional services, Incubators/Accelerators, Peer networks | S: Servicios de Apoyo |
| **Regulatory Framework & Infrastructure** | Ease of starting business, Tax incentives, Business-friendly legislation, Basic infra, Telecoms, Transport | P: Politica y Regulacion |
| **Education & Training** | Pre-university workforce, University workforce, Entrepreneur-specific training | H: Capital Humano |
| **Major Universities as Catalysts** | Culture of respect for entrepreneurship, Idea-formation role, Graduate pipeline | H/I: Capital Humano / I+D |
| **Cultural Support** | Risk/failure tolerance, Self-employment preference, Role models, Research culture, Positive image, Innovation celebration | C: Cultura |

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Use as cross-reference when scoring. WEF s 8 pillars decompose into ANDE s 7 domains with significant overlap. The WEF framework is perception-based (entrepreneur surveys), while ANDE uses observable indicators. Both converge on the same structural elements.

---

## Extracto 10B — The Big Three Ecosystem Pillars

From a survey of 1,000+ entrepreneurs across 43 countries, entrepreneurs consistently ranked three pillars as most important to company growth, regardless of region, country, or industry:

1. **Accessible Markets** (60% avg. importance across regions)
2. **Human Capital/Workforce** (62% avg.)
3. **Funding & Finance** (59% avg.)

The remaining five pillars ranked significantly lower:
- Mentors/Advisors/Support Systems: 26%
- Cultural Support: 18%
- Regulatory Framework/Infrastructure: 18%
- Education & Training: 15%
- Major Universities as Catalysts: 8%

**Key finding:** The same Big Three dominate whether analyzing growth accelerators or growth challenges. The top four growth accelerators are: Market Opportunity (49-56%), Top Management (48-50%), Human Resources (42-47%), Funding & Finance (34-42%). The top four growth challenges mirror this: Funding & Finance (52-56%), Human Resources (40-53%), Market Opportunity (32-33%), Government & Regulatory (27-36%).

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This hierarchy informs scoring weight interpretation. When an ecosystem scores low in Finance (F), Markets (M), or Human Capital (H), the impact on entrepreneurs is disproportionately severe compared to low scores in Culture (C) or Support Services (S). Use to contextualize bottleneck analysis in Section 5 of the report.

---

## Extracto 10C — Ecosystem Pillar Availability by Region (Heat Map Data)

The report provides granular data on pillar availability as perceived by entrepreneurs in different regions. Key benchmarks relevant for Latin American ecosystems:

**Mexico-specific data (readily available pillars):**
- Accessible Markets: 52%
- Human Capital: 65%
- Funding & Finance: 52%
- Mentors/Support: 48%
- Regulatory Framework: 52%
- Education & Training: 30%
- Universities as Catalysts: 35%
- Cultural Support: 22%
- **Average: 45%**

**For comparison, Silicon Valley averages 86%, South/Central America and Mexico averages 45%.**

**Pillar importance for company growth (Mexico-specific):**
- Accessible Markets: 43%
- Human Capital: 61%
- Funding & Finance: 78% (highest across all countries surveyed)
- Regulatory Framework: 39%
- Cultural Support: 13%

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Mexico shows an acute funding gap (78% say it matters vs. 52% say it is available) and extreme weakness in cultural support and education/training. Use these benchmarks when contextualizing a Mexican ecosystem s scores against WEF s global baseline. The 45% average for LatAm corresponds roughly to the Emergente tier in ANDE scoring (26-45).

---

## Extracto 10D — Breakout Companies and Skewed Distribution

Across 10 countries and 360,000+ early-stage companies surveyed:

- **Top 1%** of early-stage companies contribute **44% of total sector revenues** at Year 5
- **Top 5%** contribute **72%** of total revenues
- **Top 10%** contribute **84%** of total revenues
- Job creation follows a similar skewed pattern

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This concentration effect is critical for ecosystem diagnostics. A healthy ecosystem does not require thousands of startups; it requires conditions that enable the few high-growth ventures to scale. When scoring M1 (Startups Activas), raw count matters less than whether the ecosystem can produce and retain outlier companies. Use this data point in Section 5 (strategic analysis) and Section 7 (recommendations) to argue for quality-over-quantity approaches.

---

## Extracto 10E — Large Companies as Ecosystem Leverage

The report identifies 7 productive relationship areas between large companies and startups:

1. **Customer engagement** -- early adopter customers (69% of surveyed startups have large domestic company customers, 43% have foreign)
2. **Credibility enhancement** -- lighthouse/reference customers
3. **Strategic investors** -- corporate venture investment
4. **Mentorship and advice** -- industry insight
5. **Go-to-market partners** -- distribution, logistics access
6. **Operating capability** -- manufacturing, technology, know-how
7. **Licensing leverage** -- licensing startup technology to enter markets

And 7 challenge areas:
1. Attracting attention from large companies
2. Productive working together (coordination, commitment)
3. Value appropriation conflicts
4. Maintaining the relationship
5. Talent competition (raiding talent)
6. Loss of independence
7. IP/competitive threats

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Maps directly to M2 (Corporativos tech) and M3 (Sectores especializacion). An ecosystem with large corporate presence that is NOT engaging with startups represents a missed opportunity -- score M2 based not just on corporate count but on evidence of startup-corporate interaction. Use in Section 7 recommendations for ecosystems with corporate presence but low Finance or Markets scores.

---

## Extracto 10F — Ecosystem Growth Lessons: Buenos Aires and Istanbul

From Endeavor s longitudinal tracking of ecosystem evolution:

1. **Ecosystems are not created overnight.** Buenos Aires tech ecosystem took first steps in mid-1990s; a rich network emerged only 15 years later. Silicon Valley evolved over decades.

2. **A few people play a central role.** Networks require a few nodes that activate and connect. In Buenos Aires, entrepreneurs from 3 companies account for a significant share of network activity.

3. **Infrastructure initiatives (incubators, accelerators, funds, service providers) are complementary** -- they will not create a network alone, but provide mechanisms for high-impact entrepreneurs to activate the ecosystem.

4. **Governments should empower private actors rather than public ones.** Government-led ecosystem building tends to be less effective than enabling private ecosystem leaders.

5. **Five roles of high-impact entrepreneurs:** Inspiration, Founder crucible (employees who later found companies), Employee crucible (employees who join other startups), Investment source (reinvesting wealth), Mentor role.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Use these lessons when writing Section 6 (Retos) and Section 7 (Recomendaciones). For Naciente/Emergente ecosystems, the 15-year timeline is a realistic frame. The few key people insight justifies recommendations around identifying and supporting ecosystem champions. The five roles framework helps structure mentoring/network recommendations (S4, C1-C4).

---

## Extracto 10G — Government and Regulatory: Accelerator vs. Inhibitor

From the survey, the most cited words regarding government as a **growth accelerator** were: tax (11%), companies (7.2%), regulation (6.8%), ease (6.2%), business (4.6%), incentives (3.3%), support (3.1%).

The most cited words regarding government as a **growth inhibitor** were: tax (15.9%), regulation (11.6%), policy (4.1%), complexity (2.7%), requirements (2.2%).

Specific inhibitor categories identified:
- **Big company capture:** Government procurement biased toward large firms
- **Bureaucracy/complexity:** Unclear tax codes, confusing regulations
- **Inconsistent enforcement:** Non-level playing field
- **Regulatory burden/overkill:** Compliance costs disproportionate for small firms
- **Time delays:** Slow licensing, multi-level bureaucracy
- **Regulatory uncertainty:** Changing rules, unpredictable incentives
- **Immigration barriers:** Difficulty attracting foreign talent through visa systems

Human capital-specific regulatory findings:
- **Accelerators:** Easy visa regimes, non-compete limits (California model), government training reimbursement, access to global talent pool
- **Inhibitors:** Stock option taxation as income at grant time, rigid employment laws, immigration restrictions, social insurance complexity

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Maps directly to P1 (Dias constituir empresa), P2 (Regimen fiscal), P3 (Programas publicos). When scoring Policy domain, look beyond days-to-register to the full spectrum: tax treatment, regulatory clarity, government procurement accessibility for startups, and labor flexibility.

---

## Extracto 10H — Alignment Problem: Entrepreneur vs. Government Time Horizons

A key structural finding: there is a **potential alignment issue** between entrepreneurs and governments. Governments adopt strong country/regional focus in policies, while entrepreneurs think globally about accessible markets. Politicians follow electoral cycles (4-5 year horizons), while entrepreneurs pursue sustained scaling that requires longer-term consistency.

The report notes that public sector investments in ecosystems made without systematic input from entrepreneurs risk misinformed decisions with outcomes far from the desired surge in entrepreneurial activity.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Use in Section 6 (Retos) to contextualize when government programs (P3) exist but entrepreneurs report them as ineffective. The misalignment between government program design and entrepreneur needs is a common pattern in Emergente/En Desarrollo ecosystems. Recommend in Section 7 that public programs include entrepreneur advisory boards or feedback mechanisms.



---

## [Ref 11]

# Reference Extracts: Bolay - Intermediate Cities in Latin America
**Source:** `REF_bolay.pdf`
**Title:** Intermediate cities in Latin America: risk and opportunities of coherent urban development
**Authors:** J.-C. Bolay, A. Rabinovich
**Year:** 2004
---

## Relevance Assessment

This paper focuses on **urban planning, governance, and spatial development** in medium-sized Latin American cities (case studies: Maranguape/Brazil, Camilo Aldao/Argentina, Manta and Cuenca/Ecuador). Its primary contribution is a conceptual framework for understanding how intermediate cities (50K-500K population) function as interfaces between local/rural contexts and global markets.

**Direct relevance to ANDE entrepreneurial ecosystem diagnostics: MINIMAL.** The paper does not address entrepreneurship, startups, innovation ecosystems, venture capital, accelerators, or any of the 7 ANDE domains directly. It is an urban sociology/planning paper from 2004.

However, two narrow aspects provide contextual background useful for ecosystem diagnostics in smaller Latin American cities:

---

## Extracto 11A — Definition and Dynamics of Intermediate Cities in Latin America

The paper proposes moving beyond static population-based city classifications toward a functional, multi-dimensional definition. An intermediate city is defined not by population alone but by its role as an interface between local/rural environments and national/global networks.

Key demographic context: In Latin America, 95% of municipalities have populations under 100,000. Approximately 48% of the urban population lives in cities under 500,000 inhabitants. These cities are growing proportionally faster than megacities in many countries.

The paper identifies 11 functional types of intermediate cities, including: regional market center, service center, regional capital, economic hub for specific industries, tourist destination, border city, and satellite/dormitory city near a metropolis.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** When diagnosing ecosystems in cities under 500K population, the intermediate city context matters. These cities operate as regional service hubs with strong rural-urban linkages. Their entrepreneurial ecosystems are shaped by their intermediation function -- they connect local production (agriculture, manufacturing) to broader markets. This framing helps contextualize why M3 (Sectores especializacion) in smaller cities tends to reflect regional economic vocations (agribusiness, tourism, logistics) rather than pure tech plays. Useful as background context for the population adjustment methodology (cities under 1M inhabitants).

---

## Extracto 11B — Governance Challenges for Intermediate Cities Seeking Global Integration

The paper documents a recurring pattern: intermediate city leaders focus their development strategies on international positioning (tourism, exports, attracting foreign investment) while neglecting domestic market integration and internal governance capacity.

Four observed biases in intermediate city planning:
1. Focus on local affairs to the detriment of understanding the city s role in broader networks
2. Preference for future projections over analysis of current constraints
3. Positivist attitude favoring new initiatives over addressing structural problems (migration pressure, environmental degradation)
4. Primacy of global dynamics, prioritizing projects dependent on external resources

The study found that decentralization has weakened the link between national policy and local strategy, creating initiatives that are creative but also fragmented and fragile in a competitive global context.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This pattern is relevant when diagnosing ecosystems where P3 (Programas publicos) reflects a disconnect between ambitious international-facing programs and the actual local entrepreneurial base. In intermediate cities, ecosystem builders may overemphasize global positioning (tech hubs, innovation districts) while the local economy and talent base remain rooted in traditional sectors. The governance fragility described here also contextualizes why institutional support (P domain) can appear strong on paper but weak in execution.

---

**Note:** No additional extractos are warranted. The remaining content covers detailed case studies of urban planning in the four cities, demographic migration patterns, environmental degradation, and governance theory -- none of which provide actionable frameworks, benchmarks, or data for ANDE ecosystem scoring or recommendations.



---

## [Ref 12]

# Reference Extracts: Audretsch & Belitski - Entrepreneurial Ecosystems in Cities
**Source:** `REF_david.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial ecosystems in cities: establishing the framework conditions
**Authors:** David B. Audretsch (Indiana University), Maksim Belitski (University of Reading / Henley Business School)
**Year:** 2016
---

## Extracto 12A — Six-Domain Framework for Urban Entrepreneurial Ecosystems

Audretsch and Belitski propose a six-domain framework for entrepreneurial ecosystem framework conditions, empirically tested across 70 European cities using Eurostat perception surveys and structural equation modeling (SEM). The six domains are:

1. **Culture and Norms** -- Safety perceptions (neighborhood and city level), interpersonal trust
2. **Physical Infrastructure and Amenities** -- Transport satisfaction, green spaces, cleanliness, cultural facilities (theatres, museums, coffee shops)
3. **Formal Institutions** -- Perception that public resources are spent responsibly, administrative services help efficiently
4. **Internet Access and Connectivity** -- Satisfaction with internet access as gateway to digital technologies
5. **Melting Pot Index** -- Perception that foreigners are well integrated (diversity and tolerance)
6. **Demand and Workforce** -- Housing demand, ease of finding good jobs

Of these six, **only four proved statistically significant** for predicting startup rates: Culture, Infrastructure/Amenities, Formal Institutions, and Internet Access. The Melting Pot and Demand domains were dropped from the final model due to weak fit.

**Mapping to ANDE domains:**

| Audretsch-Belitski Domain | Statistical Significance | ANDE Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| Culture and Norms | Supported (H1) | C: Cultura (C5 Confianza social) |
| Infrastructure & Amenities | Strongest significance (H2) | S: Servicios de Apoyo (S5 Internet) + contextual |
| Formal Institutions | Supported at 1% (H3) | P: Politica y Regulacion |
| Internet Access | Supported (H4) | H: Capital Humano (H5 Acceso internet) |
| Melting Pot | Not supported (H5) | -- |
| Demand/Workforce | Ambiguous (H6) | M: Mercados (partial) |

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This framework validates ANDE s domain structure through independent empirical analysis. The finding that Culture, Institutions, Infrastructure, and ICT are the four significant predictors of startup formation reinforces the ANDE approach of measuring these as separate domains. Particularly relevant for the Policy domain (P): the study operationalizes institutional quality as a perception-based measure of government efficiency and responsible resource allocation, not just regulatory burden metrics.

---

## Extracto 12B — Culture, Trust, and Safety as Ecosystem Foundations

The study provides empirical evidence that local culture facilitating trust and safety improves the entrepreneurial ecosystem. This was measured through three perception variables:

- **Neighborhood safety** (Mean: 89.8/100, Range: 59.8-99.6 across 70 European cities)
- **City-level safety** (Mean: 84.2/100, Range: 42.3-99.2)
- **Interpersonal trust** (Mean: 64.9/100, Range: 22.3-93.5)

Key theoretical grounding: Norms and trust that reward entrepreneurial action provide additional resources and enhance cooperation between actors. Entrepreneurs may choose not to start a business in unsafe areas where customers do not trust suppliers and vice versa. West European communities are perceived as having safer, more trusted neighborhoods, while Eastern European societies with higher bureaucracy and corruption are perceived as lacking trust.

The study found that the error terms for Trust and Formal Institutions are positively correlated, suggesting an unobserved factor (likely corruption levels) that affects both simultaneously.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Directly relevant to scoring C5 (Confianza social). The trust-institutions correlation validates ANDE s inclusion of social trust as a culture indicator alongside institutional measures. In Latin American ecosystems where corruption perception is high, expect both C5 and P-domain scores to be depressed simultaneously. The trust measurement benchmarks (European city range: 22-94 on a 0-100 scale) can be used as reference points when calibrating C5 scores.

---

## Extracto 12C — Formal Institutions: Beyond Regulation to Government Efficiency

The formal institutions domain is operationalized through two perception variables:

- **Resources spent responsibly** (Mean: 49.3/100, Range: 11.5-82.5) -- the widest variance of any variable in the study
- **Administrative services help efficiently** (Mean: 80.0/100, Range: 52.7-92.0)

Theoretical basis: Entrepreneurs are discouraged from starting businesses when constrained by bureaucracy, numerous local regulations, time requirements, and large government size. Efficient administrative services, prioritization in resource allocation, and government support in providing jobs and finance create a conducive ecosystem.

The hypothesis that responsibly spent resources and efficient administration improve the ecosystem was **supported at 1% significance level**.

Key policy insight: Efficient government increases demand and supply for private and public services. The positive outcomes of efficient administrative services create stability and make places more attractive to live, work, and invest. However, **excessive** administrative support is not supportive to entrepreneurship -- the relationship is about efficiency, not size.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This reframes the Policy domain (P1-P3) assessment. Beyond measuring days to register a business (P1) or counting active programs (P3), the perception of institutional efficiency matters. A city with fast business registration but widespread perception that public resources are wasted or captured will still underperform. The 49.3 mean for responsible resource spending (with a range down to 11.5) suggests this is a major bottleneck even in European cities. In Latin American contexts, this variable likely scores even lower and represents a structural constraint on ecosystem development. Use this framework to add depth to P-domain analysis in Section 3.

---

## Extracto 12D — Physical Infrastructure as the Strongest Predictor

The study found physical infrastructure and amenities to have the **strongest statistical significance** of all six domains in predicting startup rates. This domain was measured through:

- **Transport satisfaction** (Mean: 71.8/100)
- **Green space satisfaction** (Mean: 72.6/100)
- **City cleanliness** (Mean: 52.9/100)
- **Cultural facilities satisfaction** (Mean: 86.1/100)

Theoretical basis: Cities that provide amenities and infrastructure attractive to high human capital residents will thrive (Glaeser et al. 2001). Physical conditions either foster or constrain interaction between ecosystem agents. Infrastructure enhances connectivity and linkages that facilitate the recognition of opportunities. Cultural amenities (coffee shops, bars, theatres, museums, green areas, libraries, parks, clubs) add to a city s attractiveness and ability to attract creative class and highly skilled human capital.

The concept of "third spaces" in the ecosystem is introduced: physical places where proactive people, authorities, researchers, education institutions, non-profits, and societal organizations come together informally.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** While ANDE measures physical infrastructure primarily through S5 (Internet speed) and H5 (Internet access %), this finding suggests that broader quality-of-life infrastructure matters significantly for ecosystem development. When an ecosystem has adequate digital infrastructure but lacks physical spaces for interaction (coworkings, event venues, quality public spaces), the infrastructure gap may be invisible in ANDE scoring but real in its effect. Consider noting in Section 5 analysis when a city has good connectivity metrics but poor physical infrastructure for entrepreneurial interaction.

---

## Extracto 12E — Internet and ICT as Ecosystem Enabler

Internet access satisfaction (Mean: 59.5/100, Range: 27.8-88.7) was found to be a statistically significant predictor of entrepreneurial ecosystem health, though with the weakest effect among the four retained domains.

Theoretical basis: The digital age puts firm capability to manage information at the heart of survival. Key technologies available via internet -- cloud computing, social media, IoT, mobile computing, big data analytics -- enable better understanding, processing, adoption, and application of new technologies. Access to information via internet enables a broader spectrum of business activities and opportunities.

The study emphasizes that beyond connectivity itself, the innovative use of internet and orchestration of existing technologies is what drives startup competitiveness.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** Validates the inclusion of S5 (Velocidad internet) and H5 (Acceso internet %) as ANDE indicators. The finding that ICT is significant but with weaker effect than culture, institutions, or physical infrastructure suggests that internet access is a necessary but insufficient condition. An ecosystem can score well on S5/H5 but still underperform if the other framework conditions are weak. Use this nuance in Section 5 when an ecosystem has decent connectivity but poor overall scores.

---

## Extracto 12F — Bottleneck Method and REDI Integration

A central methodological contribution is the joint testing of local framework conditions alongside the Regional Entrepreneurship and Development Index (REDI). Key findings:

- Both REDI and framework conditions are positively associated with startup rates
- The framework conditions latent variable has **higher predictive impact** than REDI alone
- This is because framework conditions are compound, multi-domain indicators measured at city level, while REDI operates at regional level

The bottleneck leveraging method (from Szerb et al. 2013, Levie and Autio 2014) is referenced: to pursue opportunities, an entrepreneur needs access to ALL framework conditions with a minimum number of bottlenecks. This means the weakest domain constrains the entire system.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** This directly validates ANDE s bottleneck methodology (identifying the lowest-scoring domain as the primary constraint). The finding that local (city-level) framework conditions predict startup rates better than regional indices supports ANDE s city-level diagnostic approach. The bottleneck concept reinforces the ANDE principle that bringing up the lowest domain has more impact than further strengthening an already-strong domain.

---

## Extracto 12G — Five Policy Recommendations from the Study

The authors propose five policy actions based on their findings:

1. **Enable greater labor mobility and reform formal institutions** to improve reliance on administrative support, including financing entrepreneurship and resource allocation to help startups. Efficient administration enhances startups and promotes growth, but excessive support is counterproductive.

2. **Combine public and entrepreneurial leaders** in private-public partnerships. The role of entrepreneurial leaders and e-leaders who bridge various ecosystem domains through IT alignment should be elevated.

3. **Improve cultural facilities** (coffee shops, bars, theatres, museums, green areas, libraries, parks) to add to city attractiveness. Cultural amenities are city-specific, cannot be moved but can be created, and attract creative class and skilled human capital.

4. **Grow entrepreneurship culture** through embeddedness programs (community identity campaigns, educational programs targeting communities) as a core determinant of individual choice to start a business locally.

5. **Stimulate entrepreneurial awareness and access to growth finance** to select individuals with higher human capital into entrepreneurship. Particular focus on high-growth businesses, tailoring demand-side public interventions to identify better criteria for reaching second-stage growth projects quickly.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** These five recommendations map cleanly to ANDE intervention priorities:
- Recommendation 1 maps to P domain (institutional reform)
- Recommendation 2 maps to S domain (support services, mentors)
- Recommendation 3 maps to C domain (cultural infrastructure) and S domain (coworkings, event spaces)
- Recommendation 4 maps to C domain (culture building)
- Recommendation 5 maps to F domain (financing) and H domain (human capital selection)

Use these as evidence-backed intervention templates in Section 7 of the ANDE report, particularly for ecosystems at Emergente or En Desarrollo maturity levels where institution-building and culture development are priority actions per Table 4.3.

---

## Extracto 12H — Empirical Benchmarks from 70 European Cities

The study provides descriptive statistics that serve as benchmarks for ecosystem framework conditions measured on a 0-100 perception scale across 70 European cities:

| Variable | Mean | SD | Min | Max |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Neighborhood safety | 89.8 | 7.2 | 59.8 | 99.6 |
| City safety | 84.2 | 10.8 | 42.3 | 99.2 |
| Trust | 64.9 | 17.8 | 22.3 | 93.5 |
| Transport | 71.8 | 17.8 | 2.3 | 96.0 |
| Green space | 72.6 | 17.1 | 23.0 | 95.6 |
| City cleanliness | 52.9 | 22.5 | 8.2 | 96.5 |
| Cultural facilities | 86.1 | 11.4 | 45.9 | 99.4 |
| Internet | 59.5 | 12.2 | 27.8 | 88.7 |
| Resources spent responsibly | 49.3 | 16.8 | 11.5 | 82.5 |
| Administrative efficiency | 80.0 | 7.9 | 52.7 | 92.0 |
| Foreigner integration | 57.8 | 16.8 | 10.0 | 91.6 |
| Housing demand | 70.3 | 19.2 | 21.7 | 97.3 |
| Easy to find good job | 35.6 | 17.8 | 2.6 | 74.8 |
| Startup rate (% new firms) | 14.91 | 12.73 | 0.4 | 56.9 |
| REDI score | 47.5 | 15.6 | 18.4 | 82.2 |

**Aplicacion ANDE:** While these are European benchmarks and not directly applicable to Latin American scoring, the relative positions and variances are informative. The lowest-scoring variables are: job availability (35.6), resources spent responsibly (49.3), and city cleanliness (52.9) -- suggesting these are universal weak points even in developed economies. The REDI mean of 47.5 across European cities provides a reference for what Emergente-to-En Desarrollo looks like in a European context.



---

## [Ref 13]

# Extractos: REF_giz_2018.pdf

- **Source:** `drive/REF_giz_2018.pdf`
- **Title:** Guide for Mapping the Entrepreneurial Ecosystem: Observe -- Analyse -- Visualise
- **Authors:** Annabell Kreuzer, Katharina Mengede, Alexandra Oppermann, Mariella Regh (GIZ Sector Programme Sustainable Economic Policy and Private Sector Development)
- **Year:** 2018
- **Publisher:** Deutsche Gesellschaft fur Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH, on behalf of German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ)
- **Pages:** 73
- **Relevance ANDE:** High. Practical diagnostic toolkit for ecosystem mapping in developing economies. Directly references ANDE toolkit. Compatible 6-domain framework (Policy, Finance, Markets, Human Capital, Support, Culture) maps onto ANDE's 7 domains.

---

## Extracto 13A -- Three-Element Ecosystem Framework

GIZ defines the entrepreneurial ecosystem as composed of three interconnected elements:

1. **Business environment and investment climate** -- legal, administrative, regulatory framework (maps to ANDE domains: Policy, partially Finance)
2. **Interacting actors** -- individuals, organizations, institutions clustered by Isenberg's 6 domains (maps to: Support Services, Finance, Human Capital, Markets)
3. **Entrepreneurial culture and attitude** -- behavioral patterns, social norms, perceptions of entrepreneurship (maps to: Culture)

Key insight: Neither biological nor entrepreneurial ecosystems can be created, designed, or built by an outside actor. Interventions should focus on improving conditions, not engineering outcomes.

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 2 and 5. Reinforces that ecosystem diagnosis must address all three elements simultaneously; addressing one in isolation produces perverse outcomes.

---

## Extracto 13B -- Five-Step Mapping Methodology

GIZ prescribes a 4-week mapping process:

| Step | Duration | Activity |
|------|----------|----------|
| 1. Research Design | 1 day | Define scope, target group, research question, methods, comparative values, expected results |
| 2. Data Collection | 2 weeks | Desk research (benchmark reports) + primary data (interviews, focus groups) |
| 3. Data Analysis | 5 days | Scorecard analysis, actor visualization, hypothesis formulation |
| 4. Data Validation | 2 days | Written review or stakeholder workshop to verify/falsify hypotheses |
| 5. From Mapping to Activity | 1 week+ | Stakeholder dialogue, intervention design, strategy development, monitoring |

Key methodological principle: Start with secondary data (benchmark reports), identify gaps, then use primary data (interviews, focus groups) to fill those gaps. Culture and attitude data relies most heavily on primary sources since benchmark reports rarely cover it.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (methodology context). Validates the structured approach of ANDE Phase 1; provides timeline benchmarks for ecosystem assessments.

---

## Extracto 13C -- Actor Categories by Ecosystem Domain

GIZ provides a comprehensive actor classification aligned with Isenberg's domains:

| Domain | Actor Types |
|--------|------------|
| **Finance** | Banks, microfinance institutions, fintech/crowdfunding platforms, cooperative financial institutions, DFIs, venture capital funds, equity investors, public capital market, angel investors, corporations, foundations |
| **Support** | Incubators, accelerators, industry associations/networks, business service providers (legal, accounting), mentors, business plan contests/conferences, co-working spaces |
| **Policy** | National/regional/state government, local government, regulators, government agencies, central bank, PPD mechanisms |
| **Markets** | Domestic/international corporations, marketing platforms (e-commerce, online), consumers |
| **Human Capital** | Universities, market-driven research institutes, technical training institutes, schools |
| **Media** | TV, social media, blogs, podcasts, role models |

Additional categories for **green/inclusive entrepreneurship** and **women entrepreneurship** include: impact investors, GIB-specific PPD mechanisms, standards/certification systems, sustainable product marketplaces, women entrepreneur organizations, microfunds for women, female role models.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 4 (actor mapping). Cross-reference with ANDE indicator list to ensure all actor categories are represented in the 30-indicator framework.

---

## Extracto 13D -- Benchmark Reports Scorecard

GIZ recommends tracking ecosystem evolution through a multi-year scorecard of international rankings:

| Report | Focus | Level | Frequency |
|--------|-------|-------|-----------|
| Doing Business Report (World Bank) | Business registration, taxes, credit, insolvency, contracts | National/sub-national | Annual |
| WEF Competitiveness Report | 12 pillars of competitiveness | National | Annual |
| Global Entrepreneurship Monitor (GEM) | Entrepreneurial attitudes, activity, aspirations | National | Annual |
| Global Innovation Index (WIPO) | Innovation inputs/outputs, 7 pillars | National | Annual |
| GEDI Index | 15 pillars: environment, ecosystem, aspirations | National | Annual |
| Enterprise Surveys (World Bank) | Business environment perceptions, firm-level | National | Irregular |
| VC/PE Country Attractiveness (IESE) | 6 drivers including capital market depth | National | Annual |
| BTI (Bertelsmann) | Democracy, market economy, political management | National | Annual |

Critical note: GEM is the only benchmark that covers culture/attitude with primary data. For countries not in GEM, primary data collection (interviews) is necessary.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 3 (data sources), Section 8 (indicator sourcing).

---

## Extracto 13E -- Indicators Table for Ecosystem Assessment

GIZ provides a comprehensive indicator framework organized by business environment and investment climate:

**Business environment indicators:**
- Business registration: Ease of starting business index (DB), procedures/days/cost (DB), firms choosing licensing as obstacle (ES), informality rates (ES)
- Tax policies: Ease of paying taxes (DB), firms choosing tax rates as obstacle (ES)
- Access to finance: Financial market development (WEF), VC/PE index (IESE), credit to private sector % GDP (WB), microfinance loans % GDP (GII), firms identifying finance as constraint (ES)
- Labour: Labour market efficiency (WEF), firms choosing labour regulations as obstacle (ES)
- Regulatory governance: Government effectiveness percentile (WB WGI), regulatory quality percentile (WB WGI)
- PPD: Existence of PPD mechanism (primary data only)

**Investment climate indicators:**
- GDP growth, local competition intensity, political stability, corruption constraints, unemployment, efficient use of talent

**Culture and attitude indicators:**
- Entrepreneurship as good career choice (GEM), media stories about entrepreneurs (GEM), high status of entrepreneurs (GEM)

**Use in ANDE report:** Direct cross-reference for sourcing the 30 ANDE indicators. Identifies which indicators require primary data collection versus available from benchmarks.

---

## Extracto 13F -- Visualization Methods for Actor Analysis

GIZ offers five visualization approaches, each with distinct diagnostic value:

1. **Actor category map** (Tool T8) -- Bubble diagram grouping actors by Isenberg domain. Quick overview of density per category. No qualitative element.
2. **Capacity WORKS stakeholder map** (Tool T9) -- Concentric circles with target group at center, primary actors in inner ring, secondary in outer. Shows cooperation intensity between actors.
3. **Abstract ecosystem visualization** (Tool T10) -- Bubble sizes indicate number of actors per category, color indicates capacity development needs (red to green), connecting lines show cooperation intensity. Includes text boxes for strengths/weaknesses per domain.
4. **Regional distribution map** (Tool T11) -- Geographic visualization showing number of service providers per region/city. Identifies spatial gaps in ecosystem support.
5. **Business phase mapping** (Tool T12) -- Matrix: actor types (rows) vs. business phases (columns: idea, startup, early, later, growth, mature). Identifies which phases lack support, revealing "valley of death" gaps.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 4, Section 7. The business-phase mapping is especially useful for identifying where the ecosystem fails to support transitions (e.g., from early stage to growth), which is a common bottleneck in developing economy ecosystems.

---

## Extracto 13G -- From Mapping to Intervention: Challenge-Goal-Intervention Framework

GIZ provides a structured pathway from diagnostic findings to actionable interventions:

**Business Environment examples:**

| Challenge | Goal | Possible Interventions |
|-----------|------|----------------------|
| Tax administration is severe obstacle for young firms | Reduce admin costs for startups/SMEs | Introduce SBA principles; electronic tax services |
| No insolvency law/procedures | Enhance bankruptcy procedures | Collect international good practices; assist in resolution plans |
| No legal form for social enterprises | Introduce legal form | Policy advice to Ministry; stakeholder roundtable |
| High informality in women-led businesses | Increase formalization rate | Information campaign; support scheme as pull-factor |

**Actors examples:**

| Challenge | Goal | Possible Interventions |
|-----------|------|----------------------|
| No finance for growth-phase entrepreneurs | Improve access to growth-stage finance | Detailed gap assessment; advisory on international financing |
| No BDS in regions | Improve regional access to advisory | Remote services; support orgs to open regional offices |
| No advocacy for green/inclusive entrepreneurs | Ensure barriers communicated | Capacity development for advocacy org; GIB Roundtable |

**Culture examples:**

| Challenge | Goal | Possible Interventions |
|-----------|------|----------------------|
| Fear of failure prevents business creation | Introduce "second chance" mentality | Events sharing failure experiences; information campaign |
| GIB concept not well-known | Increase awareness of GIB models | Ideas competition; media articles on GIB |
| Entrepreneurship not seen as career for women | Raise attractiveness for women | Training for female university students; identify role models |

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 (recommendations). This challenge-goal-intervention framework provides a template for structuring actionable recommendations.

---

## Extracto 13H -- Hypothesis-Driven Analysis: Archetypes for Developing Economies

GIZ analysis produces hypotheses validated with stakeholders. Example archetypes:

**Business environment hypotheses:**
- No national entrepreneurship strategy in place or not known to ecosystem players
- Business registration requires physical space, creating a barrier
- No regulatory framework for crowdfunding
- No formal PPD mechanism; entrepreneurs lack channel to public sector

**Actor hypotheses:**
- Confusing range of non-financial services; unused synergies and room for collaboration
- Ecosystem evolves around the capital city; services need extending to rural areas
- Oversupply of early-stage support but undersupply for later stages; need "graduation programmes"
- Lack of skilled asset managers prevents increase in equity finance
- Business angels present but increasingly risk-averse

**Culture hypotheses:**
- Public sector jobs considered safer/more prestigious, but entrepreneurship as career ranks relatively high
- High status of entrepreneurs has decreased in recent years
- Fear of failure prevents many from starting businesses
- Role of women as entrepreneurs remains unclear (very low female TEA)

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 3 and 5. These hypotheses serve as archetypes for developing economy ecosystems. When analyzing a specific city/region, compare findings against these common patterns.

---

## Extracto 13I -- Performance Indicators for Ecosystem Monitoring

Beyond diagnostic indicators, GIZ specifies outcome/performance indicators for tracking ecosystem health over time:

| Category | Indicator |
|----------|-----------|
| **Business demographics** | Enterprise birth rate trend; death rate trend; 5-year survival |
| **Business development** | Employment share of enterprise births; high-growth enterprise shares by sector |
| **Innovation** | Turnover from e-commerce; sales of new-to-market innovations |
| **Competitiveness** | Foreign direct investment; exports |

These performance indicators complement ANDE diagnostic framework (which measures ecosystem determinants) with outcome tracking.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 (recommendations -- suggest monitoring framework), Section 6 (contextualizing ecosystem performance).

---

## Extracto 13J -- Interview Protocol for Ecosystem Assessment

GIZ provides structured interview questions across three elements:

**Business Environment questions (14 items):** Regulatory environment assessment, business registration challenges, bankruptcy framework, tax framework impact, finance types available, labour law influence, commercial court access, land administration, PPD channels, market information access, economic factors, entrepreneurship strategy existence, political effects, labour market effects.

**Actor questions (8 items):** Organization role in ecosystem, gateway person/org, most relevant actors, missing players/services (equity, incubation, specialized services, advocacy, education, infrastructure, corporate support, media), stakeholder coordination, cooperation intensity, barriers to interaction, international connectedness.

**Culture questions (8 items):** Society perception of entrepreneurship, honoring success, tolerance for creativity/experimentation, reaction to failure, fear of failure as barrier, entrepreneurship as career choice, ethnic/social/religious restrictions, reliance on social networks.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (methodology justification). These questions validate the quantitative indicator-based approach of ANDE Phase 1 with qualitative diagnostic probes.



---

## [Ref 14]

# Extractos: REF_harvard.pdf

- **Source:** `drive/REF_harvard.pdf`
- **Title:** How to Start an Entrepreneurial Revolution
- **Author:** Daniel J. Isenberg
- **Year:** 2010
- **Publisher:** Harvard Business Review (June 2010, Reprint R1006A)
- **Pages:** 12
- **Relevance ANDE:** Foundational. Isenberg's framework (6 domains: Policy, Finance, Culture, Support, Human Capital, Markets) is the basis for the ANDE Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Diagnostic Toolkit. This article provides the strategic principles and case studies that underpin the methodology.

---

## Extracto 14A -- The Isenberg Ecosystem Framework (6 Domains)

Isenberg defines the entrepreneurship ecosystem as individual elements that combine in complex ways. The framework identifies six domains, each with specific sub-components:

| Domain | Sub-components |
|--------|---------------|
| **Policy** | Leadership (unequivocal support, social legitimacy, advocacy, strategy, urgency); Government (institutions for investment/support, R&D financial support, regulatory incentives/tax benefits, research institutes, venture-friendly legislation, contract enforcement, property rights, labor law) |
| **Finance** | Micro-loans, angel investors (friends/family), zero-stage venture capital, venture capital funds, private equity, public capital markets, debt |
| **Culture** | Societal norms (visible successes, wealth for founders, international reputation, tolerance of risk/mistakes/failure, innovation/creativity/experimentation, social status of entrepreneur, wealth creation, ambition/drive/hunger) |
| **Support** | Non-governmental institutions (entrepreneurship promotion nonprofits, business plan contests, conferences, entrepreneur-friendly associations); Support professions (legal, accounting, investment bankers, technical experts/advisors); Infrastructure (telecom, transportation/logistics, energy, zones/incubators/co-working/clusters) |
| **Human Capital** | Labour (skilled and unskilled, serial entrepreneurs, later-generation family); Education (general degrees professional/academic, specific entrepreneurship trainings) |
| **Markets** | Early customers (early adopters for proof of concept, expertise in productizing, reference customer, first reviews, distribution channels); Networks (entrepreneur networks, diaspora networks, multinational corporations) |

Key principle: In isolation, each element is conducive to entrepreneurship but insufficient to sustain it. Together, these elements turbocharge venture creation and growth. Most governmental efforts go wrong because they address only one or two elements.

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 2 and 5. This is the foundational framework that ANDE adapts into 7 domains (splitting I+D from Human Capital). Reference this framework when explaining the theoretical basis of the diagnostic.

---

## Extracto 14B -- Nine Prescriptions for Ecosystem Building

Isenberg distills practical principles from global case studies:

**1. Stop emulating Silicon Valley.** It evolved under unique, unreplicable circumstances. VCs there reject 97% of businesses they look at, and a significant proportion of that select group fails. Silicon Valley is as much a magnet for ready-made transplant entrepreneurs as a local breeding ground.

**2. Shape the ecosystem around local conditions.** Rwanda focused on coffee, tea, and tourism. Chile on fishing and natural resources. Israel and Iceland on human capital (resource-poor "islands"). Taiwan and Ireland on technology diaspora. Every ecosystem must be tailored to local dimensions, style, and climate.

**3. Engage the private sector first.** In numerous instances, corporate executives, family-business owners, universities, professional organizations, foundations, labor organizations, financiers, and entrepreneurs themselves have initiated and even financed entrepreneurship education, conferences, research, and policy advocacy. Private initiative makes it easier for governments to act.

**4. Favor the high potentials.** Especially if resources are limited, programs should focus on ambitious, growth-oriented entrepreneurs who address large potential markets. While 500 microfinanced sole proprietorships and one rapidly globalizing 500-person operation create the same number of jobs, wealth creation, inspirational power, labor force enrichment, and reputational value are much greater with the latter.

**5. Get a big win on the board.** Even one success can have a surprisingly stimulating effect -- the "law of small numbers." Examples: Skype in Estonia, Baidu in China, Celtel in sub-Saharan Africa, Elan/Iona in Ireland. Early visible successes reduce perceived barriers and highlight tangible rewards. Governments should overcelebrate successes through media events, awards, and public recognition.

**6. Tackle cultural change directly.** Chile transformed negative perceptions of entrepreneurs as greedy exploiters in just one decade through concerted government economic liberalization. Ireland went from stigmatizing bankruptcy to celebrating founders. Puerto Rico used weekly newspaper pages of startup success stories. Media plays a critical role in changing attitudes.

**7. Stress the roots.** Do not flood even high-potential entrepreneurs with easy money. New ventures must be exposed early to market rigors. Malaysia's program awarding 90% of 21,000 applicants had disappointing results due to lack of market discipline. Governments should "stress the roots" by meting out money carefully to develop toughness and resourcefulness.

**8. Do not overengineer clusters; help them grow organically.** Government-created clusters have a poor track record (Germany wasted $20B on biotech clusters). Governments should remain sector-neutral and "pave the footpath" by gently encouraging supportive activity around already-successful ventures, rather than planning new sidewalks.

**9. Reform legal, bureaucratic, and regulatory frameworks.** Removing barriers is better than creating incentives to overcome them. France's Auto-entrepreneur program simplified legal processes, leading to 300,000+ new businesses. The biggest impact may be reversing long-held negative attitudes about starting a business.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 (recommendations). Each prescription provides a strategic lens for framing ecosystem interventions. Particularly relevant: Prescription 2 (local context), 4 (focus on growth-oriented), 5 (success stories for culture), 7 (avoid easy money), 9 (regulatory reform).

---

## Extracto 14C -- The Ecosystem Assessment Checklist (BEEP Framework)

Isenberg, via the Babson Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Project (BEEP), provides diagnostic questions for each ecosystem element:

**Leadership/Policy:**
- Do public leaders act as strong advocates of entrepreneurs?
- Do governments create effective institutions directly associated with entrepreneurship?
- Have governments removed structural barriers (bankruptcy legislation, contract enforcement)?

**Culture:**
- Does the culture tolerate honest mistakes, honorable failure, risk taking, contrarian thinking?
- Does the culture respect entrepreneurship as a worthy occupation?
- Are there visible success stories that inspire youth and show ordinary people they too can become entrepreneurs?

**Human Capital:**
- Are there enough knowledgeable people with experience creating organizations, hiring, building systems?
- Are there experienced professional board members and advisers?

**Finance:**
- Are there capital sources providing equity at pre-sales stage?
- Do capital sources add non-monetary value (mentorship, contacts)?

**Support:**
- Do nonprofits and industry associations help investors and entrepreneurs network and learn?
- Are there educational institutions teaching financial literacy and entrepreneurship?
- Does public infrastructure provide sufficient transportation, communication (digital, broadband, mobile)?

**Markets:**
- Are there geographic concentrations of high-potential ventures?
- Are there formal/informal groups linking entrepreneurs, diaspora networks, and multinationals?
- Are there venture-oriented professionals (lawyers, accountants) who work on contingency or for stock?
- Are there local potential customers willing to try new products even if the company is new?

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (methodology), Section 3 (diagnostic framework). These questions can be mapped directly to ANDE's 30 indicators as qualitative validation probes.

---

## Extracto 14D -- Case Studies: Ecosystem Interventions That Worked

**Israel -- Yozma Fund (Finance domain):** In 1993, the government created a $100M fund of funds that in 3 years spawned 10 venture capital funds. Each fund had Israeli government, Israeli private partner, and foreign private partner with fund management expertise investing approximately equal amounts. Critical design feature: from the start, private partners had an option to buy out government interest at attractive terms. Eight of 10 funds exercised this option. Five years after founding, remaining assets were liquidated by auction. The government exit served as market proof that real value had been generated. The VC industry became self-sustaining and achieved quantum growth. Key lesson: design in self-liquidation from the start.

**Taiwan -- Diaspora engagement (Human Capital + Policy):** Government engaged the Taiwanese diaspora, consulting prominent executives in U.S. technology companies and establishing ongoing forums for input. Programs were built on expat suggestions. Diaspora liked how ideas were implemented and returned in droves in the 1990s, many to prominent policy positions or running new plants. Example: Morris Chang, former VP of Texas Instruments, returned to set up TSMC.

**Rwanda -- Targeted sector development (Markets + Policy):** Post-genocide government identified three local industries (coffee, tea, tourism) with proven potential. Actively organized supporting institutions, trained farmers to international standards, connected to overseas distribution. Result: 72,000 new ventures (mostly 2-3 person operations), tripled exports, reduced poverty by 25% in a decade.

**Ireland -- Cultural transformation (Culture):** In the 1980s, only 4% of the population was self-employed, the country was in deep recession, and bankruptcy was stigmatized. By the 1990s, after successful pioneers paved the way, hundreds of new software companies launched, some exporting, some going public. Entrepreneurs learned failure was survivable.

**France -- Auto-entrepreneur (Policy):** Simplified legal process for creating small businesses, allowed avoiding onerous tax prepayments, eliminated disincentives. Result: 300,000+ new businesses, 10,000+ new starts per month.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 (recommendations). These case studies provide evidence-based models for specific interventions. Israel/Yozma for finance recommendations, Taiwan for diaspora/human capital, Rwanda for market-focused sector strategies.

---

## Extracto 14E -- Strategic Principles for Ecosystem Intervention

Key strategic insights synthesized from the article:

1. **Holistic approach is mandatory.** Taiwan strengthened several elements simultaneously: IC research, science park, training programs, diaspora engagement, VC legislation. Ignoring interconnected nature leads to perverse outcomes (brain drain from aspirations without environment; overvalued deals from government money without deal flow).

2. **Incubators are not a quick fix.** When well conceived and managed, they can take 20 years or longer to generate a measurable impact. Poorly conceived, they become white elephants.

3. **Removing barriers beats creating incentives.** All else being equal, removing administrative and legal barriers to venture formation is better than creating incentives to overcome barriers.

4. **The horizon always recedes.** Even Massachusetts and Israel, two of the most entrepreneurial places in the world, are not satisfied. There is never "enough" entrepreneurship, no right answers, and no choice but to continue experimenting and learning.

5. **Private sector must share responsibility.** Government cannot do everything on its own. Corporate executives, universities, professional organizations, foundations, and entrepreneurs themselves must initiate and even finance ecosystem development.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 5 (strategic analysis), Section 6 (challenges). These principles frame the overall strategic posture of ecosystem development -- avoid silver-bullet thinking, emphasize systemic intervention, set realistic timelines.



---

## [Ref 15]

# Extractos: REF_impacts.pdf

- **Source:** `drive/REF_impacts.pdf`
- **Title:** Entrepreneurial ecosystems: economic, technological, and societal impacts
- **Authors:** David B. Audretsch, James A. Cunningham, Donald F. Kuratko, Erik E. Lehmann, Matthias Menter
- **Year:** 2019 (published online September 2018)
- **Journal:** The Journal of Technology Transfer, 44:313-325
- **DOI:** 10.1007/s10961-018-9690-4
- **Pages:** 13
- **Relevance ANDE:** Moderate-High. Provides theoretical grounding for ecosystem impact measurement across three dimensions (economic, technological, societal). Introduces the "oikos" framework for understanding ecosystem boundaries and resource allocation. Useful for framing ANDE's Condition-Result pattern and justifying multi-dimensional assessment.

---

## Extracto 15A -- Three Dimensions of Ecosystem Impact

Audretsch et al. argue that entrepreneurial ecosystems generate impacts across three interconnected dimensions, drawing from the ancient Greek concept of the oikos:

**1. Economic impacts:**
- Anticipated increase in locational capital wealth and prosperity
- Value creation through regional agglomeration of local factors/resources and their entrepreneurial exploitation
- Spillover effects contributing to regional vibrancy, sustainability, and viability
- Competitive advantages and value for individual firms, sectors, and regional innovation outcomes
- National and regional reputation that attracts financial and human capital into the ecosystem

**2. Technological impacts:**
- Regional innovation mechanisms and the efficiency of how innovation is pursued and realized
- Efficient transformation of ideas/inventions to innovative products and services (technology transfer)
- Disruptive potential of ideas that influence competitive positioning of a region
- Processes that encourage or discourage entrepreneurial behavior

**3. Societal impacts:**
- Networks among a range of actors participating in ecosystems
- Co-existence of new ventures, SMEs, large firms, universities, and research institutions
- Governance and organization enabling efficient knowledge flows and value creation
- Social clusters forming boundaries along venture type, support institution type, gender, and ethnicity

Key insight: These three dimensions are not independent -- economic success supports reputation, which attracts human and financial capital (technological); social networks enable knowledge flows that drive innovation (economic). The challenge is that input-output relations are often multiple, and inputs/outputs cannot be clearly defined.

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 2 and 5. This three-dimensional framework complements ANDE's Condition-Result pattern. Condition domains (Policy, Support, Human Capital, R&D) primarily affect technological and societal dimensions. Result domains (Finance, Culture, Markets) primarily reflect economic and societal outcomes.

---

## Extracto 15B -- The Ecosystem Definition Problem and Boundary Conditions

The paper identifies a fundamental challenge in ecosystem research: exact definitions of what ecosystems really comprise are scarce and often inconsistent. Key issues:

1. **Boundary problem:** Existing vague descriptions do not consider the boundaries of respective agglomerations, impeding evaluation of performance and outcome measures. Ecosystems can be described by economic activities ("innovation ecosystem"), by actors ("entrepreneurial ecosystems"), or by boundaries ("national ecosystems").

2. **No unified theory:** Despite the explosion of ecosystem literature (from near-zero in 2000 to 2500+ studies by 2017 on Google Scholar), there exists no "theory" of the ecosystem. The literature suffers from lack of development and chronic inconsistency.

3. **Metaphor risk:** The ecosystem metaphor can be pushed too far. It is often treated as a metaphor for a special kind of network, for a certain market or niche, for the complementarity of assets, or for externalities of certain activities. Critical questions remain unanswered about spatial and virtual boundaries, governance aspects, and whether ecosystems are artificial or built from scratch.

4. **Practical implication:** For ecosystem diagnostics like ANDE Phase 1, this means geographic scope must be explicitly defined (city, region, country), and the assessment must acknowledge that ecosystem boundaries are porous -- actors, capital, and knowledge flow across them.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (methodology). Acknowledge the boundary challenge when defining the scope of the diagnostic. The ANDE city-level assessment is a pragmatic choice, but note that ecosystems extend beyond city limits and interact with regional/national systems.

---

## Extracto 15C -- Key Findings from Special Issue Papers (Empirical Evidence)

The paper introduces six empirical studies, each with actionable findings for ecosystem diagnostics:

**1. Public Research and Innovation (Fudickar & Hottenrott 2018):**
- New technology-based firms (NTBFs) engaging in knowledge interactions with public research institutions are more likely to introduce new products/services to market
- Continuous informal interactions complement formal ones in the absence of own R&D activity
- Implication for ANDE: I+D domain scores should consider not just patents/publications but the quality of university-industry interaction channels

**2. Research Network Diversity (Nepelski et al. 2018):**
- Innovations developed by research networks with higher organizational diversity have more commercial potential
- Research networks with wider geographic dispersion have less innovation potential
- Implication for ANDE: Local research density matters more than international research connections for ecosystem innovation output

**3. Academic Spinoffs and Internationalization (Civera et al. 2018):**
- University spinoffs are more prone to internationalize than non-academic counterparts
- Universities as key actors disseminate and commercialize knowledge through spinoffs
- Implication for ANDE: H1 (universities with entrepreneurship) should weight commercialization capacity, not just program existence

**4. Patent-Based Investment Funds (Jarchow & Rohm 2018):**
- Patent-based funds decrease knowledge filters and fill financing gaps in early technology development
- Commercialization strategies must be linked to specific technology characteristics
- Implication for ANDE: Finance domain should distinguish between general venture capital and technology-specific funding mechanisms

**5. University Ecosystem Patenting (Backs et al. 2018):**
- Technology transfer offices (TTOs) facilitate and promote patenting activities and entrepreneurship
- Agent-based simulation shows that evaluation measures can stimulate academic patenting and spin-off creation
- Implication for ANDE: I1 (patents) indicator gains meaning when contextualized by TTO effectiveness

**6. Social Boundaries in Ecosystems (Neumeyer & Santos 2018):**
- Entrepreneurial ecosystems consist of different social clusters, forming boundaries along venture type (high-growth vs. lifestyle), support institution type (university vs. government), gender, and ethnicity
- These social boundaries affect knowledge flows and opportunity access
- Implication for ANDE: Culture domain (C5 social trust) and ecosystem analysis should account for fragmentation along social lines

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 3, 5, and 7. These empirical findings provide nuance for interpreting domain scores and crafting recommendations. Particularly useful for R&D/Innovation and Human Capital domains.

---

## Extracto 15D -- The "Oikos" Framework for Ecosystem Resource Allocation

The paper traces the ecosystem concept to the Greek philosopher Hesiod (700 BC) and the concept of oikos:

- An oikos was the basic economic unit of ancient Greek city-states: a self-sufficient, autarkic unit encompassing different people, materials, goods, and activities under governance of the despot as an institution
- A well-developed oikos allocates resources to support value creation for economic, technological, and societal benefits
- The ecosystem is self-defined by boundaries, where the species within live together in autarky. Boundaries could be physical or not, but are associated with entry and exit barriers
- Agents absorb necessary resources from the ecosystem and produce critical resources for others, which spillover within and beyond boundaries

**Practical framework translation for ANDE diagnostics:**

| Oikos element | ANDE equivalent | Diagnostic question |
|--------------|-----------------|-------------------|
| Boundary definition | City/metropolitan area scope | Is the geographic scope well-defined? Do actors identify with it? |
| Resource allocation | Domain balance (Condition vs. Result) | Are resources flowing to where they create most value? |
| Self-sufficiency | Ecosystem maturity level | Can the ecosystem sustain entrepreneurial activity without external intervention? |
| Governance (despot) | Policy domain + backbone organizations | Is there coordinated leadership or fragmented governance? |
| Entry/exit barriers | Regulatory environment + market access | What prevents new actors from entering or failed ones from exiting? |
| Spillovers | Cross-domain effects | Do improvements in one domain cascade to others? |

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (theoretical framing), Section 5 (strategic analysis). The oikos framework helps explain why ecosystem maturity levels (Naciente through Autosostenible) correspond to increasing degrees of self-sufficiency and internal resource generation.

---

## Extracto 15E -- Research Gaps Relevant to Ecosystem Diagnostics

The paper identifies critical research gaps that have practical implications for ANDE Phase 1 diagnostics:

**1. Micro-level analysis needed:** How do different actors, materials, and goods organize -- formally and informally -- to achieve beneficial outcomes individually and collectively? Is it the same group of actors that drives development irrespective of geography, or do external shocks instigate ecosystem creation?

**2. Role of individuals:** What role do individuals play in shaping creation, evolution, and sustainability of ecosystems? This is relevant to ANDE's assessment of mentors (S4) and the concept of "backbone organizations" from the Tec de Monterrey model.

**3. Entry and failure dynamics:** The entry of new firms can build capability and capacity but may have unintended consequences. Firm failure can undermine perceived reputation and destroy individual/collective value. However, failure also frees up resources and capital for new opportunities. This tension is central to ANDE's Culture domain assessment (tolerance for failure as part of C5 social trust).

**4. Evaluation methodology:** Advancing the array of appropriate evaluation tools to assess performance, vibrancy, stability, and resilience of an ecosystem is necessary. Standard production functions (input-output) are inadequate because: (a) input-output relations are often multiple, (b) inputs and outputs cannot be clearly defined, (c) entrepreneurial firms serve as both inputs to and outputs of ecosystems simultaneously.

**5. Cross-context replication:** How replicable are entrepreneurial ecosystems across different sectoral, technology, geographic, regulatory, and legal environments? Future studies should apply cross-country, cross-industry, cross-societal research designs.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 6 (challenges/limitations). Acknowledge that the ANDE Phase 1 diagnostic captures a snapshot of ecosystem determinants, not a dynamic model. Recommend longitudinal tracking (Phase 2+) to capture evolution, entry/exit dynamics, and the role of key individuals.

---

## Extracto 15F -- Ecosystem Governance and the "Knowledge Filter"

The paper introduces the concept of the "knowledge filter" in entrepreneurial ecosystems:

- Entrepreneurial firms and new venture creation serve as important inputs to ecosystems in order to penetrate the knowledge filter
- The knowledge filter refers to the gap between knowledge creation (at universities, research labs) and knowledge exploitation (by firms, in markets)
- Ecosystems differ in their efficiency at converting knowledge into commercial value
- Standard economic theory fails in ecosystem analysis because profit-maximizing assumptions do not hold for public institutions (universities, government agencies) that are key ecosystem actors

**Governance implications:**
- Market theory assumes the "invisible hand" coordinating agents via the price mechanism, but this does not apply to ecosystems where many actors are non-profit or public
- Ecosystem governance requires intentional coordination mechanisms (what GIZ calls PPD mechanisms, what the Tec de Monterrey model calls backbone organizations)
- The governance question is especially critical in developing economy ecosystems where public institutions play outsized roles

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 5 (strategic analysis), Section 7 (recommendations). The knowledge filter concept helps explain why I+D domain scores may be high (knowledge creation) while Markets domain scores are low (knowledge exploitation). Recommendations should address the transfer mechanisms, not just increase inputs.

---

## Extracto 15G -- Ecosystem Impact Measurement Framework

Synthesizing the paper's contributions, a practical framework for measuring ecosystem impacts emerges:

| Impact dimension | What to measure | ANDE domain mapping | Data challenge |
|-----------------|----------------|---------------------|---------------|
| **Economic** | Firm creation rates, employment, GDP contribution, export growth, investment attraction | Markets (M1, M2, M3), Finance (F1, F2, F5) | Attribution problem: isolating ecosystem effects from macro trends |
| **Technological** | Patents, spinoffs, technology transfer deals, new product introductions, R&D expenditure | R&D/Innovation (I1, I2, I3), Human Capital (H2, H4) | Lag effects: R&D inputs today produce outputs years later |
| **Societal** | Network density, social trust, inclusion (gender, ethnicity), failure tolerance, aspirational role models | Culture (C1-C5), Support Services (S4 mentors) | Measurement validity: culture indicators are perception-based |

**Key methodological insight:** Entrepreneurial ecosystem performance cannot be captured by a single aggregate measure. Multiple inputs create multiple outputs, and the same actor can be both input and output simultaneously (e.g., a successful startup is both a product of the ecosystem and a contributor to it as employer, role model, and potential investor).

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 2 (methodology), Section 8 (indicator table notes). This framework justifies ANDE's multi-domain approach and explains why global scores should be interpreted as approximations rather than precise measurements.



---

## [Ref 16]

# EXTRACTS: REF_kauffman.pdf
## Measuring an Entrepreneurial Ecosystem
**Autores:** Dane Stangler, Jordan Bell-Masterson
**Fuente:** Kauffman Foundation Research Series on City, Metro, and Regional Entrepreneurship (March 2015)
**Foco:** Marco de medicion de ecosistemas emprendedores -- 4 indicadores de vitalidad

---

### 16A -- Marco de 4 indicadores de vitalidad ecosistemica
**Seccion:** Introduction (pp. 1-2)
**Dominio ANDE:** Transversal (metodologia de medicion)
**Tipo:** Marco conceptual

La Kauffman Foundation propone cuatro indicadores agregados para medir la vitalidad de un ecosistema emprendedor: **Densidad, Fluidez, Conectividad y Diversidad**. La premisa fundamental: la mayoria de ecosistemas fallan porque dependen de metricas de insumo (I+D universitario, capital disponible, titulos de ingenieria) en lugar de medir resultados. El otro extremo --medir todo-- tampoco funciona porque no ofrece priorizacion. Estos cuatro indicadores buscan el punto medio entre ambos extremos. Critico: lo que importa no es la fotografia del momento sino la **trayectoria en el tiempo**, por lo que todos los indicadores deben rastrearse de forma continua, idealmente con frecuencia anual.

---

### 16B -- Densidad: empresas jovenes per capita como metrica central
**Seccion:** Density (pp. 2-3)
**Dominio ANDE:** M (Mercados), transversal
**Tipo:** Indicador operativo + benchmark

La densidad mide tres cosas: (1) Numero de empresas nuevas y jovenes (<5 o <10 anos) por cada 1,000 habitantes; (2) Participacion en el empleo de empresas nuevas y jovenes (captura fundadores + empleados); (3) Densidad sectorial, especialmente alta tecnologia, usando poblacion como denominador. La densidad es el equivalente estadistico de la probabilidad de encontrar emprendedores caminando por la calle (referencia a Brad Feld). Empresas nuevas y jovenes NO son lo mismo que pequenas empresas -- la distincion es critica. La densidad de alta tecnologia importa por su efecto multiplicador sobre empresas no tecnologicas.

**Aplicacion ANDE:** El indicador M1 (Startups activas) se alinea directamente. Para ciudades <1M hab, el ajuste per capita que usa ANDE (valor/poblacion x 1M) sigue la misma logica que Kauffman recomienda.

---

### 16C -- Fluidez: movimiento de personas, empleo y empresas de alto crecimiento
**Seccion:** Fluidity (pp. 3-4)
**Dominio ANDE:** H (Capital Humano), M (Mercados)
**Tipo:** Indicador operativo

La fluidez mide la capacidad del ecosistema para reasignar recursos.
