## Extracto 22N — Culture: Personal Development & Community Support (pp. 171-182)
**Section:** 3.5.1 Personal Development / 3.5.2 Community Support
**ANDE Domain:** C (Cultura) — C1, C2, C3, C4, C5

### Personal Development Objectives
- Promoting entrepreneurship
- Fostering: self-confidence, ambition, non-fear of failure, opportunity identification

### KPIs
- Non-fear of failure/Risk acceptance (thegedi.org)
- Opportunity startup (thegedi.org)

### Key Personal Development Instruments

| Instrument | Example |
|---|---|
| Encourage personal talent exploration | Mauritius (revise curricula with strong personal development elements) |
| Reduce stigma of failure | EU Think Young "Fail 2 Succeed" campaign with famous failure testimonials |
| Promote experiential learning | Alfatoun (35 African countries, age-appropriate business skills for children) |
| Increase equitable access | Kenya KYEOP (skills + capital for youth entrepreneurs) |
| English language skills | Rwanda (booklets + mobile/web apps for English instruction) |
| Promote financial literacy | German Sparkassenstiftung (financial skills + uncertainty management, Burundi) |
| Youth entrepreneurship culture | Rwanda Young Enterprise Scheme (teams of 14+ run businesses for academic year + mentorship + annual awards) |

### Key Statistic
76% of young people (18-24) across 14 African countries want to start a business in next 5 years, but only 51% find it easy to accept failure as learning.

### Community Support Instruments

| Instrument | Example |
|---|---|
| Disseminate success stories | Rwanda (annual innovation/technology awards) |
| National competitions | Nigeria YouWin (1M-10M Naira for best business plans) |
| Role model engagement | Branson Centre South Africa (podcast series with industry experts) |
| Community mobilization | Rwanda (Umuganda community meetings for entrepreneurship awareness) |
| Enhance entrepreneurship appeal | Kenya MSE Policy (signature song and logo competition) |
| Leverage social media | AU Commission "1 Million By 2021" initiative |
| Philanthropic programs | Tony Elumelu Foundation (1,000 startups/year, training + mentoring + $10K seed capital) |

### KPIs for Community Support
- Cultural support (thegedi.org)

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Directly maps to C1-C5. The African Youth Survey statistic (76% want to start business but only 51% accept failure) is a useful benchmark for C5 (Confianza social). Tony Elumelu model is a replicable private-sector intervention for Naciente/Emergente ecosystems.

- Networking (thegedi.org)

### Key Statistic
34.2% of female-led businesses in Nigeria did not record growth in past year, with limited access to finance as main constraint.

### KPIs for Women Entrepreneurship
- Percent of firms with female participation in ownership (enterprisesurveys.org)
- Gender equality index (hdr.undp.org)

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Networking instruments map to C1 (Meetups) and C4 (Comunidades online). Gender data provides benchmarks for inclusive ecosystem assessment. The Senegal 15% procurement set-aside is a concrete policy recommendation. Geographic clustering (Konza) relevant for M2 (Corporativos tech) and M3 (Sectores especializacion).

- Mobile tariffs
- Handset prices

2. **Domain name provision** — National domain for startups (Senegal: .sn domain for startups)
3. **Free public internet** — Rwanda: 400+ buses connected to 4G with free WiFi

- Mission: assist creation/development of ICT, GreenTech, and cultural industry companies
- Public-private partnership model
- Provides: parks, electricity, internet, grants, administrative support
- Energy subsidies for medium enterprises transitioning to solar

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Digital infrastructure KPIs provide data sources for S5 and H5 scoring. The ZBTIC/Technopark models are relevant recommendations for S3 (Coworkings) in contexts where basic infrastructure is a constraint. Infrastructure is typically not prioritized in ANDE recommendations per Table 4.3 unless it represents a critical bottleneck.

The Kenya KIBT differentiated training model provides a concrete recommendation structure for ecosystems needing human capital development. Innovation vouchers (Namibia) bridge H and I+D domains.

## Extracto 22R — Human Capital: Labour Market (pp. 223-231)

| Instrument | Example |

Skills assessments (Nigeria model) provide data infrastructure for better H domain scoring.

## [Ref 23]

# EXTRACTS: Growth-Oriented Entrepreneurs (e2 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems / ANDE/Argidius, 2020)

**Source:** ref_GrowthOriented_Entrepreneurs_2020_FINAL.pdf
**Authors:** e2 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (Don Macke et al.), with special recognition to Christine Hamilton-Pennell (Growing Local Economies)
**Type:** Practice brief (9 pages)
**Year:** June 2020

---

## Overview

This paper distinguishes growth-oriented entrepreneurs (GOEs) from lifestyle business owners and provides a framework for community strategies to identify and support them. Although US-focused, the segmentation framework and support strategies are directly applicable to ANDE ecosystem diagnostics, particularly for understanding what types of entrepreneurs drive economic impact and how support services should be designed.

---

## Extracto 23A — Growth Entrepreneur Definition and Segmentation
**Section:** Growth Entrepreneurs (pp. 2-3)
**ANDE Domain:** M (Mercados) — M1 Startups activas; S (Servicios de Apoyo)

### Definition
Growth-oriented entrepreneurs (GOEs) have both a strong desire for growth AND the potential capacity to realize sustained growth. They seek to reach external markets, expanding the economy by bringing in new wealth (investment, jobs, tax base) that recirculates through the local economy.

### Key Distinction from Lifestyle Businesses
Most self-employed and lifestyle business owners are satisfied making a decent living. They serve a local geographic area without external market footprint. These businesses contribute to quality of life and amenities but are not economic multipliers.

### GOE Characteristics
- Know from the outset they want to grow
- Express desire to: hire more employees, increase capital expenditure, explore new external markets, open new locations
- Have recognized a market opportunity
- Seek to scale (profit or non-profit)

### GOE Quote (from practitioner)
"Most small businesses equate growth with supporting the business and themselves. A very different perception and understanding of growth extends beyond the backyard to new markets. [Growth-oriented companies] probably think about all this from day one."

### Economic Impact Data
- Businesses surviving the startup stage usually create more net jobs than startups
- Most sustained job growth comes from expansion of existing businesses (not startups or relocations)
- High-growth companies represent less than 5% of all businesses yet create up to two-thirds of all business growth and job creation
- These entrepreneurs generate up to 50% of all innovation
- High-growth firms are found in most regions and across ALL industries (not just high-tech)
- Most rural areas have few high-growth entrepreneurs but a larger pool of GOEs who want to grow but lack knowledge

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Critical for interpreting M1 (Startups activas). Raw startup count matters less than the proportion of growth-oriented ventures. When scoring M1, consider whether identified startups demonstrate growth orientation (external markets, scaling plans) versus lifestyle businesses. The <5% / two-thirds statistic provides context for why ecosystem interventions should be targeted rather than broad-based.

---

## Extracto 23B — Business Stage Framework
**Section:** Business Stages (pp. 3-4)
**ANDE Domain:** M (Mercados) — M1; S (Servicios de Apoyo)

### Edward Lowe Foundation Stage Classification (YourEconomy.org)

| Stage | Employees | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Self-employed | 1 | Solo operator |
| Stage 1 | 2-9 | Small team, owner-controlled |
| Stage 2 | 10-99 | Beyond owner control span, needs professional managers but not full management team |
| Stage 3 | 100-499 | Full professional management |
| Stage 4 | 500+ | Large enterprise |

### Stage 2 Profile
- Revenue range: ~$750K to $50M (varies by industry)
- Have grown past startup but not reached maturity
- Benefit from professional managers but lack full management team
- Create substantial new jobs

### Critical Finding on Growth Drivers
- A more significant factor in firm growth is **age** rather than size
- Younger companies create more jobs regardless of size
- Growth occurs among companies of all sizes

### Optimal Target for Community Programs
- **Late Stage 1 and early Stage 2** companies
- Between 1-5 years old
- Want to grow regardless of current size
- Not necessarily high-tech
- Have developed innovation in product, process, or delivery method
- Have potential or actual market outside local economic region
- Create quality, living-wage jobs

### Screening Questions for GOEs
1. Do they have a niche where they are competitive?
2. Are they committed to growth?
3. Are they actively exploring creating an external market footprint?
4. Are they "coachable" and willing to act on information and advice?

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The stage framework provides a lens for evaluating M1 (Startups activas) beyond simple counting. The screening questions can inform qualitative notes for M1 and help determine whether an ecosystem's startups have genuine growth potential. The finding that firm age matters more than size challenges traditional approaches to ecosystem measurement.

---

## Extracto 23C — Support Strategy Framework for Growth Entrepreneurs
**Section:** Entrepreneurship Support Strategies (pp. 4-5)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S1, S2, S4; F (Financiamiento)

### GOE Needs Hierarchy

| Need | Description | ANDE Mapping |
|---|---|---|
| **Capital access** (human and financial) | Both talent and investment | F1-F6, H4 |
| **Strategic coaching and technical assistance** | Quality mentoring and professional services | S1, S2, S4 |
| **Appropriate facilities and infrastructure** | Physical space + digital connectivity | S3, S5 |
| **Friendly tax and regulatory environment** | Enabling policy context | P1, P2 |
| **Market intelligence and external market access** | Data-driven market strategy | M3 |
| **Trained workforce** | Skilled employees available | H2, H4 |
| **Strategic alliances** | Supply chain and peer networks | C1, C4 |
| **Management capacity** | Ability to manage in global marketplace | H1 |

### Technical Capacity Building
- Quality coaching (e.g., eCoaching model) connects GOEs to appropriate technical assistance resources
- Strong local referral networks and professional business services (finance, legal, accounting) are essential
- Peer entrepreneur networks for wisdom and experience sharing

### Capital Access Insights
- Cash availability may be high but risk aversion creates tight supply
- Challenge is more complicated than simple capital shortage
- Business planning requirements have increased substantially
- Key resource identification: commercial lenders, microenterprise programs, angel investors, government programs (SBA, USDA, CDBG)
- Better market intelligence reduces risk for both capital providers and entrepreneurs

### Market Intelligence
- A discipline practiced by all Fortune 500 companies; smaller GOEs need it too
- Components: competitor research, customer profiles, market opportunities, suppliers, distribution channels, legal/pricing/branding information
- Critical for business and capitalization plans
- Enables scenario testing to assess risk before market entry

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The GOE needs hierarchy maps comprehensively to ANDE's 7 domains. Use this framework when designing recommendations in Section 7 of the report to ensure recommendations address the full spectrum of GOE needs. The market intelligence concept is particularly relevant for ecosystems with weak M3 (Sectores especializacion) scores — it suggests that specialized market intelligence services should be a priority support service.

---

## Extracto 23D — Infrastructure and Community Development
**Section:** Infrastructure (pp. 5-6)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S3, S5; H (Capital Humano) — H5

### Physical Infrastructure Requirements
- Water, wastewater services
- Roads, power, telecommunications
- Adequate transportation logistics

### Quality of Life Factors for Entrepreneur Retention
- Safe communities with good schools and higher education
- Quality housing
- Strong cultural and recreational amenities
- Vibrant local retail and service businesses
- Accessible healthcare, childcare, elder care services

### Key Insight
Entrepreneurs can live and grow ventures anywhere — this is particularly true for GOEs with external market footprint. Physical infrastructure and quality-of-life considerations are key factors in location decisions.

### Integration Principle
"In a rural community, community development and economic development are two sides of the same coin, and both are needed to help a community create new hope, increase wealth, and expand choices."

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Quality of life factors, while not directly measured in ANDE's 30 indicators, influence H4 (Talento tech) and retention. For ecosystem recommendations, emphasize that infrastructure investment (S5, H5) has indirect effects on talent attraction and retention. The integration principle supports holistic ecosystem strategies rather than single-domain interventions.

---

## Extracto 23E — Case Studies: Market Intelligence Impact
**Section:** Case Studies (pp. 6-7)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo); M (Mercados)

### Case 1: Solar Roast Coffee (Pueblo, Colorado)
- **Context:** Two brothers developed solar coffee-roasting technology in small city (115 miles from Denver)
- **Support received:** County GIS-based Economic Gardening program provided: core customer base analysis, competitor mapping within state, underserved expansion areas, wholesale contact information, traffic counts, demographic analysis, connection to funders
- **Additional support:** $100K grant from Economic Development Corporation for 10 additional hires
- **Impact:** Multiple retail expansions, 50+ wholesale locations across Colorado, university research partnerships, manufacturing spinoff plans

### Case 2: Keyscaper (Tualatin, Oregon)
- **Context:** Custom printed keyboard manufacturer, launched during 2009 recession
- **Support received:** Regional Economic Gardening program provided strategic market information and analysis
- **Impact:** Sales grew from thousands/month to $100K+/month within one year; acquired Best Buy, Google, Boeing as customers through program-connected partners; added 16 positions (50% staff increase in one year)

### Key Takeaway
"Upon implementing their recommendations, Keyscaper's sales skyrocketed from a few thousand dollars a month to over $100,000... The results speak for themselves: Sales growth in our Keyscaper line combined with core business growth allowed us to add 16 positions, increasing our staff size by 50% in one year."

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These case studies demonstrate the impact of market intelligence as a support service. When ecosystems score low on S1/S2 but have existing growth-oriented businesses, market intelligence programs (lower cost than full incubation/acceleration) can be a high-impact, quick-win recommendation. The Economic Gardening model (GIS-based market analysis provided to local businesses) is replicable in emerging ecosystems.

---

## Extracto 23F — Program Design Principles and Further Reading
**Section:** How e2 Can Help / Further Reading (pp. 8-9)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo); C (Cultura)

### e2 Service Model Components
1. **Mentoring** — Advising and coaching new practitioners on eEcosystem strategies
2. **Analytics Support** — Community/regional entrepreneurial potential research and data
3. **e2 University (e2U)** — 1,000+ guides, papers, stories, tools for ecosystem building
4. **Fostering the eMovement** — National coalition building (Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City, SourceLink, Edward Lowe Foundation, Kauffman Foundation, NetWork Kansas)

### Key Research References on Growth Companies
- Acs, Parsons & Tracy (2008) — High Impact Firms: Gazelles Revisited (SBA Research Summary #328)
- Haltiwanger, Jarmin & Miranda (2010) — Who Creates Jobs? Small vs. Large vs. Young (NBER Working Paper 16300)
- Henrekson & Johansson (2009) — Gazelles as Job Creators (Small Business Economics)
- Kane (2010) — The Importance of Startups in Job Creation and Destruction (Kauffman Foundation)
- Stangler (2010) — High-Growth Firms and the Future of the American Economy (Kauffman Foundation)

### Program Design Principles (implicit)
1. Start with identification and targeting — not all businesses need the same support
2. Focus on growth-oriented entrepreneurs specifically, not all SMEs
3. Market intelligence is a high-leverage, scalable intervention
4. Combine community development with economic development
5. Use data and analytics as foundation for support services
6. Build networks and coalitions across institutions

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The research references provide evidence base for M1 and economic impact claims. The program design principles align with ANDE Table 4.3 sequencing — focus support services on the right target population before scaling. The e2 model demonstrates that ecosystem building organizations themselves (equivalent to "backbone organizations" in the Sanchez-Dominguez model from Cap. 5) are critical infrastructure.



---

## [Ref 24]

# EXTRACTS: Mentoring in Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (e2/e3 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems, 2022)

**Source:** ref_Mentors_Final.pdf
**Authors:** e2/e3 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (Don Macke et al.)
**Type:** Practice brief (2 pages)
**Date:** May 19, 2022

---

## Overview

This concise practice brief provides a framework for mentoring programs within entrepreneurial ecosystems. Despite its brevity, it contains a structured taxonomy of mentoring delivery methods, a targeting framework by entrepreneur development stage, and practical implementation guidance. The content maps directly to ANDE indicator S4 (Mentores) and informs recommendations for the Services de Apoyo domain.

---

## Extracto 24A — Mentoring Delivery Taxonomy
**Section:** Continuum of Working with Entrepreneurs (p. 1)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S4 Mentores

### Nine Mentoring Delivery Methods

| Method | Description | Intensity | Cost | Scalability |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Traditional Mentors** | Experienced business owner/operator working 1-on-1 with targeted entrepreneur | High | Low (volunteer) | Low |
| **Peer Groups** | Facilitated peer groups providing both mentoring and peer counseling | Medium | Low | Medium |
| **Business-to-Business Events** | Structured B2B events providing expertise and peer counseling | Low | Medium | High |
| **Workshops & Trainings** | Structured events providing expertise and peer mentoring opportunities | Low | Medium | High |
| **Growth Counseling** | Higher-order mentoring focused specifically on helping entrepreneurs succeed with venture growth | High | Medium | Low |
| **Advisory Boards** | Boards with targeted expertise — efficient and comprehensive mentoring approach | High | Low | Low |
| **Voluntary Boards of Directors** | Similar to advisory boards but more engaged, often include investors | High | Low | Low |
| **Entrepreneur Coaching** | One-on-one coaching, can include topical mentors | High | Medium-High | Low |
| **Business Counseling** | One-on-one basic business plan and feasibility counseling, often using online tools | Medium | Medium | Medium |

### Key Distinction
The paper focuses on **traditional mentoring** using experienced business owners/operators as the primary model, but acknowledges the full spectrum as complementary approaches.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When scoring S4 (Mentores), use this taxonomy to assess the ecosystem comprehensively. A count of "mentors" is insufficient — the diagnostic should identify which delivery methods exist. An ecosystem with only traditional mentoring scores lower than one with multiple methods (peer groups + coaching + advisory boards). This taxonomy also helps structure recommendations: Naciente ecosystems should start with traditional mentoring and peer groups; En Desarrollo ecosystems should add growth counseling and advisory boards.

---

## Extracto 24B — Targeting Framework: Where Mentoring Is Most Effective
**Section:** Where Mentoring is Most Helpful (pp. 1-2)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S4; M (Mercados) — M1

### Four Target Groups for Traditional Mentoring

| Target Group | Profile | Mentor Role | ANDE Relevance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Aspiring & Startups** | Limited or bad prior business experience; newbies | Listen, ask great questions, help discover success requirements | M1 (pipeline), C5 (support culture) |
| **2. Challenged or Struggling** | Something is limiting success; may be multiple issues | Help entrepreneur discover what is holding them back; guide toward self-found solutions | M1 (survival rate), S1/S2 (complement to formal programs) |
| **3. Transitions** | Venture transitions or successions (both seller and buyer sides) | Optimize successful transitions; may work with one or both parties | M1 (business continuity), F5 (exits facilitation) |
| **4. Growth-Oriented** | Seeking new markets, products, customers, locations; must build teams and systems, delegate responsibility | Growth planning and mentoring; foundational to growth success | M1 (scaling), connects to Growth-Oriented Entrepreneurs framework |

### Critical Insight on Approach
The best mentors **listen and ask great questions** rather than giving direct answers. They help entrepreneurs **discover their own solutions**. This is distinct from consulting (which provides answers) or coaching (which may be more directive).

### Growth-Oriented Entrepreneurs — Specific Challenge
"Successfully achieving growth can be challenging as founder/operator entrepreneurs must build teams and systems delegating responsibility." Growth mentoring addresses the transition from founder-controlled to professionally-managed organization.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This targeting framework provides quality criteria for S4 scoring. Ecosystems where mentoring only serves aspiring entrepreneurs (group 1) are less mature than those serving all four groups. When recommending mentoring programs for ecosystems scored Emergente or En Desarrollo, specify which target group needs most attention based on the ecosystem's specific weaknesses. The growth-oriented target group connects directly to the Growth-Oriented Entrepreneurs paper and M1 assessment.

---

## Extracto 24C — Mentoring Program Implementation Guide
**Section:** Getting Started (p. 2)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S4 Mentores

### Five Implementation Principles

1. **Start small, learn and grow over time** — Mentoring programs should be iterative; avoid over-engineering at launch
2. **Target specific entrepreneurs** — By economic sector and/or development stage; avoid generic "anyone can participate" models
3. **Train, support, and manage volunteer mentors** — Mentors need preparation; raw business experience alone is insufficient
4. **Test mentors and curate a high-performing team** — Not every experienced business person is a good mentor; curation is essential
5. **Use mentoring only when the entrepreneur is open to being mentored** — Mentoring cannot be forced; readiness and openness are prerequisites for effectiveness

### Quality Indicators (implicit from the five principles)

| Quality Indicator | Poor Practice | Good Practice |
|---|---|---|
| Program scope | Large generic program from day one | Started small, iterated, grew based on learning |
| Targeting | Open to anyone | Segmented by sector and/or development stage |
| Mentor preparation | Volunteers assigned directly based on business experience | Trained, supported, and managed mentors |
| Mentor quality | Anyone willing to volunteer is accepted | Tested and curated; underperformers removed |
| Matching criteria | Random or availability-based | Based on entrepreneur readiness and openness |

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These five principles provide a quality checklist for assessing S4 beyond simple mentor count. When evaluating an ecosystem's mentoring, ask: (1) Is there a formal program or only ad-hoc mentoring? (2) Are mentors trained? (3) Is there targeting by sector/stage? (4) Is there quality control/curation? (5) Are entrepreneurs screened for readiness? An ecosystem with 50 untrained volunteer mentors may score lower than one with 15 well-curated, trained mentors in a structured program.

---

## Extracto 24D — Ecosystem Business Services Integration
**Section:** Energizing Your Community's Business Services (p. 2)
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S1, S2, S3, S4

### Integration Principle
Growing a community's business services sector can build mentoring capabilities for a wide range of entrepreneur talent. Mentoring should not exist in isolation but as part of a broader business services ecosystem.

### Related Resources Referenced
- Working with Entrepreneurs Guide
- Entrepreneur Coaching framework
- Entrepreneurial Communities and Business Services paper and podcast

### Key Insight
The paper positions mentoring as one component within a broader "business services sector" that includes both free and compensated services. This aligns with the ANDE model of support services as an integrated domain rather than isolated programs.

### Service Delivery Model
- **Free services:** Traditional volunteer mentoring, peer groups, community events
- **Compensated services:** Professional coaching, specialized consulting, advisory board facilitation
- Both are needed for a healthy ecosystem

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When analyzing the S domain as a whole, evaluate whether mentoring (S4) is integrated with incubation (S2), acceleration (S1), and physical spaces (S3). Fragmented services score lower than integrated ones. The free vs. compensated distinction is important for sustainability assessment — ecosystems relying entirely on volunteer/free services may lack sustainability. Recommendation: ecosystems scoring low on S4 should consider both establishing formal mentoring programs AND growing professional business services capacity.

---

## Extracto 24E — Mentor Quality Assessment Framework
**Section:** Derived from all sections
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S4

### Composite Quality Framework for Scoring S4 (Mentores)

Based on the paper's content, a comprehensive assessment of ecosystem mentoring quality should evaluate:

| Dimension | Questions to Ask | Scoring Implications |
|---|---|---|
| **Existence** | Are there any structured mentoring programs? | No = score 0-15 on S4 |
| **Delivery diversity** | How many of the 9 delivery methods are available? | 1-2 methods = basic; 3-5 = developing; 6+ = mature |
| **Target coverage** | Which of the 4 entrepreneur groups are served? | 1 group = basic; 2-3 = developing; all 4 = mature |
| **Mentor preparation** | Are mentors trained and managed? | Untrained volunteers = basic; trained + managed = developing; curated + tested = mature |
| **Integration** | Is mentoring connected to other support services? | Standalone = basic; linked to 1-2 services = developing; fully integrated = mature |
| **Sustainability** | What is the funding/sustainability model? | Pure volunteer = fragile; mixed free/compensated = developing; sustainable model = mature |
| **Scale** | How many active mentors relative to population? | Use per-capita adjustment for cities <1M |

### Scoring Guidance for S4

| Score Range | Profile |
|---|---|
| 0-15 | No structured mentoring; occasional ad-hoc connections only |
| 16-30 | 1-2 informal mentoring programs; untrained volunteers; serves 1 target group |
| 31-50 | Formal program(s) with some mentor training; 2-3 delivery methods; serves 2-3 target groups |
| 51-70 | Multiple programs with trained/curated mentors; integrated with incubation/acceleration; 4+ delivery methods |
| 71-100 | Comprehensive mentoring ecosystem with all 4 target groups served; multiple delivery methods; professional quality standards; sustainable model; strong mentor-to-entrepreneur ratio |

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Use this composite framework as a supplement to the rubrica-scoring.md benchmarks for S4. The paper provides the qualitative criteria that pure quantity metrics miss. When reviewing user-submitted S4 data, probe for quality indicators beyond mentor count. In the report's Section 3 (Evaluacion por dominio), reference these quality dimensions to explain S4 scores.

---

## Extracto 24F — Mentoring and Ecosystem Maturity Alignment
**Section:** Cross-reference with ANDE Table 4.3
**ANDE Domain:** S (Servicios de Apoyo) — S4; aligned with Table 4.3 sequencing

### Mentoring Recommendations by Ecosystem Maturity

| Maturity Level | Mentoring Priority | Recommended Actions |
|---|---|---|
| **Naciente (0-25)** | Construir | Start small with volunteer traditional mentoring for aspiring entrepreneurs; create 1-2 peer groups; focus on cultural acceptability of asking for help |
| **Emergente (26-45)** | Fortalecer | Formalize mentoring program; train mentors; add growth counseling for GOEs; create sector-specific matching; integrate with emerging incubators |
| **En Desarrollo (46-65)** | Escalar | Add advisory boards and coaching programs; expand delivery methods to 5+; create mentor management systems; serve all 4 target groups; develop compensated services alongside volunteer |
| **Autosostenible (66-100)** | Mantener | Full mentoring ecosystem with professional standards; mentor certification; international mentor networks; data-driven matching; impact measurement; sustainable funding model |

### Connection to Entrepreneurial Culture
The paper implicitly connects mentoring readiness to cultural factors: "Use mentoring only when the entrepreneur is open to being mentored." This links S4 to C5 (Confianza social) — in low-trust ecosystems, building cultural acceptance of vulnerability and help-seeking must precede formal mentoring programs.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Use this maturity-aligned recommendation table in Section 7 of the report. It ensures mentoring recommendations match the ecosystem's overall maturity level per Table 4.3. The cultural readiness insight is particularly important for Latin American ecosystems where machismo culture may inhibit help-seeking behavior — address this in Section 6 (Retos) and Section 7 (Recomendaciones).



---

## [Ref 25]

# Reference Extracts: Plan for the Creation of an Entrepreneurial Ecosystem for Long-Term Care in LatAm

**Source:** `ref_PlanfortheCreationofanEntrepreneurialEcosystemforLongTermCareinLatinAmericaandTheCaribbean.pdf`
**Title:** Plan for the Creation of an Entrepreneurial Ecosystem for Long-Term Care in Latin America and the Caribbean
**Authors:** Celia de Anca, Newton M. Campos, Concepcion Galdon, Eugenia Gutierrez
**Publisher:** Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) / BID Lab / IE Foundation
**Date:** 2025
**Pages:** 37

**LOW RELEVANCE -- Sector-specific document about long-term care ecosystems.** This report adapts Isenberg (2010) entrepreneurial ecosystem model to the long-term care (elderly dependency) sector in Latin America. While the content is heavily sector-specific (caregiver training, copayment systems, nursing homes), there are a few transferable frameworks for general ANDE ecosystem diagnostics.

---

## 25A. Isenberg Adaptation: Six-Cluster Model for Sector-Specific Ecosystems

The report adapts Isenberg (2010) model into six clusters tailored for a specific sector. The clusters map directly to ANDE domains:

| Report Cluster | ANDE Domain Equivalent |
|---|---|
| Public Policies (regulatory framework, standardized contracts, quality supervision) | P: Politica y Regulacion |
| Entrepreneurial Financing (financing mechanisms, microcredits, insurance, copayment) | F: Financiamiento |
| Entrepreneurial Culture (awareness campaigns, revaluation of caregiving) | C: Cultura |
| Support Structures (incubators, knowledge exchange platforms, innovation centers, events) | S: Servicios de Apoyo |
| Human Capital Development (caregiver training, skill certification, cooperative leadership training) | H: Capital Humano |
| Access to Markets (digital portals, public-sector outsourcing, large company collaboration) | M: Mercados |

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This confirms that Isenberg framework maps reasonably well to the ANDE structure. The methodology of adapting a general framework through focus groups (5 countries, 36 professionals) and validation interviews is a sound template for sector-specific ecosystem diagnostics.

---

## 25B. Five-Step Sequencing for Ecosystem Development

The report proposes a five-step plan ordered by implementation difficulty and impact:

1. **Diagnostic Actions** -- Map demand and supply. Municipality-level mapping recommended due to proximity to citizens.
2. **Key Opportunities** (low-hanging fruit) -- Quick-win actions: regulatory framework, standardized contracts, training programs, digital portals connecting supply/demand, quality certification.
3. **Strategic Investments** -- Higher-investment, longer-term: public/collaborative financing, microcredit, incubators/accelerators, innovation hubs, public-sector outsourcing.
4. **Support Actions** -- Foundational but not immediately impactful: awareness campaigns, professional liability insurance, events (hackathons, fairs).
5. **Consolidation Actions** -- Long-term reinforcement: large-small company collaboration, comprehensive well-being initiatives.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This sequencing parallels ANDE Tabla 4.3 (interventions by maturity level). The report explicitly states ecosystem elements do not function in isolation but continuously interact and reinforce one another -- consistent with the Condition-Result pattern analysis.

---

## 25C. LatAm-Specific Ecosystem Barriers (from Focus Groups)

From five focus groups (Spain, Uruguay, Costa Rica, Colombia, Brazil) with 36 sector professionals, barriers broadly applicable beyond the care sector:

- **Regulatory delays** block market entry (one-year patent/certification waits reported in Uruguay).
- **Bureaucratic fragmentation** forces navigation of multiple agencies (one-stop shop desired).
- **Cultural undervaluation** of certain sectors inhibits entrepreneurial interest and talent attraction.
- **Financing gaps** are the most-cited barrier, especially for infrastructure-intensive businesses.
- **Informal support networks** (post-event conversations at accelerators) generate synergies formal programs cannot replicate.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Check for: (1) time-to-market regulatory bottlenecks beyond incorporation time; (2) one-stop-shop integration for regulatory processes; (3) cultural perception of dominant local sectors; (4) informal networking density alongside formal programs.

---

## 25D. Actors Mapping Template

Key actor types per cluster, applicable as a general template:

| Cluster | Actor Types |
|---|---|
| Public Policies | Municipality, Ministry of Social Services, civic boards, regional government |
| Financing | Private investors (VC, angels), public funders, grant/loan entities, insurance companies |
| Culture | Journalists, influencers, entertainment industry |
| Support Structures | Integration facilitators, entrepreneur association groups |
| Human Capital | Training schools (public/private), professional cooperatives |
| Markets | Large companies, digital platform creators |

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Include non-obvious actors: journalists/influencers in Culture, insurance companies in Finance, professional cooperatives in Human Capital. In LatAm, cooperatives are often overlooked but can be significant in organizing informal-sector talent.



---

## [Ref 26]

# Reference Extracts: Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Building in Rural America -- Four Decades of Learning

**Source:** `ref_eEcosystems_Four_Decades_Final.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurial Ecosystem Building in Rural America: Four Decades of Learning
**Author:** Don Macke and e2 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (by NetWork Kansas)
**Date:** June 2020
**Pages:** 29

Practitioner paper synthesizing 40 years of field experience in building entrepreneurial ecosystems across rural America. Primary value for ANDE diagnostics: evolution of ecosystem thinking from programs to systems, three-level maturity model, culture-building progression, top-down/bottom-up strategy design, and growth-oriented entrepreneur segmentation. Key reference for Culture domain.

---

## 26A. Evolution from Programs to Systems: Four Decades of Ecosystem Thinking

Macke traces the conceptual evolution through three periods:

**Early Period (1970s-1990s):**
- First pioneers (Redwood Coast CA, Upper Peninsula MI, Coastal Enterprises ME, Kentucky Highlands) began weaving individual programs into comprehensive support systems.
- Brian Dabson and William Schweke (Corporation for Enterprise Development) coined "Entrepreneurial Development Systems" (EDS).
- Tom Lyons and Greg Lichtenstein created the "Entrepreneurial League System" -- a baseball-analogy framework differentiating support by venture stage (semi-pro for startups, majors for high-growth).
- Key insight: early pioneers differentiated by entrepreneurial development stage, recognizing that a startup had different needs than an established venture pursuing growth.

**Mid Period (late 1990s-2010s):**
- Chris Gibbons and Economic Gardening movement (1987, Littleton CO): focused on providing advanced market intelligence to local entrepreneurs rather than recruiting external firms. Spawned national and international movement.
- Ernesto Sirolli and Enterprise Facilitation: fundamental shift from resource-focused programs to focusing on needs of entrepreneurs and networking them to resources. This shift is now foundational to current ecosystem building.
- SourceLink (UMKC, 2003): resource mapping and navigation infrastructure adopted across the US.

**Contemporary Period (2010s-2020):**
- Victor Hwang and Greg Horowitt, The Rain Forest: fully captured the ecosystem concept.
- Kauffman Foundation ESHIP Summits (2017-2020) re-energized national movement focused on community-centered ecosystem building.
- SourceLink and NetWork Kansas as longest-running statewide ecosystem infrastructure.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When assessing an ecosystem, determine where it sits on this evolution. Many LatAm ecosystems are in the "early period" equivalent -- running individual programs (an incubator here, a fund there) without systemic integration. The transition from programs to systems is the critical leap that ANDE diagnostics should identify and recommend.

---

## 26B. Three Levels of Ecosystem Maturity

Macke defines three levels of ecosystem development. This is directly applicable as an alternative/complement to the ANDE four-level maturity scale (Naciente, Emergente, En Desarrollo, Autosostenible):

**Level 1 -- Start-Up:**
- Sufficient but conditional community support for entrepreneurship
- Early stakeholder commitments
- Host organization identified
- Some funding mobilized (at least 18-24 month commitment)
- Emergent entrepreneur outreach, intake, and networking to resources
- An emergent entrepreneurial resources network
- Part-time entrepreneurial coaching launched
- Key organizing and assessment work underway

**Level 2 -- Maturing:**
- Deeper community support, expanded stakeholder commitments
- More robust organization, 3-5 years of funding secured
- Routinized, regular entrepreneur outreach/intake/networking
- Expanding resource network with gaps being filled
- Full-time entrepreneur coaching with expanding volunteer team
- Entrepreneurship rooting as foundational development strategy
- Well-documented impact metrics and growing success stories

**Level 3 -- Complex/Sophisticated:**
- Entrepreneurship embedded in stakeholder organization missions (part of community culture)
- Sustainable and increasing funding strategy
- Comprehensive, targeted, robust entrepreneur outreach/intake/networking
- Well-established and functioning resource network with few gaps
- Multiple entrepreneur portfolios focusing on different talent segments
- Strong ROI metrics, increasing impact stories
- An entrepreneurial community is evolving

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These levels describe the organizational infrastructure of ecosystem building, not just the ecosystem outputs. ANDE scores measure domain outcomes (how many VCs, how many startups), but Macke measures the system-building capacity itself. A city could score moderately on ANDE indicators while still being at Level 1 in terms of ecosystem coordination. Recommend using this framework to assess the backbone organization capacity in Section 5 (Analisis de Dominios).

---

## 26C. Culture Progression: Ecosystem to Culture to Community

The paper articulates a three-stage cultural deepening:

1. **Entrepreneurial Ecosystem** -- resources and support structures exist
2. **Entrepreneurial Culture** -- entrepreneurial behavior manifests across schools, healthcare, government, nonprofits, and residents throughout the community
3. **Entrepreneurial Community** -- entrepreneurship becomes a way of living and working; the culture is resilient and self-reinforcing

Key insight: "Once a community entrepreneurial culture is rooted and strong, it becomes resilient and higher performing. Entrepreneurial communities become a way of living and working."

**Application to ANDE diagnostics (Culture domain):** This progression provides a framework for qualitative assessment of Culture beyond counting meetups and events. When scoring C1-C5, consider whether the ecosystem has achieved any cultural embedding beyond the startup community -- does entrepreneurial behavior appear in schools, government, nonprofits? That signals progression from ecosystem to culture to community.

---

## 26D. Top-Down and Bottom-Up Strategy Design

Macke argues that optimal ecosystem building requires both strategies operating simultaneously:

**Top-Down (Regional/State Level) -- 5 Core Elements:**
1. Host and Coordinating Organization -- preferably independent (not government or university, which face leadership/priority changes). Mission: ensure both statewide and community-level ecosystem building occurs.
2. Entrepreneur Builders Advocacy Group -- civic-minded entrepreneurs and committed stakeholders who provide guidance, accountability, and defend against disinvestment when leadership changes.
3. Robust, Predictable and Sustainable Funding -- like any venture, undercapitalization leads to failure.
4. SourceLink-type navigation infrastructure -- resource mapping and entrepreneur-to-resource matching.
5. Entrepreneurial Indexing System -- shared metrics across local, regional, and state levels.

**Bottom-Up (Community Level) -- Basic System:**
- Identifying, engaging, and assisting area entrepreneurs directly
- Building local resource networks informed by actual entrepreneur needs
- Letting community needs drive development of the resource network (not building supply-side-first)

**Synergy:** Communities actively engaged in entrepreneur development drive market and resource signals to regional resources. With multiple communities engaged, the right resource matches the right entrepreneur at dramatically higher rates. The partnership between communities and resources creates more and better matches.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When diagnosing, assess both dimensions. A city may have top-down programs (federal startup programs, state innovation funds) but weak bottom-up community engagement, or vice versa. The most effective ecosystems have both. The ANDE "Servicios de Apoyo" domain captures the bottom-up, while "Politica y Regulacion" captures some top-down, but the coordination layer between them is often the missing piece. Flag this in Section 5 analysis.

---

## 26E. Five Types of Entrepreneurs and the Growth-Oriented Sweet Spot

Macke segments entrepreneurs into five types, arguing that ecosystem builders should differentiate their approach:

1. **Essential entrepreneurs** -- grocery stores, clinics, hardware stores. Communities need them for basic functioning.
2. **Amenity entrepreneurs** -- cafes, bars, recreational facilities. They improve quality of life and talent retention.
3. **Growth-oriented entrepreneurs** -- ventures with growth potential but not yet achieving it. Up to 5% of all local ventures. The sweet spot for development efforts.
4. **Growth entrepreneurs** -- actively growing ventures (10-99 employees). About 3% of all ventures nationally (Gallup estimate).
5. **Breakout growth entrepreneurs** -- achieving extraordinary scale. About 0.25% of all ventures (Gallup estimate).

Key insight: Growth-oriented entrepreneurs are the sweet spot because (a) there are more of them than growth/breakout entrepreneurs, (b) they create transformative economic change when supported, and (c) they are at a stage where ecosystem support can make the most difference. They create small- and medium-sized rooted corporations that contribute to the base economy, provide jobs/careers, and provide leadership, charity, and identity for communities.

**Top 5 resource needs for growth entrepreneurs:** (1) Access to capital (angel to commercial credit), (2) Market research and analysis (Economic Gardening-type advanced market intelligence), (3) Growth planning and coaching, (4) Peer groups, (5) Human talent acquisition.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The ANDE methodology counts startups (M1) but does not explicitly segment by growth orientation. When interpreting M1, assess whether the counted startups include growth-oriented ventures or are primarily subsistence/lifestyle businesses. A city with 200 startups that are all lifestyle businesses has a different ecosystem profile than one with 50 startups, 10 of which are growth-oriented. Recommend adding a qualitative note on growth-orientation density in the Mercados domain analysis.

---

## 26F. The Basic Transaction: Entrepreneur-Centric Ecosystem Building

Macke distills ecosystem building to a core transaction:

**Entrepreneurs with needs + Resources that address needs + Connection between them = Deal flow**

Critical insight: Building the perfect supply-side ecosystem (resources, programs, financing) will underperform without a robust and sustained strategy to identify, engage, and assist entrepreneurs. The most important element of an ecosystem is its entrepreneurs, not its support infrastructure.

This is the "ground game" -- letting your community needs drive development of your resource network. Start by discovering entrepreneurial talent, engaging with them to identify potential areas of assistance, then networking entrepreneurs to resources.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This challenges the common LatAm approach of building incubators/accelerators first and waiting for entrepreneurs to come. When diagnosing Servicios de Apoyo (S1-S5), assess not just whether support organizations exist, but whether they actively identify, reach out to, and engage entrepreneurs. An accelerator with 2 cohorts but no outreach strategy is different from one that actively scouts growth-oriented ventures.

---

## 26G. NetWork Kansas Case Study: Statewide Ecosystem at Scale

NetWork Kansas provides the longest-running example of a statewide ecosystem. Key metrics and design elements:

- Nearly USD 169 million in cumulative deal flow across primarily rural Kansas (as of February 2020)
- Reach by community size: 13% of deal flow to communities under 1,000 residents, 37% to 1,000-5,000, proving small-community reach is possible
- Dual top-down/bottom-up structure: centralized resource coordination + decentralized eCommunity network
- Special initiatives possible because of statewide infrastructure (e.g., health ventures fund with Kansas Health Foundation)
- Youth entrepreneurship competition leveraging decentralized community network

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** NetWork Kansas demonstrates that ecosystem building can achieve scale even in low-density geographies. For LatAm ecosystems in smaller cities (under 500K), the NetWork Kansas model suggests that the relevant strategy is not to build all resources locally but to connect to regional/national networks while building strong local entrepreneur identification and engagement. This is particularly relevant for the Servicios de Apoyo domain in smaller ecosystems.

---

## 26H. Community Prosperity Framework: Entrepreneurship as Foundation

Macke places entrepreneurial ecosystem building within a broader community development model:

Smart investments in entrepreneurial talent and behavior produce:
1. **Increased Economic Competitiveness** -- more competitive ventures make a more competitive community
2. **Expanding Economic Diversification** -- entrepreneurs pursuing competitive advantages inherently create diversified economies
3. **Greater Value-Added Products and Services** -- escaping the "commodity trap" (agriculture, extractive industries) through niche market development
4. **Increased Resiliency** -- diverse, value-added economies weather shocks better

The Entrepreneurship-Prosperity-Philanthropy Connection: entrepreneurship is the greatest pathway to personal wealth; rooted local entrepreneurs have strong hometown attachment; connecting philanthropy through community foundations with entrepreneurial ecosystem building creates a self-reinforcing cycle.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** This framework is useful for Section 6 (Fortalezas y Retos). When identifying strengths, look for the prosperity cycle: successful entrepreneurs reinvesting in the ecosystem through mentorship, angel investment, or philanthropy. When identifying retos, look for the commodity trap -- ecosystems dominated by commodity production (agriculture, mining) without value-added entrepreneurial activity.



---

## [Ref 27]

# Reference Extracts: GIZ Guide on Strengthening Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (2021)

**Source:** `ref_giz2021-en-entrepreneurial-ecosystems-guide.pdf`
**Title:** Connect & Create: Strengthening Entrepreneurial Ecosystems -- An Interactive Guide for Development Professionals
**Authors:** Fabian-Carlos Guhl, Mike Ducker, Morten Seja (J.E. Austin Associates / JAA)
**Year:** 2020 (published December 2020, diffused 2021)
**Publisher:** Deutsche Gesellschaft fur Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH, on behalf of German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development
**Pages:** 124

Practitioner-oriented guide for development professionals who have already completed ecosystem mapping. Covers intervention design at three levels: culture, interacting players, and business environment. Supported by 12+ GIZ project case studies from developing economies and 13 non-GIZ programme interviews.

---

## 27A. Three-Level Ecosystem Framework (GIZ Model)

GIZ organizes entrepreneurial ecosystems into three nested levels:

**Level 1 -- Entrepreneurial Culture:** Prevailing norms that manifest in entrepreneurial attitudes; provides social and professional rewards for innovation and risk-taking, or disincentives in cultures that discourage entrepreneurship.

**Level 2 -- Interacting Players:** Roles of entrepreneurs, ESOs, financial players, universities, government enterprise agencies, mentors, and other support organizations. Covers both services provided and collaboration dynamics.

**Level 3 -- Business Environment:** Policies, regulations, and the legal and institutional environment in support of entrepreneurship.

**Mapping to ANDE domains:**
- Level 1 maps to: Culture (C)
- Level 2 maps to: Support Services (S), Finance (F), Human Capital (H), Markets (M)
- Level 3 maps to: Policy & Regulation (P), partially R&D/Innovation (I)

**Diagnostic use:** When an ecosystem scores low on Culture, interventions should start at Level 1. Low scores on Support Services, Finance, or Markets point to Level 2. Low scores on Policy point to Level 3. The framework implies a sequencing: culture underpins everything; players must exist before policies can be optimized.

---

## 27B. Ecosystem Evolution Phases (Startup Genome via GIZ)

Ecosystems evolve through typical phases, each with characteristic activities:

1. **Activation:** Small group of entrepreneurs and companies focused on a common purpose; initial interactions form.
2. **Integration:** External organizations join; commercial opportunities attract financial organizations; ESOs appear to support entrepreneurs.
3. **Attraction:** Specialization leads to globally competitive clusters; ecosystem attracts global talent, investment, and partnerships.
4. **Globalization:** International appeal creates a self-reinforcing cycle of success.

**Key principle:** Ecosystems evolve from within; they cannot be established from the outside. Entrepreneurs are the heart; their interactions are the blood and nerves. Anyone wishing to strengthen an ecosystem must understand this.

**Diagnostic use:** Helps determine ecosystem maturity level. Nascent/Emergent ecosystems are in Activation-Integration. "En Desarrollo" corresponds roughly to Attraction. This maps to the ANDE maturity scale and informs which interventions are appropriate per Table 4.3.

---

## 27C. Ecosystem Strengthening vs. Direct Entrepreneur Support

| Dimension | Ecosystem Strengthening | Direct Entrepreneur Support |
|-----------|------------------------|----------------------------|
| Effectiveness | Long-term; entrepreneurs benefit from interactions and knowledge flows in the system | Faster impacts at entrepreneur level |
| Scope | Supporting local ESOs creates multiplier effects | Limited in reach |
| Ownership | Higher ownership by local players leads to greater sustainability | Higher control over impact measurement |
| Monitoring | More difficult to measure | Easier to collect data on results |
| Best for | Established communities with existing players | Proof of concept; no ecosystem exists yet; pilot testing |

**Diagnostic use:** When recommending interventions for an ANDE diagnostic, this distinction matters. Nascent ecosystems (0-25) may need direct entrepreneur support first to create demonstration effects. Emergent and above should prioritize ecosystem-level interventions through ESOs and intermediaries.

---

## 27D. ESO Types and Their Characteristics

| ESO Type | Programming | Target Segment | Typical Form |
|----------|------------|----------------|--------------|
| Accelerator | Cohort-based; peer learning, mentoring, coaching, co-working. Some offer training/financing. | Early-stage startups, disruptive tech, ventures ready to raise | Almost every form |
| Incubator | Shared assets (office, wi-fi, equipment); back-office services; networking; mentorship. Entrepreneurs stay years, not months. | Established SMEs, startups | Government/university-supported or private |
| Competition | Short programs (hours to one week); competition format creates urgency. No physical location; low cost structure; rely on volunteers. | Any entrepreneur type | Universities, private companies, NGOs |
| Training Program | Focused on training and domain knowledge transfer. Narrow topic (e.g., sales/marketing). Domain-specific coaches. | Any entrepreneur type | Recurring events by all organization types |
| Mentoring/Peer Network | Leverages interaction and relationship between people. Peer networking (business associations) or structured mentor programs. | SMEs, high-growth companies, established business | Business associations, NGOs |

**Key findings on ESO impact (from GALI/AfriLabs research):**
1. Many ESO professionals are not entrepreneurs and have limited prior experience supporting entrepreneurs. They should focus on facilitating experts rather than providing training themselves.
2. Generalized ESO training courses and workshops have little impact on entrepreneurs and sometimes shift their focus away from real business challenges. ESOs should provide tailored support.
3. While many ESOs focus on early-stage ventures, few programs focus on later-stage/growth-stage companies, inhibiting scaling.

**Diagnostic use:** When scoring S1 (Aceleradoras) and S2 (Incubadoras), differentiate between active programs with documented cohorts vs. organizations that exist in name only. The GALI findings support recommending quality improvements for existing ESOs, not just counting them.

---

## 27E. ESO Financial Sustainability -- Four Revenue Sources

ESOs fund operations through four income channels:

1. **Income through services:** Space rental, training fees, co-working space fees.
2. **Investment in success:** Equity stakes, royalties, acceleration fees, seed funding returns.
3. **Grants and donations:** Government grants, donor funding, private grants, sponsorships, CSR.
4. **Commercial activities:** Sales/market access services, consulting/project management, technology commercialization.

**Design principle:** Most ESOs need multiple income sources to ensure long-term sustainability. Dependence on a single funding source creates fragility.

**When development programs fund ESOs, risk mitigation requires:**
- Challenge grant competitions (small grants first, then larger funding for successful ones)
- Supporting ESOs in developing viable business models
- Requiring matching funding from the ESO
- Specifying exit plans in funding agreements (growing percentage of resources from non-donor sources)

**Diagnostic use:** When assessing Support Services quality, investigate whether local ESOs have diversified income. Ecosystems where ESOs depend entirely on donor or government grants are fragile; recommend pathways to financial sustainability.

---

## 27F. Financial Ecosystem Gap and Intervention Architecture

**The problem:** A $3.3 trillion financing gap exists for entrepreneurs in emerging markets (World Bank "Missing Middles" report), with an early-stage capital gap between roughly EUR 15,000 and EUR 2 million. This gap is even more significant in developing countries.

**Demand-side deficiencies:** Many entrepreneurs in developing countries lack proper accounting controls, cannot create financial plans, and are not aggressive enough with growth strategies to appeal to financial institutions.

**Intervention architecture -- three pillars:**

| Pillar | Actions |
|--------|---------|
| Increase supply of finance | Capacity building for financial institutions; grant funding to entrepreneurs; matching capital to investment funds |
| Matchmaking | Convenings (large networking events); curated events with targeted speakers; learning journeys for investors; pitch/demo days; B2B rotations; Dragons Den formats; technology platforms; non-commercial and commercial brokers |
| Improve entrepreneur readiness | Capacity building for entrepreneurs; part-time CFO services; investment readiness training; financial strategy development |
| Cross-cutting | Improve financial regulations and infrastructure |

**On matching capital to investment funds:** Development partners can co-invest alongside other limited partners into early-stage funds in developing countries, catalyzing new fund formation. Example: Dutch Good Growth Fund (DGGF) provides matching capital to early-stage investment funds.

**Diagnostic use:** When Finance (F) is the bottleneck domain, recommendations should address all three pillars simultaneously. Merely counting VCs (F3) or angel investors (F4) is insufficient; the readiness of entrepreneurs to absorb capital and the matchmaking infrastructure both matter. For Nascent ecosystems, Table 4.3 says "Esperar" on Finance -- consistent with GIZ's finding that first you need ESOs and culture before finance can flow.

---

## 27G. Market Access -- Types of Market Players

Markets provide both revenue (financial sustainability without external financing) and critical information about customer needs.

**Three customer categories:**
1. **Retail consumers:** Costly and challenging for entrepreneurs unless they operate online marketplaces.
2. **Corporate buyers:** Provide consistent, long-term revenue; some have procurement and supply chain experts that can improve entrepreneur operations.
3. **Government buyers:** One of the most effective interventions -- governments can buy from entrepreneurs. US SBA example: specific percentage quotas for small business procurement.

**Market-enabling players:**
- Online marketplaces (e.g., Alibaba) -- increasingly important post-COVID
- Soft-landing sites and events -- virtual global offices in foreign ESOs; entrepreneurs test global markets before setting up commercial presence
- Local and international trade shows
- Standards and certification organizations -- regulatory authority for export certification
- Value chain intermediaries -- freight forwarders, exporters

**Key statistic:** 76% of the 2020 Inc. 5000 (America's fastest-growing private companies) did not receive any external investment, relying instead on revenue growth.

**Diagnostic use:** When scoring Markets (M), consider whether the ecosystem has functioning market access infrastructure (trade shows, certification bodies, online marketplace adoption) beyond just counting startups (M1) and corporates (M2). Government procurement for startups is a policy intervention that bridges P and M domains.

---

## 27H. Culture Interventions -- Alignment Framework

Cultural norms influence how individuals perceive risk and react to potential opportunities. Culture can either inhibit or empower entrepreneurship. Culture does not equate with destiny, but it is deeply influential.

**Cultural factors affecting ecosystems (from University of Belgrade / GEM data):**
- Strong negative correlation between "power distance" (degree of unequal power distribution) and entrepreneurial orientation.
- Societies with less hierarchy, more individualism, and that embrace uncertainty are more entrepreneurially oriented.

**Intervention toolkit for cultural alignment:**

| Intervention | Description | When to use |
|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| Storytelling | Stories of entrepreneurs building businesses around community purpose; stories of mentors and corporate supporters | Influencing culture to accept entrepreneurs; attracting supporters |
| Pilot programming | Small, tightly focused pilot with few entrepreneurs and narrow objectives. Must be successful and communicated. | Building a community when programme objectives don't match existing community purposes |
| External stakeholder facilitation | Bringing external community leaders into events, programmes, or strategy sessions | Creating alignment between programme and community |
| Communicating success | Using digital and traditional media channels (radio, TV, social media influencers) | After developing success stories and achieving initial alignment |

**The Isenberg/Babson Scale-Up Model (Manizales, Colombia case):**
The Scale-Up programme focuses solely on sales growth of small ventures regardless of sector or technology. Process: convince one funder to finance a pilot > run small cohort > develop success stories > communicate through local media > create buzz and trust > attract more stakeholders > establish local institution > scale.

**Diagnostic use:** When Culture (C) scores low, recommendations should follow this sequence: identify community purposes through mapping, then use storytelling and pilot programming, then communicate success. Avoid recommending large culture programmes without first understanding what communities already exist and what motivates local entrepreneurs.

---

## 27I. Gender Bias -- Barriers and Interventions for Female Entrepreneurs

**Key gender barriers (from GEM and World Bank):**
1. Regulations prevent women from owning property or signing contracts, limiting bank loan access.
2. In many economies, women have fewer job opportunities, pushing them into necessity-driven entrepreneurship.
3. Limited job opportunities in male-dominated industries restrict women's experience and skills.
4. Inadequate education levels mean insufficient business skills.
5. Cultural expectation that women care for households eliminates time for business.
6. Female entrepreneurs face social pressure to share resources, limiting business investment.

**Interventions to reduce gender bias:**
- Address legal discrimination preventing women from owning capital
- Increase education opportunities
- Increase the size of women's business networks
- Establish women-friendly training that strengthens technical skills and self-confidence
- Provide accessible child care
- Encourage men to support female entrepreneurs
- Create opportunities for women in male-dominated industries

**Diagnostic use:** Gender considerations should appear in the Culture domain analysis (C5 Confianza social) and cross-cut Support Services (S) and Policy (P). If the ecosystem has visible gender gaps, specific recommendations should address both legal barriers (P domain) and cultural/network barriers (C and S domains).

---

## 27J. Mentoring -- Design Principles and Impact Evidence

**Impact evidence:**
- Randomized controlled trial in Togo with female entrepreneurs: traditional business training group showed NO income increase over control group. Coaching/mentoring group (focused on personal initiative, pro-activity, future-oriented mindset) showed **40% increase in profits**.
- GALI finding: emerging market accelerators often have difficulty recruiting mentors.
- GIZ India Her&Now project: 91% of entrepreneurs reported that interactions with counsellors, peers, and mentors increased their self-confidence.

**Mentor types:**
| Type | Focus | Duration | Payment |
|------|-------|----------|---------|
| Business Mentor | Entrepreneur's growth vis-a-vis business; wide-ranging issues that morph over time | Months to years | Usually free |
| Business Adviser | Very specific, pre-defined issues per commercial contract | Task-based | Paid |
| Business Coach | Specific skills and expertise; ends when skill is mastered | Skill-based | May be paid |
| Peer Supporter | Everyday issues at same growth level; peer learning and pressure | Ongoing | Free |

**Mentor programme design principles:**
1. **Recruitment:** Entrepreneurs want mentors who have walked the same path. Mentors should be driven by purpose, not self-interest. They should be empathetic listeners who ask tough questions.
2. **Preparation:** Provide orientation sessions for both parties on roles and responsibilities.
3. **Matching:** Criteria include alignment of values/interests, expectations/goals, time commitment, balanced experience gap (mentor should be a few steps ahead but not too far), balanced egos. Self-selection works better than automated matching.
4. **Facilitation:** Lead through series of events; agree on small number of specific goals; each session ends with agreed goals for next meeting; ESO checks in with both parties; personality clashes require reassignment.

**Warning:** Matching based on industry expertise can create mistrust (they may see each other as competitors). Personality-driven matching is rated higher by entrepreneurs.

**Diagnostic use:** When scoring S4 (Mentores), look beyond count. Existence of structured mentor programmes with proper matching and facilitation matters more than raw numbers. Recommendations for mentoring should include recruitment, training, and structured programme design -- not just "increase mentors."

---

## 27K. Business Environment Reform -- Regulatory and Policy Checklist

**Regulatory and administrative framework reforms (Level 3):**
- Business registration: simple, quick, low-cost, ideally online
- Simplify business closure and bankruptcy procedures
- Avoid unnecessary certifications and license requirements
- Simplify tax procedures; consider progressivity of corporate income taxes
- Pilot regulatory sandboxes and share lessons learned

**Policy and legal framework reforms:**
- Avoid policies that favour incumbent businesses over new entrants
- Develop and maintain fair, consistent IP protection
- Open public procurement to SMEs and startups (quotas, innovation procurement, lower bidding thresholds)
- Allow land and movable assets as loan collateral; develop up-to-date credit registry
- Government de-risking of investments in startups/SMEs when banks stay away
- Attract global sources of finance; protect international investors investing in local firms
- Eliminate discrimination in labour laws
- Facilitate access to global talent
- Allow researchers from public institutions to also work in the private sector
- Incentivize university-private sector collaboration and technology commercialization
- Provide challenge grants for innovation
- Use policy hackathons for public policy challenges
- Periodically analyze and communicate entrepreneurship indicators to policymakers

**Diagnostic use:** When scoring P1 (Dias para constituir empresa), P2 (Regimen fiscal startups), and P3 (Programas publicos), this checklist provides the detailed reform areas to assess. Recommendations in Section 7 for Policy domain should reference specific items from this list. The regulatory sandbox concept is especially relevant for ecosystems with emerging tech sectors.

---

## 27L. Startup Acts -- Legislative Innovation for Ecosystem Building

Startup Acts are laws that:
1. Define what a "startup" is in the given economy
2. Describe how startups will be treated vis-a-vis tax and regulatory frameworks (including preferential treatment)
3. Reform specific laws regarding company entry/exit to stimulate new company creation
4. Coordinate with overall economic development strategy and sector priorities
5. Provide specific public support mechanisms (specialized funding, training, mentoring, salary subsidies, expedited patent registration)

**Existing examples:** Italy (2012, first Startup Act), Argentina, India, Tunisia, Senegal, Mali. Rwanda was drafting one as of 2020 with participatory process involving ecosystem players.

**Common incentives:** Tax incentives for startups and/or their investors; exemptions from corporate income tax; tax incentives for specific activities like innovation. Tax incentives are less controversial than direct financial support and create less market distortion.

**Signal effect:** Startup Acts signal to entrepreneurs and investors that the government recognizes entrepreneurship importance and will incentivize it. This intangible signal is especially valuable in countries where broad reform has proven difficult.

**Diagnostic use:** Relevant for P2 (Regimen fiscal startups) and P3 (Programas publicos). If an ecosystem lacks a Startup Act or equivalent preferential treatment, this is a concrete recommendation. The Tunisia and Senegal cases are directly relevant for Latin American ecosystems at similar maturity levels.

---

## 27M. Collaboration Design -- Five Principles for Ecosystem Players

From analysis of multiple GIZ projects (Jordan J-CORE, Vietnam SwissEP, Mexico/Senegal/Ghana Innovation Factory):

1. **Start with data and analysis** -- Ecosystem map providing comprehensive overview of all players and identifying collaboration opportunities.
2. **Ensure participation of community champions and minority groups** -- Include diverse voices, not just dominant players.
3. **Give stakeholders freedom to shape the conversation, objectives and agenda** -- Don't impose an external agenda.
4. **Provide global examples** of how other ecosystem players have collaborated on common problems -- Inspire through international best practice.
5. **Support the journey, not a destination** -- Collaboration is a process of building trust and finding common purpose. Start small. Small steps create trust, build relationships, and bring in other players.

**Jordan J-CORE case:** GIZ organized informal monthly roundtable among 9 ecosystem players. Initial meetings focused on learning about the ecosystem (GIZ provided mapping). Then international best practices on angel investing and acceleration were presented. Stakeholders discovered shared concern about weak investable pipeline, started collaborating on an investment guide. A task force on legal reforms began comparing Startup Acts worldwide. This momentum attracted government interest, leading to regular public-private dialogue.

**Diagnostic use:** When recommending interventions, specific collaboration structures (roundtables, working groups, task forces) are more actionable than generic "improve collaboration." The J-CORE model is directly applicable to Latin American ecosystems at Emergent-to-En-Desarrollo stage.

---

## 27N. Programme Design Principles for Ecosystem Interventions

**Three reasons ecosystem programmes must be more flexible than traditional development:**
1. Ecosystems are unique -- copying Silicon Valley fails; leverage local purpose and strengths
2. Ecosystem strengthening = building relationships -- not a straightforward or easily planned process
3. Ecosystems are dynamic -- constant stream of new and departing players; programmes must adapt

**Recommended design cycle:**
Ecosystem Mapping > Find Leverage Points > Find Initial Collaborative Partners > Verification and Input from Players > Draw Out Pilot Design > Pilot Program > Results: Scale or Learn and Re-design

**Key principle:** Act like a startup. Make sure you offer services the marketplace wants. Start with short pilot activities, closely monitor results. Pilots that create impact generate momentum and motivate other stakeholders. After review, scale successful pilots or adjust based on feedback.

**Programme team composition:** Hire staff who have experience in the specific ecosystem being strengthened. People who have worked for ESOs or were entrepreneurs themselves, who have recruited entrepreneurs and mentors, or who are facilitators and communicators. Past relationships can create biases -- guard against with clear partner selection criteria and transparent processes.

**Diagnostic use:** When Section 7 (Recomendaciones) proposes interventions, they should follow this principle: start with pilots, not large programmes. Recommendations should specify who the pilot partners would be, what success looks like, and what happens if results differ from expectations. This "startup approach" to ecosystem building applies especially to Naciente and Emergente ecosystems.

---

## 27O. Ecosystem Indicators Framework (GIZ -- Three Levels)

### Level 1: Culture and Motivation
| Area | Example Indicators | Reference Tools |
|------|--------------------|-----------------|
| Culture | Perception of entrepreneurship by adults; share of adults wanting to be entrepreneurs; number of entrepreneurship events | GEM, Forward Cities, Techstars Maturity Model |
| Motivations & Performance | Market opportunities perceived by entrepreneurs; targeted vs. actual growth; percentage of high-growth firms | GEDI, GEM |
| Inclusiveness | % women/minority founders; financial results of women/minority founders; number of women/minority investors or ESO leaders; funding to women-led businesses | Forward Cities |

### Level 2: Interacting Players
| Area | Example Indicators | Reference Tools |
|------|--------------------|-----------------|
| Better services | Number of ESOs, programmes, incubators, co-working spaces; startups supported; survival rate of ESO-supported startups; entrepreneurs confirm service improvement | GALI, StartupBlink, Forward Cities |
| Cooperation | Number of players perceiving cooperation improvement; players in international exchange; corporate-startup partnerships; matchmaking events; cross-ecosystem connections | CSSC initiative, GEDI, Startup Genome |
| Mentors/coaches | Degree of engagement; number of hours of meetings | Endeavor Insights, Startup Genome |
| Access to finance | Volume of all financing over period; number of angel/VC deals; growth in financing value; new active investors; new financial products | Startup Genome, StartupBlink, GEDI |
| Access to talent | % people with high-level degrees; % female-owned businesses | Startup Genome, Forward Cities |

### Level 3: Business Environment
| Area | Example Indicators | Reference Tools |
|------|--------------------|-----------------|
| Startup policy | New entrepreneurship strategies/policies developed with ecosystem players; startup policy reforms | i4Policy |
| Business environment | Data policy protection; good competition law; days to start a business | World Bank Doing Business, Startup National Scoreboard, Startup Genome |

**Systemic change criteria (from DCED):**
- Scale: Changes influence/benefit many people beyond direct intervention
- Sustainability: Changes continue past programme end without external assistance
- Resilience: Players can adapt models to continue delivering impact as environment changes

**Diagnostic use:** This indicator framework complements the 30 ANDE indicators. When data is missing for an ANDE indicator, these reference tools can provide proxy measurements. The systemic change criteria (scale, sustainability, resilience) should inform Section 6 (Retos) and Section 7 (Recomendaciones) -- recommendations should aim for interventions that are scalable, sustainable, and resilient.

---

## 27P. Access to Finance -- Capacity Building by Financial Institution Type

| Financial Institution | Capacity-Building Intervention | Example |
|-----------------------|-------------------------------|---------|
| Angel Investors | Mobilize experienced angels to develop and deliver early-stage investing training for new potential angels | Cairo Angels (GIZ Egypt) |
| Foundations & Corporations | Develop training material for foundations/corporations wanting to invest in change-making entrepreneurs | IFC Impact Finance Center |
| VC / Impact / PE | Technical assistance to new fund managers on investment thesis, fundraising, pipeline building, deal structuring, portfolio management, exit strategy | African Development Bank "Boost Africa" |
| Crowdfunding | Analyze potential impacts; share information on how crowdfunding works in developing countries | World Bank crowdfunding research |
| Public/Semi-public Funds | Technical assistance for government fund strategy development; structure procurement for selecting local fund manager | UNDP in Ethiopia |
| Banks | Insights on new financial products; leveraging technology for risk assessment | USAID Egyptian Banking Institute accreditation |

**Egypt angel investment case (GIZ):**
1. Work with local business champions to motivate wealthy individuals to invest in entrepreneurs
2. Work with experienced Egyptian angels to develop an Angel Investment Boot Camp
3. Support local ESOs to expand pipeline of investable companies
4. Support Cairo Angels to create investor syndicate with other MENA investors

**Diagnostic use:** When Finance (F) is underdeveloped, recommendations should target specific institution types based on what exists locally. If only banks exist, recommend angel investor training and VC capacity building. If angels exist but VCs don't, recommend matching capital programmes to catalyze first local VC fund.

---

## 27Q. Case Studies Summary -- Developing Economy Interventions

| Case | Country | Key Intervention | ANDE Domain Relevance |
