|------|---------|-----------------|----------------------|
| Darfur employment initiative | Sudan | Bringing diverse stakeholders together through storytelling and shared economic purpose in conflict-affected region | Culture (C), Support Services (S) |
| Scale-Up programme (Isenberg/Babson) | Colombia (Manizales) | Sales growth focus regardless of sector; pilot > story > media > trust > scale | Culture (C), Markets (M) |
| Startup Namibia | Namibia | Created ESO where none existed; maker space + co-working + mobile outreach; competitive tender for consortium with sustainability exit plan | Support Services (S) |
| Cairo Angels | Egypt | Angel investor training; pipeline expansion through ESOs; investment syndication with MENA investors | Finance (F) |
| WeCode | Rwanda | Women-only coding agency; 11-week boot camp + 15-week advanced + BPO employment | Human Capital (H), Culture (C) |
| Honduras Global | Honduras | Diaspora knowledge transfer; visiting professorships, internships abroad | Human Capital (H) |
| Her&Now | India | Women-led startup incubation/acceleration; mindset change, role models, mentoring, peer networks; 91% reported increased self-confidence | Support Services (S), Culture (C) |
| Vietnam Mentor Initiative | Vietnam | SwissEP facilitated ESO collaboration on shared mentoring problem; ESOs created joint mentor recruitment/training initiative | Support Services (S) |
| Innovation Factory | Mexico, Senegal, Ghana, Ethiopia, Indonesia | Cross-country ESO collaboration; co-run accelerator in Gender, Climate, EdTech tracks | Support Services (S) |
| J-CORE | Jordan | Monthly roundtable of 9 players > ecosystem mapping > international best practices > investment guide > legal reform task force > government dialogue | Policy (P), Finance (F), Culture (C) |
| Jaffna ecosystem | Sri Lanka | Trust-building with local partners in conflict-affected region; maker space; coding schools; international investor roadshow; linked to Colombo ecosystem | Support Services (S), Culture (C), Markets (M) |
| Make-IT in Africa | Africa (24 countries) | Cross-continental tech startup communities; MNC partnerships (Merck, Airbus); flexible design with different partners | Support Services (S), Markets (M) |
| i4Policy | Africa (45 countries) | Policy hackathons co-creating policy recommendations; Startup Acts advocacy in Tunisia, Senegal, Mali; civic tech tools for distributed consultation | Policy (P) |

**Diagnostic use:** These cases provide concrete, citable intervention models for Section 7 recommendations. Match cases to the ecosystem's maturity level and bottleneck domain. The Sudan and Sri Lanka cases are especially relevant for fragile/conflict-affected contexts. The Colombia Scale-Up model works for ecosystems with Emergente maturity where culture needs strengthening.

---

## 27R. Policy Advocacy -- Startup Voice Mechanisms

**The problem:** Startups are busy and lack clout to lobby effectively. Established advocacy channels (chambers of commerce, industry associations) often represent incumbent interests and may even advocate barriers to entrepreneurship.

**Emerging solutions:**
- Grassroots advocacy groups representing startups and ecosystem players (e.g., Entrepreneurs Policy Network in US, TunisiaStartups)
- Policy hackathons that co-create policy recommendations (i4Policy model)
- Innovation for Policy Framework -- crowd-law framework leveraging public participation and deliberation
- Civic tech tools for distributed consultation (e.g., iConsult mobile/web app for ranking, commenting, annotating policy documents)

**i4Policy Manifesto areas:**
- Vision, values
- Principles of sound public policy
- Infrastructure
- Education and R&D
- Multidisciplinary public spaces
- Ease of doing business
- Finance for innovation and entrepreneurship
- Local and pan-African cultures, products, and markets
- Intellectual property rights
- Taxation system

**Diagnostic use:** When Policy (P) recommendations involve new regulations or Startup Acts, the process of creating them matters as much as the content. Recommend participatory policy design involving ecosystem players, not top-down government-only approaches. The i4Policy model of policy hackathons is applicable to Latin American contexts.

---

## 27S. Partner Selection Criteria for Ecosystem Programmes

| Criteria | Key Questions |
|----------|--------------|
| Alignment of objectives | Do impacts match programme objectives? Are marketing materials focused on objectives? Does organization target same entrepreneur community? |
| Ability to benefit from interventions | Are planned interventions part of partner strategy? Does staff have expertise and commitment? Can partner estimate impacts? |
| Connections to entrepreneur community | Does partner have outreach channels with many followers? Network of relevant mentors/coaches? Network of relevant partners? |
| Ability to mobilize resources | Has partner invested own resources in previous programmes? Is partner committing own resources? Does partner have existing partnerships and diversified income sources? |

**Diagnostic use:** When recommending institutional strengthening in Section 7, include criteria for selecting which organizations to support. Not all ESOs or intermediaries are equally effective. This framework helps prioritize investment in organizations most likely to create sustainable impact.



---

## [Ref 28]

# EXTRACTS: How to Fulfill the Potential of Business Development Services Using SCALE

**Source:** `ref_scale.pdf` — Argidius Foundation / ANDE, September 2021, 121 pp.
**Relevance:** Support Services (S) domain — primary reference. Also relevant to Culture (C), Finance (F), and Policy (P) domain recommendations.
**Key construct:** SCALE framework — five evidence-based considerations that distinguish high-performing BDS programs from low-performing ones.

---

## 28A — THE SCALE FRAMEWORK: DEFINITION AND ORIGIN

The SCALE framework emerged from 8 years of evidence gathering by the Argidius Foundation (2013-2021), analyzing 100+ projects, 500+ cohorts, 50+ organizations, and performance data from 12,000 enterprises. It synthesizes findings from the Argidius portfolio, global academic literature, GALI evaluations, USAID PACE, and World Bank studies.

SCALE is NOT a prescriptive checklist. It is a set of evidence-based considerations proven to increase the effectiveness of BDS programs:

- **S — Select** the right enterprise
- **C — Charging** enterprises improves performance
- **A — Address problems**: we learn best by problem solving
- **L — Learn** by evaluating enterprise performance
- **E — Lead by Example**: improve your own organization to better serve enterprises

Critical finding: what distinguished high performers from low performers were not the practices alone, but HOW they were implemented. Even small, straightforward adjustments made by BDS providers can lead to tangible improvements.

**Use in ANDE report:** Sections 5, 7. Evaluating whether an ecosystem's support services incorporate SCALE considerations provides a diagnostic lens for quality beyond counting the number of programs.

---

## 28B — EVIDENCE: BDS IMPACT VARIATION IS DRIVEN BY DESIGN, NOT TYPE

Evaluations by Argidius, GALI, USAID PACE, and World Bank show HIGH VARIABILITY in BDS program impacts on enterprise outcomes. Even programs delivered by the SAME provider using the SAME approach and similar curriculum can yield vastly different performance outcomes.

Key finding: approaches to design, delivery, and evaluation drive variation MORE than the type of service offered (acceleration vs. incubation vs. consulting, etc.).

High-performing vs. low-performing programs differ in:
- **Time allocation:** High performers give entrepreneurs more time working on their own/with their teams; low performers spend more time on standard training delivery
- **Applicant pool:** High performers have fewer but more qualified/experienced applicants; low performers attract more applicants but less qualified
- **Networking:** High performers emphasize strong networking, peer learning, and collaboration; low performers have low emphasis on these

Quantified variation (Village Capital data, 15 programs evaluated by GALI): highest performing programs generated USD 86,000 in 1-year incremental investment growth per enterprise vs. USD 28,000 per enterprise in the lowest performing programs.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 5 (Support Services analysis) — the number of aceleradoras/incubadoras is necessary but insufficient. Quality indicators matter: do programs select participants rigorously, charge fees, focus on problem-solving, evaluate impact?

---

## 28C — SELECT (S): TARGETING THE RIGHT ENTERPRISES

Selection involves identifying which enterprises and entrepreneurs will benefit most from what the BDS provider is offering.

**Why it matters:**
- 80% of surveyed BDS providers consider selection critical and central to their program
- Almost 1/3 of BDS providers report difficulties selecting the right enterprises
- High-performing programs target enterprises with: initial revenue but need for increased investment, intellectual property (e.g., patents), entrepreneurs with higher education, entrepreneurial background, and management experience
- BDS providers that focus on attracting a small but qualified pool of candidates through referrals or targeted outreach have higher performing programs than those seeking maximum applicant volume

**Six dimensions of good selection:**
1. Establish clear selection criteria to profile enterprises and entrepreneurs
2. Leverage referrals from past and current participants and ecosystem actors
3. Set clear and realistic expectations for enterprises
4. Segment the portfolio and tailor the approach
5. Make selection a multistage process — periodically assess partnerships and allocate resources to most engaged enterprises
6. Integrate feedback loops to inform selection criteria going forward

**Data point:** An effective communication plan can result in 2-3x more applicants than available spots within the target segment.

**Case evidence (Alterna, Central America):** By investing in a dedicated pipeline team and segmenting enterprises into "inclusion" and "impact" categories with tailored selection criteria, Alterna doubled the number of programs it could run per year (from 3-4 to 8 annually). Multi-stage selection (50-70 entrepreneurs at stage 1, 20 at stage 2, 4-6 at stage 3) allows progressive filtering.

**Case evidence (TechnoServe):** In some programs, over 80% of participants come through referrals, resulting in improved candidate quality and lower selection costs.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 recommendations. For ecosystems with nascent/emergent support services, recommend that existing programs adopt rigorous selection and segmentation practices before scaling quantity.

---

## 28D — CHARGE (C): ENTERPRISE CONTRIBUTIONS IMPROVE OUTCOMES

Charging is about finding a price point that encourages engagement and learning but does not exclude target firms.

**Key evidence:**
- Evaluation of a BDS program that introduced fees found: participating enterprises DOUBLED the number of jobs created in HALF the time, and return on investment increased by MORE THAN 10-fold
- Trial in Jamaica: ALL microenterprises were willing to contribute at least a nominal fee (countering the assumption that enterprises cannot pay)
- On average, only 65% of participants attend business training when fully subsidized; enterprises that paid attended MORE classes
- Over 80% of organizations surveyed are currently charging or considering charging for BDS programs
- 75% of organizations surveyed charge 40% or less of the full cost per participant
- Higher prices drive further engagement BUT charging close to full-cost screens out many business owners, making it costlier to achieve recruitment targets

**Quantified impact (Bpeace case):** After introducing fees + adjusting program duration, a new cohort generated USD 3.4M in incremental revenue and 62 full-time jobs in ONE year, vs. a previous cohort that generated USD 180,000 in incremental revenue and 32 jobs after TWO years. Applications decreased 60% but applicant quality increased substantially; charging increased ROI by more than 10x.

**Contribution models beyond direct fees:**
- Freemium: free digital content, paid in-person/high-touch services
- Shared success: agreed percentage of future revenue or equity
- Satisfaction guarantee: pay upfront, decide at program end what percentage BDS provider keeps
- Deposits: pay at beginning, refunded if participation criteria are met (Village Capital uses USD 500-1000 deposits)

**Gender consideration:** Women-owned firms have profits 34% lower than male-owned on average. Women use business profits disproportionately for household needs. Charging has NOT been found to differentially affect female business owners, but contribution levels should involve women entrepreneurs in definition process.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — recommend emerging ecosystems' BDS providers test nominal fees. This is both a sustainability mechanism AND a quality-improvement intervention. Relevant to S1, S2 indicators.

---

## 28E — ADDRESS PROBLEMS (A): PROBLEM-SOLVING OVER TRADITIONAL TRAINING

Traditional business training (teaching broad topics in short periods) has LIMITED effect on enterprise performance. Approaches that help entrepreneurs diagnose and solve their problems improve business outcomes.

**Key evidence:**
- Consulting services that help enterprises address specific problems increase productivity, employment, and wages
- Bringing in experts through outsourcing/insourcing has been found to improve outcomes and can be MORE cost-effective than skill-building through consultation
- Targeted training in marketing and finance improves profits. Enterprises with no prior experience benefit from marketing training; established businesses benefit more from financial training
- Working with teams, peers, and mentors improves performance more than "in-classroom" activities

**Group-based consulting is cost-effective:**
- Group-based consulting (cohorts of 3-8 enterprises) can improve management practices AS MUCH as individual consulting at ONE-THIRD the cost
- A group-based intervention led to: increase of 6-15 workers, 28-33% growth in sales, 5-26% uptick in profits for SMEs in Colombia's auto-parts sector

**Peer-to-peer learning drives growth:**
- Entrepreneurs grouped with more experienced peers generate faster growth
- Peer network members exhibit median annual revenue growth higher than OECD definition for high-growth firms
- Networks are most effective when successful entrepreneurs lead the effort and serve as mentors
- Monthly meetings at one another's premises for one year resulted in 10% growth
- 94% of BDS providers in a Village Capital program found exposure to other BDS providers very or extremely valuable

**Program design principles:**
1. Assess enterprise problems accurately — the initially perceived problem is not always the real one
2. Build trust and relationships — empathy and personal connection enable information sharing
3. Facilitate peer learning among entrepreneurs with similar challenges
4. Make education relevant, engaging, easy to apply — Village Capital caps lectures at 15% of program time; remaining time for implementation plans, feedback, and changes. This led to increased engagement, improved NPS, and higher quality applicant pools
5. Build in time for learning to be applied — program shortened from 18 to 12 months resulted in better engagement and higher likelihood of post-program implementation

**Flipped curriculum approach:** Focus on problem solving first, then teach how to avoid that problem in the future. Small groups (6-8) problem-solve challenges relevant to a particular topic, followed by self-study.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — for Support Services recommendations. In ecosystems where programs exist but quality is low, recommend adoption of problem-based approaches, peer-learning structures, and reduced lecture time. Relevant to S1, S2, S4 indicators.

---

## 28F — LEARN (L): EVALUATING ENTERPRISE PERFORMANCE

BDS providers that effectively monitor and use enterprise performance data generate stronger SGB growth over time.

**Key evidence:**
- An RCT on a social entrepreneurship program in France found no detectable impact initially, but the findings were used to iterate program design; the subsequent model, also evaluated by RCT, generated strong impact
- No strong correlation between participant satisfaction and improved business performance (TechnoServe finding) — measuring satisfaction alone is insufficient
- BDS results may take 2-3 years to become evident (Bpeace finding)

**Three essential metrics to track:**
1. Revenue levels
2. Number of full-time employees with salaried jobs
3. Investment levels

**Data collection best practices:**
- Avoid extensive monitoring that disincentivizes reporting
- Set expectations during selection stage
- Cultivate relationships — relational data collection (personal calls from known contacts) outperforms extractive processes (surveys alone)
- Share analysis back with entrepreneurs: how they compare to peers, trend identification

**Overcoming data collection challenges (TechnoServe and CEED examples):**
- Low response rates: integrate reporting into enterprise development process; offer performance benchmarking against peers; provide advance notice; offer staff incentives for highest response rates
- Survey fatigue: switch from lengthy annual surveys to more frequent collection of fewer data points; map each data point's usage before collecting
- Entrepreneurs in survival mode: embed data collection into services they use

**Evaluation approaches:**
- A/B testing: useful for iterating programs before committing to RCT. Example: testing 12-month vs. 18-month program duration on completion rates
- RCTs: costly and time-consuming but provide robust evidence. Best used after iterating to find a strong model, then proving its impact
- External evaluators: provide objective perspective, neutral actor to obtain candid feedback, new techniques

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — recommend that BDS providers and ecosystem builders invest in MEL capacity. Programs without performance measurement cannot improve. This connects to both S-domain (quality of services) and F-domain (demonstrating ROI to funders).

---

## 28G — LEAD BY EXAMPLE (E): ORGANIZATIONAL DEVELOPMENT OF BDS PROVIDERS

BDS providers are themselves growing businesses facing internal challenges. Organizational development is a precondition to impact.

**Key evidence:**
- BDS providers that receive organizational support consistently grow and improve impact
- 40-65% of accelerators take equity in participating enterprises, but exits are insufficient to cover operational costs for 90% of this group — alternative income sources are necessary
- Only 1 in 20 accelerators in North America and Europe are sustained primarily through "cash-for-equity" models; the rest rely on public, corporate, or private funders
- Organizations with former entrepreneurs on their boards are MORE effective than those run by people without entrepreneurial experience

**Four dimensions:**
1. **Clear and focused strategy** — balance between execution and growth; communicate to entire organization; avoid over-prescriptive strategies
2. **Governance with entrepreneurial representation** — board members who share values, include successful entrepreneurs, and evolve as organization evolves. Recommended size: 5-12 members
3. **Team development** — clear roles and responsibilities, address attrition drivers (main driver: horizontal structure with limited growth opportunity), succession planning, delegate responsibilities
4. **Diversified income streams** — avoid dependence on single funder; blended models (subsidy + enterprise contributions); charging fees as unrestricted revenue source

**Case evidence (Alterna):** Diversified funding: 30-40% from governments/international cooperation, 30-40% from corporations, 20-30% from international funders. Client fees represent additional unrestricted revenue.

**Case evidence (Village Capital):** Unrestricted funding to establish back-office operations allowed integration of technology, new processes, and better program support. VilCap's Innovations Team incubates new ideas but rejects those misaligned with core mission.

**Funder role:** Multi-year grants that allow BDS providers to build/strengthen MEL structures; prioritize outcomes over outputs in reporting; unrestricted funding for organizational development.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — for ecosystems where support organizations are fragile or donor-dependent, recommend organizational development pathways alongside programmatic improvements. Relevant when scoring S-domain and evaluating sustainability of support infrastructure.

---

## 28H — CASE STUDY IMPACT METRICS: COST-EFFECTIVENESS BENCHMARKS

These benchmarks can be used to evaluate BDS provider cost-effectiveness in an ecosystem:

| Organization | Cost/Enterprise | Cost/Job Created | Finance Mobilized/Program | Median CAGR |
|-------------|----------------|-----------------|--------------------------|-------------|
| Alterna (Central America) | USD 6,700 | USD 3,700 | USD 9.9M | 40% |
| Bpeace (conflict-affected countries) | USD 16,500 | USD 5,200 | N/A | 6% |
| PUM (40+ countries) | USD 10,000 | USD 900 | USD 87.5M | 15% |
| TechnoServe (global) | USD 6,100 | USD 2,120 | USD 3.5M | 9% |
| Village Capital (global) | USD 25,500 | USD 3,100 | USD 49.2M | 139% |
| Villgro (India) | N/A | N/A | N/A | N/A |

**PUM detail:** 70 participating enterprises generated USD 84M in incremental revenues and nearly 700 jobs. PUM contributes to positive changes in knowledge and business practice for 90% of businesses supported.

**TechnoServe detail:** Impulsa Tu Empresa program worked with 2,000+ businesses; increased enterprise sales by >USD 45M; created 1,600+ new jobs; mobilized >USD 5M since 2012. Programs cost USD 500-5,000 per enterprise served.

**Village Capital detail:** Alumni raise 3x more capital, earn 2.3x more revenue, and create 40% more jobs vs. non-participating startups.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — use as benchmarks when recommending cost-effective BDS models. For nascent/emerging ecosystems with limited funding, lower cost-per-enterprise models (TechnoServe, Alterna) may be more appropriate than high-touch models.

---

## 28I — SGB SEGMENTATION: FOUR ENTERPRISE PROFILES

SGBs are NOT monolithic. They can be segmented into four profiles based on market growth potential, product/service innovation, and entrepreneur behavioral attitudes:

1. **High-Growth Ventures** — enterprises with 100+ employees; founders typically ~45 years old with 20+ years combined work experience, technical and managerial experience, often received mentoring from successful entrepreneurs
2. **Niche Ventures** — innovation-driven, smaller market
3. **Dynamic Enterprises** — commercially viable, growth-oriented, often family businesses generating USD 200K-3M annual revenue
4. **Formalizing Enterprises** — transitioning from informal to formal economy

Each segment has different needs requiring different BDS program designs. Selection criteria, charging models, and problem-solving approaches should be adapted per segment.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 5, 7 — when analyzing an ecosystem's support services, consider whether programs are properly segmented for different enterprise types, or applying one-size-fits-all approaches. Especially relevant for S1, S2 indicators.

---

## 28J — SYNERGIES BETWEEN SCALE COMPONENTS

The five SCALE considerations reinforce one another. Key synergies documented:

- **Charging + Selection:** Charging acts as a self-selection mechanism. Bpeace saw applications decrease 60% but applicant quality increase dramatically. TechnoServe found that when targeting a sufficiently large segment, charging posed little obstacle to recruiting.
- **Selection + Addressing Problems:** Segmenting enterprises during selection (Alterna, TechnoServe) enables tailored problem-solving approaches. Enterprises with common needs can be grouped for efficient group-based consulting.
- **Addressing Problems + Learning:** Entrepreneur feedback on program content drives iteration. Bpeace shortened its program from 18 to 12 months based on entrepreneur feedback that urgency was lacking.
- **Learning + Leading by Example:** Data-driven organizations (TechnoServe, Villgro) embed learning into strategy. TechnoServe tested business-model vs. business-plan methodology across cohorts and found the business-model approach (Lean Startup, Business Model Canvas) generated greater revenue and employment growth.
- **Selection + Learning:** Alterna places MEL team members on the selection team, ensuring collected data feeds back into selection criteria refinement. High-performing past participants' characteristics inform future selection.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — when recommending BDS improvements, frame them as interconnected system improvements rather than isolated fixes. A program that improves selection but ignores learning will not sustain gains.

---

## 28K — GENDER AND INCLUSION LENS FOR BDS PROGRAMS

Cross-cutting findings on gender and social inclusion in BDS:

**Selection:**
- Use inclusive language and imagery in outreach materials
- Proactively recruit women and minorities through dedicated networks
- Ensure female representation in cohorts, especially in male-dominated sectors
- Track % of employees and leadership that are women/minorities in enterprises selected

**Charging:**
- Women-owned firms have 34% lower profits on average
- Women disproportionately use business income for household needs, leaving less for BDS fees
- However, charging has NOT been found to differentially affect female business owners
- Involve women in defining contribution levels; offer scholarships for vulnerable groups

**Problem-solving:**
- Consider women's disproportionate household/care responsibilities when designing program schedules
- Offer childcare services or caretaker cost coverage
- Create women's networks — women tend to have smaller, less diverse networks; stronger networks increase access to financing
- Socio-emotional skills training (confidence, leadership, creativity, resilience) positively impacts women entrepreneurs' profits
- Access to high-quality mentor can increase likelihood of scaling women-led enterprise

**Learning:**
- Disaggregate data by gender to identify performance gaps (female-led enterprises have 82% gap in profits vs. male-led on average)
- Ensure women are represented in evaluation samples
- Include women on data collection teams

**Peer selection mitigates gender bias:** VilCap Investments' peer-selection method resulted in 46% female-led portfolio vs. 15% industry standard.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — include gender and inclusion considerations in BDS recommendations. Especially relevant for ecosystems where female entrepreneurship participation is low.

---

## 28L — KEY DESIGN PRINCIPLES FOR BDS PROGRAM EFFECTIVENESS (SYNTHESIS)

Consolidated actionable design principles from SCALE evidence:

1. **Select fewer, better-matched enterprises** rather than maximizing volume. Quality of match > quantity of participants.
2. **Charge a nominal fee** (even < 5% of annual revenue) to drive self-selection, engagement, and feedback quality. Iterate the amount.
3. **Cap lectures at 10-15% of program time.** Allocate remaining time to implementation plans, peer feedback, and enterprise changes.
4. **Use group-based consulting** (3-8 enterprises per group) to achieve individual consulting impact at 1/3 the cost.
5. **Build in peer-learning structures** — regular interactions among non-competing entrepreneurs with common challenges drives growth.
6. **Shorten program duration** if engagement drops — 12 months outperformed 18 months in Bpeace's case (19x revenue impact improvement).
7. **Follow up after initial support** — PUM found that enterprises performed dramatically better when they received follow-on visits after initial advisory.
8. **Track revenue, employment, and investment** as three core metrics. Satisfaction scores do NOT correlate with business performance.
9. **Use A/B testing between cohorts** before committing to costly RCTs. Iterate program model until solid.
10. **Invest in organizational development** of BDS providers themselves — strategy, governance, team, and diversified funding.
11. **BDS providers should collaborate** rather than compete — pipeline partnerships enable seamless entrepreneur journeys across growth stages.
12. **Funders should provide multi-year, unrestricted grants** to enable BDS provider organizational development and learning investment.

**Use in ANDE report:** Section 7 — select 3-5 principles most relevant to the specific ecosystem's maturity level and bottlenecks. For nascent ecosystems, focus on principles 1, 3, 5, 11. For emerging ecosystems, add principles 2, 4, 8, 10.



---

## [Ref 29]

# Reference Extracts: Evidence and Best Practice in Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Development (ILO)

**Source:** `ref_wcms_901836.pdf`
**Title:** New Frontier or the Latest Buzzword? Evidence and Best Practice in Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Development
**Author:** Lorenz Wild (lead researcher), commissioned by ILO under guidance of Elisa Mandelli
**Publisher:** International Labour Organization (ILO), Geneva
**Date:** 2023
**Pages:** 114
**Methodology:** 18 interviews with EE leaders, 30 academic articles reviewed, 100+ secondary materials from 31 EED organizations

Comprehensive ILO study mapping the global landscape of entrepreneurial ecosystem development (EED). Directly relevant to ANDE diagnostics: provides a four-category actor taxonomy, critiques static frameworks, proposes process-oriented approach, identifies common shortcomings in diagnostics and measurement, and offers actionable recommendations for implementers, funders, and thinkers. HIGH RELEVANCE for ecosystem diagnostic methodology.

---

## 29A. Four Actor Categories for Ecosystem Builders

The study proposes four categories of EED actors (system builders), used throughout the report. This taxonomy is useful for mapping who is doing what in any ecosystem:

1. **Thinkers** -- Investigate and publish research. Universities, think tanks (WEF), thematic work groups (IDIA). Do not do EED directly but influence it through knowledge products.
2. **Informers/Advisors** -- Provide decision-making data or context-specific guidance. Indices (GEI, GSEI), annual reports (GEM, Startup Genome), consulting firms (Deep Ecosystems, MIT-REAP). Paid by actors to help fulfill EED objectives.
3. **Funders** -- Provide funding for EED (not to be confused with investors who invest in firms). Foundations (Argidius, Lemelson, Kauffman), bilateral/multilateral donors (World Bank, USAID, IDB), governments. 
4. **Implementers** -- Take action in the EE. Can be inside or outside the ecosystem. Global NGOs (ANDE, Swisscontact, ACDI/VOCA), governments (Start-up Chile, Start-up Tunisia), ESOs (accelerators, angel networks, co-working spaces), groups of entrepreneurs.

Implementer subcategories: (a) inside/outside the ecosystem, (b) short-term (1-4 years) / long-term (7+ years indefinite), (c) whole-of-ecosystem / limited scope.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When mapping actors in Section 4 (Mapeo de Actores), use this taxonomy to classify each actor. An ecosystem with many implementers but no thinkers or informers may be action-heavy but knowledge-poor. An ecosystem where all actors are outside/short-term signals fragility -- sustainable ecosystems need inside/long-term actors.

---

## 29B. Five Main Categories of Ecosystem Critical Success Factors

Analysis of 27 EE frameworks (16 practitioner, 11 academic) reveals five main categories of factors, with 31 specific critical success factors:

| Category | Specific Factors |
|---|---|
| **Actors** | Universities/research organizations, companies/industry, government (formal institutions), mentors/dealmakers, entrepreneur community, intermediaries, civil society |
| **Functions/Roles** | Finance/funding/capital, human capital/talent/knowledge, support services, leadership, education/training, R&D, on-ramps, celebrate, engagement, identify, enable |
| **Policy** | Policies (regulation, procedures, quality of public-private policy-setting) |
| **Conditions** | Markets/demand or opportunity perceptions (domestic/international), physical infrastructure |
| **Relational Characteristics** | Culture (risk acceptance, rewards/incentives, motivations), networks/connectedness (including international), network density, collaboration, inclusiveness, diversity, fluidity, trust, reciprocity |

Most common factors (in ~70% of frameworks): finance/funding/capital and human capital/talent/knowledge. Next tier (~50%): markets/demand, policy, culture, support services, networks/connectedness.

**Key finding:** Most frameworks focus on static factors (actors, functions, policy) and underrepresent relational characteristics (network density, power/leadership, collaboration, trust, diversity, inclusiveness). These semi-implicit and implicit factors are harder to identify and address but are critical for system functioning.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The ANDE 7-domain model covers actors, functions, policy, and conditions well. The gap is in relational characteristics. When writing Section 5 analysis, explicitly assess trust/collaboration levels, network density, inclusiveness, and power dynamics -- these are not captured by the 30 indicators but are critical for interpreting the scores.

---

## 29C. The "Don't-Copy-Copy" Paradox and Process-Oriented Approach

The study identifies a fundamental paradox in EED:

1. System builders state: each ecosystem is unique, do not copy other ecosystems.
2. System builders also develop frameworks implying: if you replicate these critical success factors, you will have a successful ecosystem. That is, do copy this framework.

The study argues this reliance on replicating static critical success factors from mature ecosystems is insufficient for facilitating systems change in complex adaptive systems.

**Recommended shift:** From factor-oriented approach to process-oriented approach. The key question changes from "what factors need to exist?" to "what process should we follow to catalyze change?"

Overarching principles for EED:
1. Embrace complexity (recognize working in a complex adaptive system)
2. Involve entrepreneurs as leaders in every step of the process/program lifecycle
3. Internalize evidence-based decision making
4. Create a culture of learning/adapting
5. Focus on systemic changes that are sustainable, scalable, and meaningful

Six-step process recommended:
1. Define clear boundaries (target outcomes/impact, target group, spatial boundary, sector/scope)
2. Select the ecosystem where objectives are viable
3. Understand the ecosystem (actors, motivations, incentives, capacities, explicit/semi-explicit/implicit characteristics, market opportunities, underlying causes of constraints)
4. Design evidence-based interventions with clear theory of change
5. Implement adaptively (monitor, learn, reflect, adapt; facilitative manner ensuring sustainability/scale)
6. Monitor and measure intended and unintended system outcomes and impact

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The ANDE Phase 1 diagnostic is essentially step 3 of this process. The study validates the ANDE approach but warns against stopping at actor/function mapping. When writing recommendations in Section 7, frame them as process steps (what to do, how to learn, how to adapt) rather than just prescribing factors to build.

---

## 29D. Common Shortcomings of EED Diagnostics

System builders self-reported and the researcher identified these diagnostic weaknesses:

1. **Mapping as inventory, not analysis** -- Many diagnostics are limited to inventorying actors and roles without understanding interactions, dynamics, or root causes of constraints.
2. **Too macro** -- Identifying main challenges without studying underlying causes. This leads to interventions that address symptoms rather than root causes (positive short-term outcomes but not sustainable/scalable).
3. **Missing relational characteristics** -- Most diagnostics do not assess network density, connectivity, collaboration, power/leadership, diversity/specialization, and inclusiveness.
4. **No focus on triggers of change** -- Diagnostics do not answer what needs to happen to trigger ecosystem evolution to the next lifecycle stage.
5. **No leverage point analysis** -- Do not identify the most influential entities and their motivations/incentives for change.
6. **Combined baseline and design** -- Trying to simultaneously establish measurement baseline and inform intervention design in one diagnostic leads to neither being done well.
7. **Not target-group specific** -- Diagnostics based on general framework factors rather than the viewpoint of specific entrepreneur segments, which can reinforce dynamics that keep marginalized communities excluded.

Swisscontact observation: ANDE Ecosystem Snapshot Toolkit, Koltai 6+6, and GIZ ecosystem mapping are powerful for mapping ESOs and services but do not consider interactions and dynamics within the system. Strengthening networks requires determining how well or badly actors are connected and how to help them evolve.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These critiques apply directly to ANDE Phase 1. The 30 indicators capture actors/functions well but miss relational dynamics. To strengthen the diagnostic: (1) add qualitative notes on network density and collaboration quality between actors; (2) identify 2-3 leverage points (most influential actors with capacity and motivation to change); (3) assess underlying causes, not just symptoms (e.g., if financing is low, is it because no funds exist, or because existing funds and entrepreneurs cannot find each other?).

---

## 29E. Common Shortcomings of EED Measurement

The study finds measurement is the biggest area needing improvement in EED:

1. **No system change measurement** -- Most efforts measure outputs (entrepreneurs trained, businesses created) but not system change (shifts in how actors interact, changes in rules/norms, improvements in connectivity).
2. **No contribution analysis** -- Even when change is identified, efforts cannot prove their contribution to it. The DCED minimum standard requires evidence that would convince a "reasonable but skeptical observer."
3. **Missing theories of change** -- Few organizations use theories of change explaining how interventions lead to system change and impact. Some have organizational-level ToCs but do not customize per ecosystem.
4. **No unintended consequences measurement** -- Neither positive nor negative unintended outcomes are tracked. Outcome harvesting recommended as a method.
5. **Benchmark paradox** -- ANDE Ecosystem Diagnostic Toolkit recommends comparable regions as benchmarks, but this contradicts the principle that every system is different. Each ecosystem should be measured against its own baseline.
6. **Anecdotal case studies** -- Most success stories assign success too easily to the implementing organization without assessing actual contribution to system change (confirmation bias).

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When writing Section 7 (Recommendations), include recommendations for the ecosystem to establish its own measurement baseline and monitoring system. Do not recommend comparison to other ecosystems as the primary metric; instead, recommend tracking change from the ecosystem's own starting point. Also recommend tracking relational indicators (collaboration quality, network density) alongside output indicators.

---

## 29F. Ecosystem Lifecycle Stages: Synthesis from Multiple Frameworks

The study compiles lifecycle models from multiple sources:

- **Startup Genome:** activation, globalization, attraction, integration
- **ANDE:** activation, growth, mature
- **Endeavor:** externally dependent ecosystem, emerging local ecosystem, self-propelling local ecosystem
- **Mack Mayer (2015):** birth, growth, sustainment, decline or re-birth
- **Cantner et al.:** birth, growth, maturity, decline, re-emergence (with theory about human resource exchange between entrepreneurial efforts and established businesses during lifecycle transitions)

General consensus: EEs develop organically and non-linearly, are self-organizing and self-sustaining.

Key critique: lifecycle models are mostly based on tech startups in high-income countries, framed too much from an investor point of view (exits, early-stage funding, unicorns), and do not translate well to developing economies. There is insufficient discussion on what factors predict or help move an ecosystem to the next phase.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** The ANDE four-level model (Naciente through Autosostenible) is a lifecycle model. The Endeavor framework (externally dependent, emerging local, self-propelling) adds a useful dimension: does the ecosystem depend on external actors (development agencies, national government programs) or is it locally driven? When scoring and writing Section 3 (Nivel de Madurez), note the degree of external dependency as a qualitative factor.

---

## 29G. Three Systemsiness Groupings: Self-Assessment Framework

The study proposes three groups for EED efforts based on their adherence to systems change principles:

**Group 1 -- Working "in" the ecosystem (using EED label but doing traditional entrepreneurship development):**
- Aware of working in an ecosystem but understanding is limited to a few functions
- Focuses on building actor capacity, not addressing underlying causes of constraints
- No clear logic for systems change, no systemic change outcomes/indicators
- Does not measure change in the system, only change in actor capacity and impact on entrepreneurs
- Low/medium focus on sustainability and scalability

**Group 2 -- Working "on" the ecosystem (following system change principles but cannot prove change):**
- Understands ecosystem includes important relational factors
- Diagnostic may include some relational assessment (culture, connectedness)
- Underlying causes may emerge during engagement even if not analyzed in diagnostic
- Has some causal logic for systemic change but not detailed or evidenced
- Adapts approach based on learning
- Does not measure system change, or if does, not measuring contribution
- Medium/high focus on sustainability and scalability

**Group 3 -- Full systems change approach:**
- Understands ecosystem comprises explicit, semi-explicit, and implicit factors
- Good understanding of underlying causes of constraints
- Implementation focuses on addressing underlying causes
- Detailed evidence-based causal logic showing how interventions lead to systemic change
- Systems change outcomes prioritized with measurable indicators
- Measures change in system and contribution to change
- May monitor unintended consequences
- Sustainability and scalability have high importance

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** Use these groups to assess the maturity of ecosystem-building efforts in the diagnosed city. Are local organizations (accelerators, government agencies, foundations) operating at Group 1, 2, or 3? This assessment belongs in Section 5 under Servicios de Apoyo analysis and in Section 7 Recommendations.

---

## 29H. Recommendations for Funders: Systems Mindset Principles

Drawing from ASHOKA report "Embracing Complexity" (60+ funders, 110+ system change leaders interviewed):

1. Embrace a systems mindset
2. Support evolving paths to system change
3. Work in true partnership
4. Prepare for long-term engagement
5. Collaborate with other stakeholders

Additional findings:
- Funders collectively provide too much funding in some ecosystems, crowding out private actors and preventing organic, entrepreneur-led development
- Individual EED efforts are often underfunded, requiring cobbled-together funding leading to short-term thinking
- Funding cycles are generally too short. System change takes years/decades; 2-3 year programs emphasize quick results over sustainable change. SECO funds Swiss EP over 12 years as a positive exception.
- Funders do not require systemic change measurement, so implementers are not incentivized to measure it
- Too much focus on employment creation outcomes, not enough on broader value creation
- Not enough focus on inclusion (women, marginalized groups) and environmental impacts

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When assessing the Financiamiento domain, consider not just private investment capital but also development funding flowing into the ecosystem. Is there funder crowding? Are programs too short-term? Is development funding creating dependency rather than self-sustaining capacity? These questions should inform Section 6 (Retos) if development funding distortion is observed.

---

## 29I. The Urban Focus and Developing Economy Challenges

The study notes that EED is predominantly urban-focused and concentrated in high-income countries:

- Most EED happens in high-income countries, followed by upper-middle income (Mexico, Colombia, Jordan), then lower-middle income.
- Within countries, EED is typically directed at the city level, specifically capital cities and commercial hubs.
- In developing economies, system builders must adjust to: varying government capacity, lower overall system effectiveness, and potentially distorting effects of too much development funding.
- Most EED focuses on tech entrepreneurship and formally registered startups/young firms.
- EEs naturally tend to evolve in urban environments due to higher concentration of actors, better access to services/resources, more idea exchanges, and density-dependent vibrant entrepreneur communities.

Key insight from Lemelson Foundation: some implementing partners focus on enabling productive entrepreneurs in rural communities with unique programs, recognizing different needs. Endeavor published a 2021 report on rural entrepreneurship exploring this topic.

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When diagnosing smaller cities or non-capital cities in LatAm, acknowledge the urban bias in most ecosystem frameworks. Do not assume that benchmark data from Mexico City, Bogota, or Santiago applies to secondary cities. Adjust expectations: smaller cities will naturally have fewer actors and less density, but this does not mean the ecosystem is failing -- it means the strategy should focus on connectivity to larger hubs and regional networks rather than replicating capital-city infrastructure locally.

---

## 29J. Working Inside vs. Outside the Ecosystem: Facilitation vs. Direct Implementation

The study distinguishes two implementation approaches:

**Working from outside (facilitation):**
- Implementer does not take on an ecosystem function/role
- Facilitates action in partnership with ecosystem actors
- Works via distributed decision-making and co-creation to foster local/collective ownership
- Less control over outcomes, but changes are more likely to be sustainable and scalable
- Shifts to system leverage points (influential actors) rather than direct delivery

**Working from inside (direct implementation):**
- Implementer becomes an ecosystem actor or takes on a function/role
- More control but higher risk of crowding out private actors
- Can create dependency; when implementer leaves, function disappears
- Risk of distorting natural ecosystem dynamics

System change is best achieved by intervening in multiple, distinct yet interrelated parts of the system -- no single innovation or change type will change the entire system (Stanford Change Labs "systems acupuncture" metaphor, cited via TMS Honduras).

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** When assessing support organizations (S1-S5), note whether key support is provided by inside actors (local organizations that will persist) or outside actors (international NGOs, short-term government programs). An ecosystem where the most active accelerator is an international NGO project ending in 2 years has a different risk profile than one where a local university-based accelerator operates continuously. Flag outside-dependent support in the notes column and in Section 6 (Retos).



---

## [Ref 30]

# Reference Extracts: 10 Entrepreneurship Movements (E-Movements)

**Source:** `refEMovements_FINAL.pdf`
**Title:** Entrepreneurship Movements
**Author:** Don Macke, Senior Vice President, e2 Entrepreneurial Ecosystems (by NetWork Kansas)
**Date:** January 1, 2023
**Pages:** 3

Short practitioner paper identifying 10 distinct movements that have shaped entrepreneur-led community economic development in the United States. Relevant to the ANDE Culture domain and, secondarily, to Support Services, Finance, and Policy.

---

## 30A. Core Framework: 10 Movements That Built Entrepreneurial Culture

Macke identifies 10 distinct but interrelated movements ("E-Movements") that have shaped the field and practice of community-centered entrepreneurial development, presented in rough chronological order of emergence:

1. **Chambers of Commerce** (oldest, ~1773)
2. **Capital-Led**
3. **SBA and Small Business Development Centers** (1953/mid-1970s)
4. **Technology-Led** (~1959 Research Triangle Park; Silicon Valley ~1970s)
5. **Incubation and Accelerators** (first incubator 1959, Batavia, NY)
6. **Microenterprise Development** (~1976 Grameen Bank; U.S. movement 1980s)
7. **Economic Gardening** (1987, Littleton, CO)
8. **Training-Led** (1993, Kauffman Foundation FastTrac)
9. **SourceLink** (2003, UMKC)
10. **Ecosystem Building** (late 1990s onward)

**Application to ANDE diagnostics:** These movements represent distinct "layers" of institutional infrastructure that accumulate in a mature ecosystem. When diagnosing a city, one can assess which of these layers exist, which are missing, and at what stage of evolution the ecosystem sits. Emerging economies in Latin America may be building several of these layers simultaneously rather than sequentially.

---

## 30B. Chambers of Commerce as First-Generation Ecosystem Builders

- Likely the oldest form of intentional, organized entrepreneurial development in the U.S. (first chamber: Charleston, SC, 1773; U.S. Chamber of Commerce created 1912).
- Original role included direct services to entrepreneurs, networking, and relationship-building environments.
- Over time, chambers evolved into primarily promotion and advocacy, moving away from direct entrepreneurial services.

**ANDE relevance (Culture, Support Services):** In Latin American ecosystems, chambers of commerce (camaras de comercio) often still play a direct services role. When diagnosing the Culture and Support Services domains, assess whether local chambers are active ecosystem participants or have become purely advocacy-oriented. The transition pattern Macke describes (from services to advocacy) may indicate a gap that newer organizations like accelerators or ecosystem-builders need to fill.

---

## 30C. Capital-Led Movement and Its Persistence

- Access to credit/capital has been a long-running entrepreneurship focus.
- Early U.S. examples: Kentucky Highlands, Maine's Coastal Enterprises, Michigan's Northern Initiatives -- all led with capital access to foster entrepreneurial development.
- Capital continues to be a central driver. NetWork Kansas (where Macke works) is itself capital-led.

**ANDE relevance (Finance):** The persistence of capital-led approaches across decades confirms that finance is a perennial bottleneck. However, the ANDE methodology should note that capital alone is insufficient; Macke's framework shows it as just one of 10 movements. Diagnostics should assess whether a given ecosystem is over-reliant on a single capital-led approach without complementary support layers (training, mentoring, incubation, ecosystem coordination).

---

## 30D. Incubation and Acceleration as Micro-Ecosystems

Macke defines incubators and accelerators as a form of "micro-entrepreneurial ecosystem" focused on a specific cohort of entrepreneurs. First U.S. incubator: Batavia, NY, 1959 (per NBIA records).

**ANDE relevance (Support Services):** This reframing is useful for ANDE diagnostics. Instead of counting incubators and accelerators only as standalone support organizations (indicators S1, S2), evaluators should also assess whether these programs function as coherent micro-ecosystems that integrate multiple support elements (capital, mentoring, training, networking) or operate as isolated service providers. A single well-integrated accelerator may contribute more to ecosystem development than several fragmented programs.

---

## 30E. Microenterprise Development: Inclusion Dimension

- Movement traced to Muhammad Yunus and the Grameen Bank (Bangladesh, 1976).
- Became a U.S. movement in the 1980s.
- Focus: smaller ventures, appropriate capital, technical assistance.
- National organization: Association for Enterprise Opportunity (AEO).

**ANDE relevance (Finance, Support Services, Culture):** Microenterprise development represents the inclusive dimension of entrepreneurial ecosystems. ANDE diagnostics that focus only on tech startups and high-growth ventures miss a large segment. For emerging ecosystems in Latin America, microenterprise programs are often the most widespread form of entrepreneurial support. Diagnostics should distinguish between microenterprise support (survival/subsistence) and growth-oriented entrepreneurship support (PECs/SGBs), assessing both.

---

## 30F. Economic Gardening: Market Intelligence as Intervention

- Founded by Chris Gibbons in Littleton, CO (1987) in response to Martin Marietta cutting half its workforce.
- Core service: market intelligence research and analysis for existing businesses.
- Has an international following.

**ANDE relevance (Markets, Support Services):** Economic Gardening is a demand-side intervention model rarely seen in Latin American ecosystems. It focuses on helping existing businesses access market intelligence -- a different approach from supply-side interventions (training, capital). For ecosystems where the Markets domain (M1-M3) scores low, Economic Gardening-style programs could be a recommendation: provide startups and SGBs with market research, competitive intelligence, and sector analysis to accelerate growth without requiring new capital injection.

---

## 30G. Training-Led Movement: Structured Entrepreneurship Education

- Kauffman Foundation launched FastTrac in January 1993 in Kansas City.
- Other programs emerged over the years (e.g., Entrepreneurial Edge -- some came and went).
- America's SBDCs provide similar training programs.
- Often combined with capital access, especially in microenterprise programs.

**ANDE relevance (Human Capital, Support Services):** Training-led approaches map directly to the Human Capital domain. Key diagnostic insight: the most durable programs combine training with capital (as Macke notes). Standalone training without follow-on financing or mentoring shows lower effectiveness. Recommendations should favor integrated models.

---

## 30H. SourceLink: Ecosystem Mapping and Coordination Infrastructure

- Created in 2003 by Maria Meyers and team at University of Missouri, Kansas City (UMKC).
- First system: KCSourceLink.
- Now nationwide in the U.S.
- Many SourceLink organizations now lead state, regional, and community ecosystem-building efforts.

**ANDE relevance (Support Services, Culture):** SourceLink represents an ecosystem coordination and mapping function. For ANDE diagnostics, this maps to the concept of a "backbone organization" that catalogs resources, connects entrepreneurs to services, and reduces search costs. When the Support Services domain shows many actors but low effectiveness, a SourceLink-type coordination layer may be what is missing. Relevant for recommendations in ecosystems that have actors but lack coherence.

---

## 30I. Ecosystem Building: From Development Systems to Complex Adaptive Systems

Key lineage Macke traces:
- **Brian Dabson, Jay Kayne, Tom Lyons** -- first framed the term "Entrepreneurial Development Systems" (EDSs).
- **Tom Lyons** -- operationalized as the "Entrepreneurial League System."
- **Center for Rural Entrepreneurship** (1999, now e2) -- 30 years of field-testing and refinement.
- **Ernesto Sirolli** -- Enterprise Facilitation program; book *Ripples from the Zambezi* (1999).
- **Victor Hwang & Greg Horowitt** -- *The Rainforest: The Secret to Building the Next Silicon Valley* (2013) -- evolved EDS idea into the "ecosystem" concept.
- **Victor Hwang + Andy Stoll at Kauffman Foundation** -- hosted summits, research, projects advancing Entrepreneurial Ecosystems.

Key conceptual evolution: the ecosystem frame incorporates complexity theory -- "the complexity inherent in a thriving and productive natural or sociological environment." Since entrepreneurial ecosystems are anchored in human dynamics, they involve "complex and reinforcing social norms, ways of perceiving and behaving and culture."

**ANDE relevance (all domains, especially Culture):** This extract traces the intellectual genealogy of the ecosystem concept that ANDE methodology builds on. The critical insight is that ecosystem building is the most recent and most integrative movement: it subsumes elements of all previous 9 movements. For diagnostics, this means that the presence of explicit "ecosystem building" activity (backbone organizations, coordination platforms, ecosystem strategies) is an indicator of maturity. Its absence suggests the ecosystem may have individual components (capital, training, incubation) that are not yet integrated into a coherent system. The emphasis on social norms, perception, and culture as foundational reinforces that the ANDE Culture domain is not ancillary -- it is the substrate on which all other domains operate.

---

## 30J. Chronological Pattern: Evolutionary Sequence of Ecosystem Development

Macke's chronology maps a rough evolutionary sequence:

| Era | Movement | Primary Function |
|-----|----------|-----------------|
| 1773-1950s | Chambers of Commerce | Networking, advocacy, basic services |
| 1950s-1970s | Capital-Led, SBA/SBDCs | Credit access, business planning |
| 1959-1970s | Technology-Led | Innovation, high-growth ventures |
| 1959-present | Incubation & Accelerators | Cohort-based support micro-ecosystems |
| 1976-1980s | Microenterprise Development | Inclusion, small-scale capital + TA |
| 1987 | Economic Gardening | Market intelligence for existing firms |
| 1993 | Training-Led | Structured entrepreneurship education |
| 2003 | SourceLink | Ecosystem mapping & coordination |
| Late 1990s+ | Ecosystem Building | Systemic integration, complexity, culture |

**ANDE relevance (all domains):** This chronology suggests a diagnostic heuristic: ecosystems in early stages will primarily show activity in the earlier movements (chambers, capital, basic training). As ecosystems mature, they add layers (incubation, acceleration, technology focus, market intelligence, coordination, and finally explicit ecosystem-building strategy). The ANDE Table 4.3 intervention matrix (Build/Strengthen/Scale/Maintain by maturity level) aligns with this evolutionary logic. Nascent ecosystems should not be expected to have all 10 layers; the diagnostic should assess which layers are present and whether they match the ecosystem's maturity level.

---

## Summary: Mapping E-Movements to ANDE Domains

| E-Movement | Primary ANDE Domain(s) | Diagnostic Use |
|------------|----------------------|----------------|
| Chambers of Commerce | Culture, Support Services | Assess if chambers still provide direct entrepreneurial services |
| Capital-Led | Finance | Evaluate diversity of capital instruments beyond single sources |
| SBA/SBDCs | Support Services, Policy | Look for government-backed advisory infrastructure |
| Technology-Led | R&D/Innovation, Markets | Assess tech-transfer and innovation commercialization pathways |
| Incubation & Accelerators | Support Services | Evaluate as micro-ecosystems, not just headcount |
| Microenterprise Development | Finance, Support Services | Distinguish from growth-oriented support; assess inclusion |
| Economic Gardening | Markets, Support Services | Recommend for low-scoring Markets domains |
| Training-Led | Human Capital | Favor integrated training + capital models |
| SourceLink | Support Services, Culture | Recommend as coordination layer when actors exist but lack coherence |
| Ecosystem Building | All (especially Culture) | Presence indicates maturity; absence signals fragmentation |
